# Autonomous execution checkpoint

Updated: 2026-10-16. Repository: https://github.com/sushantmane/peeky (private).

## Direction and authorization

The user authorized autonomous implementation and parallel agents. The user also authorized delegating to their Claude and Copilot subscriptions. Both CLIs are installed. On 2026-10-14, Claude Sonnet successfully completed a bounded no-tool design review after user login. The configured Fable default requested extra credits; use the explicit Sonnet override without changing account configuration or purchasing credits. Copilot completed a bounded Java integration review in 37.91 seconds using existing GitHub authentication; do not pass --no-auto-login, which prevented credential use. Use bounded independent reviews or isolated implementation tasks under the same correctness/CI gates. Do not treat CLI installation or auth-status output alone as a successful model invocation. Correctness comes first; optimize measured performance next. Minimum MVP languages: Python, Java, C, C++, Rust. Connect code, logs, and metrics incrementally without delaying debugger support. Keep CI independent and parallel, targeting less than five minutes of execution. Subscription usage for authorized agent work is allowed. Do not change account plans, purchase extra credits, consume reset credits, publish packages, or deploy production services.

An hourly thread heartbeat (`continue-peeky-mvp`) checks for available usage and resumes unfinished work. It cannot bypass usage limits and depends on app availability. Check active agents, Git status, and open PRs before editing; never duplicate an active implementation.

- **#35 (`1706807`):** the captured-evidence store, ADR 0004 step 4 (the owner's "as easy as heappy" direction).
  - Every probe (local, `--worker` and `--background`) is saved on the client to SQLite and gets a `run_id`.
  - `peeky sql` runs one read-only, bounded SELECT over saved runs.
  - `peeky queries` adds canned questions such as `diff`, `set_effect` and `hits`.
  - `peeky sql --schema` is the manifest, and the new error code is `INVALID_QUERY`.

- **Whole-code review (2026-10-16), #38 (`9cd8df9`), #39 (`81d2346`), #40 (`85cc7f9`).** Four independent reviewers read the whole codebase. Every finding was fixed with a regression test that fails on the old code, and each PR had its own verification review. Dispositions are in [reviews/WHOLE_CODE_REVIEW_2026-10-16.md](reviews/WHOLE_CODE_REVIEW_2026-10-16.md).
  - **Speed (the owner's "would Rust be faster?" question).** `scripts/profile_probe.py` showed Peeky's own CPU was repeated work, not the language. Every operation rehashed clang (125 MB) and the Java adapter bundle, using native SHA-256 code a Rust port would share. Digest and bundle caches fixed it: C++ probe 2.28 → 0.73 s, Java pause per hit 120 → 10 ms. Peeky's CPU is now about 0.5% of a Python probe and 10–15% of a compiled one, so ADR 0003 stands (see PERFORMANCE.md). About 0.5 s of each Python probe is a debugpy exit stall outside Peeky's control.
  - **Correctness:** Java mutations must fit the declared type; C++ links libc++; C/C++ detection links one program; non-executable Java lines are `INVALID_BREAKPOINT`; caller frames outside the program are request errors.
  - **Lifecycle:** a debugpy debuggee no longer survives transport failures; session names are reserved atomically and interrupted starts clean up; co-stopped lldb threads are served as hits; Python breakpoint conditions (which ran agent code) are refused; workers bypass HTTP proxies, close on SIGTERM and exit when idle.
  - **Provenance:** Rust interventions are allowed only on locals the program keeps in memory (LLVM IR; `.dbg.spill` and inlined copies are refused). Every file a compiler read is declared (Rust) or hashed and rechecked (C/C++ headers outside the workspace).

- **Sources with any characters in their names are debugged** (#179, found while fixing #177). Peeky read its tools' output with `str.splitlines()`, which also ends a line at U+2028, U+2029, U+0085, `\x0b`, `\x0c` and `\x1c`–`\x1e`, and read the names those tools escape as other text:
  - a C source named `main\u2028x.c` failed with `Invalid \escape` (dwarfdump writes a line table's names in octal, `main\342\200\250x.c`, which Peeky read as JSON: no C source with a non-ASCII name could be debugged at all); with that read, its locals were withheld ("Peeky cannot read that from the source as the compiler preprocessed it"), as clang writes `-E` line markers in octal too and Peeky only dropped the backslashes (as #180's inline-assembly refusal did, naming `dir/a\u2028b.c` `dir/a342200250b.c`);
  - a Rust module so named failed with `Unrecognized DWARF entry output` (`DW_AT_decl_file` is printed as it is, and its line was split); with that read, its owned `Vec` stayed raw, as LLVM IR writes the file's name in hex (`x\E2\80\A8y.rs`) and the MIR's proof never met its function;
  - every line Peeky reads (dwarfdump, LLDB's `disassemble` and `source info`, Cargo's JSON messages and progress, rustc's MIR, libtest, a checker's output, the job log, the program's output, a source's lines and counts, a `.git` file) now ends at `\n` or `\r\n` alone (`textlines.split_lines`; a test keeps `.splitlines(` out of `src`), and dwarfdump's and clang's octal and LLVM IR's hex escapes read back;
  - real builds (each fails on main and on #178's head): C sources named with a U+2028 or `größe.c`, and a Rust module named with a U+2028, whose locals read (`limit 3`, `twice 6`; `doubled 40`, `parts [20, 40, 41]` under the MIR's proof). Unit tests for each parser.
  - Review (PR #188, round 1): `Path.read_text` turned a Rust source's lone `\r` into a line break rustc does not count (clang does; neither counts `\f` or `\v`): sources are now read as they are (`read_source`) and split by their compiler's rule (`source_lines`) where lines are numbered (the line table's bounds, `macro_rules!` lines, the loop-header check, the agent's numbering), as are dwarfdump's output and MIR dumps. The octal decoders were Python's `codecs.escape_decode` (`src/\x41.c` read as `src/A.c`, unknown escapes kept, `\400` cut short): one strict decoder (`octal_string`) now reads only LLVM's `write_escaped` grammar (`\\`, `\"`, `\t`, `\n`, three octal digits up to `\377`) and UTF-8, else nothing. LLVM IR's hex decoder replaced invalid UTF-8 (`\FF` as U+FFFD, which could name a declared file) and kept a lone `\q`: `ir_string` now refuses anything but printable ASCII and `\XX`, and strict UTF-8, and IR is read without replacement; a name it refuses proves nothing. `split_lines` keeps a lone trailing `\r`. Tests (each fails on 9737ee0): the paired escapes (`src/\\x41.c` read as `src/\x41.c`, `src/\x41.c` refused), the IR's (`\FF`, `\q`, an unescaped quote), a one-line Rust file's line 2 refused while a C file's lone `\r` counts, and the line splits.
  - Review (PR #188, round 2): which rule counts lines was told by the file's `.rs` suffix, though `#[path = "module"]` lets a Rust module have any name. The session's language now says (`parse_lines`/`function_scopes(..., rust=)`, `NativeSession.rust_lines`), for the line table's bounds, `macro_rules!` lines and the loop-header check. Not reachable end to end yet: declared Rust sources must end in `.rs` and only declared files are built, so such a crate stops at `couldn't read src/module`. Unit tests with a `src/module` holding a lone `\r` (each fails on 6ea847a).
- **A Python local the loop's pass has not assigned yet says so** (#182, eighth agent evaluation, Python `articles`). Back in `group()`'s loop with `step --out` on `key = slug(title)`, `session get key` gave `'hello-world'`, the previous title's key, as a plain value: the agent read it as this pass's. C and C++ have had a note for a line a loop returns to (#140); Python had none.
  - Now such a local is shown with a `note` (`this pass's code has not assigned it before this line, and every path through the rest of the pass assigns it (line 21) before reading it: the value is likely from an earlier pass (or from before the loop at line 20)`) in `session get`, `inspect`, probe rows (`[note]`, with the warning) and the evidence's `note` column, and what is read through it carries it (#148). `set` refuses it: the code overwrites it before reading it, so a change would never be seen (`the code assigns it again (line 21) before it reads it`, with a hint to change it after that assignment). The value is never withheld.
  - `python_passes` reads the function's bytecode (`dis`), compiled from the frame's source by this interpreter where it compiles as the program's does (see the review below), plain and as `-O` compiles it (both must agree: an `assert`'s walrus is left out under `-O`). The control flow takes every jump, the next instruction, and the handler of each exception-table range from every instruction in it; natural loops come from dominators. debugpy gives a line, not an instruction, so every instruction of the line counts as where the stop may be: all must be in the same innermost loop, the one a `for` or `while` around the line makes (its head on the statement's lines, or a `while` body's first instruction: the compiler repeats the condition at each pass's end, so a local the condition stores is never noted); no path from the loop's head to them stores the local; no path from them reads or deletes it (`LOAD_FAST`, the 3.12 comprehension's `LOAD_FAST_AND_CLEAR`, 3.13's paired forms) before storing it; and every path through the rest of the pass that runs no exception stores it.
  - Not judged: a loop's header line (its code also runs before and after the loop), a local a closure shares (a cell), a module that may run other code or reach a frame's locals (see the review below), module and class code, two functions of the frame's name on its line, and a source changed since the program started or that does not parse. The module global of the name (`inspect --globals`) is never judged as the local.
  - A candidate's copy (`probe --old/--new`) is written while the run is prepared, after the clock that dates the sources started: it read as "changed after the program started", so it got no note, and no global was proven there either (#104). The clock now starts once the run is prepared.
  - Unit tests mark which locals each line notes (CPython 3.11, 3.12 and 3.13), and an oracle runs the cases under instruction tracing (`sys.monitoring`; `settrace` opcodes on 3.11): wherever a noted line runs, the local was not stored since the loop's header last ran, and the frame's next use of it is a store. Real debugpy: `get` (and `--frame 1` from inside `slug`), `inspect`, the refused `set`, the module global not noted, probe rows from the second pass, and a candidate's; each fails on 1ea0e6d.
  - Review of PR #185, four ways the note could be false:
    - compiled by Peeky's interpreter, not the program's (a 3.11 worker reads a 3.12 program's inlined comprehension, `LOAD_FAST_AND_CLEAR x`, as code that never reads `x`): judged only where the program's interpreter is this one, or reports the same implementation and bytecode magic number when asked (once, `-I -c`);
    - the frame may not run the code on disk (a module whose `exec(compile(replacement, __file__, "exec"))` replaces `f` with a same-line function that assigns `x` earlier): no function of a module (the whole file) is judged where it names `exec`, `eval`, `compile`, `types.FunctionType`, `CodeType`, `__code__`, `co_code` or `marshal`;
    - calls can read or write a caller's locals unseen (a helper writing `sys._getframe(1).f_locals["x"]`, which 3.13 writes through; an `__exit__` reading it): nor where it names `locals`, an argument-less `vars()` or `dir()`, `_getframe`, `currentframe`, `f_locals`, `f_back`, a frame of a traceback or generator, a tracer, or imports `inspect`; and the note now states facts about the code (`this pass's code has not assigned it before this line ... the value is likely from an earlier pass`), as code in other modules that reaches into the frame is not seen (documented);
    - a store reachable only through an exception's edge (`except KeyError: e = t`) or a branch (`match 0: case 1: m = t`) counted as one that happens: now every path from the stop's instructions that runs no exception must store the local before the pass ends (back at the head, out of the loop, a return or a raise), so a store behind a `continue` or `break` proves nothing either. Exception paths still take a note away (a read in a handler, a store before the stop through one).
    - Missing notes: a breakpoint stops where its line begins (an instruction of another line than the one before it, a jump's target or a handler), so its stop is judged at those instructions alone: a loop body's last line holds the jump back, after its assignment, which a caller's frame can be at but a breakpoint never stops at (`inner = x + y` in a nested loop now noted from its second hit). `key = str(x)` inside `try:` stays unnoted: its `except ValueError: pass` skips the assignment, and the `return` after the loop then reads the earlier pass's `key`.
    - Real debugpy: each of the four programs at its second hit, with no note (each noted on bbe364c), and the nested loop's last line noted (not on bbe364c). The unit oracle also checks that the store comes before the pass ends, and checks a breakpoint-only note only where the line began.
  - Review of PR #185, round 2:
    - an exception-table edge can be normal completion: a `with` whose `__exit__` returns true swallows the exception, and an `async for` ends through its handler (`END_ASYNC_FOR`), so the pass went on without the store the note promised (`x = item` after a failing call in `with Suppress():`; `x = y` in an `async for` over nothing). Exception edges still prove no store, but every path from the stop that stores nothing, through a handler in the loop, back to the head, out of the loop or to a return, now takes the note away; an exception into a handler outside the loop, or re-raised out of it, ends the pass and is not followed.
    - `vars(*args)` with no `args` is `vars()`: `vars` and `dir` are left only with one argument, not starred, and no keywords. `import builtins as b; b.exec(...)` was not seen: any use of the `builtins` module (or its name in a string), `__builtins__`, `__import__` or `importlib.import_module` now leaves the module unjudged, like `inspect` and `marshal`; `sys`, `types` and the rest were already read by attribute name, whatever the module is imported as (`import sys as s; s._getframe`).
    - One bytecode magic number spans patch versions and builds: the program's interpreter must report the same implementation, exact `sys.version` (version and build), ABI flags, cache tag and magic number as Peeky's.
    - Real debugpy: the `with` and `async for` programs at their second hit, with no note (each noted on b67abc0); unit cases for each (3.11, 3.12, 3.13), and interpreters reporting only the magic number, or another build, refused.
  - Review of PR #185, round 3:
    - a handled exception's path that assigns the local (`except ValueError: x = 2` after `x = 1` in the `try`) passed the check, but the note named only the `try` body's line: it now names each path's first assignment (`lines 11, 13`). A branch that only re-raises (an `except` that does not match, a `with` whose `__exit__` does not swallow) leaves the pass abnormally and is not a path that goes on without a store (with Python 3.13 it took the note away).
    - `globals()["__builtins__"]` and `getattr(b, "exec")` reached `exec` by name: `globals()`, `len.__self__` and a string naming a builtin or `__builtins__` now leave the module unjudged too.
    - Real debugpy: the handled program names both lines, and the module that replaces `f` through `globals()["__builtins__"]` gets no note (each fails on 920f102).
- **Compiler dependency files are read by each compiler's escaping, and fail closed** (#177, found in PR #163's review). The provenance check (every file a build read is declared, or hashed when outside the workspace) read rustc's and clang's `.d` files alike, with `splitlines()`: a U+2028 (or `\x0b`, `\x85`…) in a file name ended the line, so the rest of the rule was dropped, an outside Rust module or C header after it included; and `$$` was unescaped as make's in rustc's files too, where it is two dollars (`#[path = "c$$d.rs"]` refused as an undeclared `c$d.rs`). Now:
  - lines end at `\n` alone;
  - rustc's file (escaping only a space, `\ `) is read from its one `name:` line per file, and every rule must list exactly those names, so a name with a newline, or one starting with `#`, cannot pass for others;
  - `clang -MD`/`gcc -MD` (`\ `, `\#`, `$$`, 2N+1 backslashes before a space, continued with ` \`) must hold one rule; a name with a backslash or tab, an undoubled `$`, a bare `#` or a line neither ending nor continuing the rule is refused, as is an escaped space where the file the other reading names (one ending in a backslash, which gcc does not escape) exists, and a `$` where its undoubled twin exists (gcc before 10 does not escape `$`);
  - clang prints a name's backslash as `/`: an outside header whose path a directory on the way could also hold with a backslash (`bs\x.h` beside `bs/x.h`) is refused, as is one that does not exist;
  - review (PR #178): `/out/\x.h` printed as `/out//x.h` became the decoy `/out/x.h` once made a path, and the twin check compared only a name's part before its first backslash (empty there). A name with an empty step (`//`) or a trailing `/` is now refused before it is made a path, and the twin check also reads each backslashed entry with its `\` as `/` and refuses where that is the header or a directory on its way. Real builds `\x.h` and `\\/x.h` beside a decoy `x.h` (refused; each fails on 2a42258).
  - each refusal says "Peeky cannot read the compiler's dependency file unambiguously: …" and fails the build's preparation.
  - Real builds (each fails on main): Rust modules `c$$d.rs`, `$$.rs`, `e$f.rs`, `a b.rs` (read), a U+2028 module before an outside one (refused); C headers `g\u2028h.h` before `after.h`, with `$`, `$$` and spaces (hashed by their names, decoys not), and `bs\x.h` beside a decoy `bs/x.h` (refused). Unit tests for each producer's names and refusals.
- **A range-`for`'s structured binding names nothing in a lambda in its range** (#169, from round 6 of PR #159's review; withheld, never wrong). In `for (auto [t, u] : make([&] { long t = 40; return id(t); })) {`, the header's binding counted for the whole statement holding the stop, a lambda in the range's expression included: stopped in `id()`, frame 1 (the lambda) withheld its own `t` as "the code here names the structured binding `t`", and on macOS the binding's own row in the loop's body was withheld the same way (the lambda's `long t` on its line was read as another declaration of `t`).
  - A frame whose function is wholly on the stop line (a lambda there) is read as that function's body alone, where the line opens one function's or lambda's body (`Statements.block_storage(body=True)`; several: the line is read as before). A binding's own row is told by the declarations on its line outside the lambda bodies there. A lambda declaring its own `t` on the header's line still gets #140's note in a caller frame (declared on the frame's line, inside a call): a return address does not say whether the declaration has run.
  - At the line's first instruction (a breakpoint on the header, an unoptimized build: #105's statement start), none of the line's statements has run, so a range-`for` whose header ends the line with its body's `{` has not declared its binding yet: `t` is the outer local, which the range's expression reads, and `--set t=7` changes it (`FB=7`). The binding still counts where the loop's body has code on the line, at the header's later stops, and in `main`'s caller frame on the header line: a return address there may be in the header's own calls (`begin()`, `operator*`, a binding's `get`), which Peeky cannot tell from the range's.
  - A multi-stop line (#157) before the run: judging each stop, as the issue asked, would accept nothing more. LLDB's later stops on a line, one per block, are part-way through it, where #105 makes no change (with every stop judged, `r159f/g6` ran and each stop refused `--set t.x=50`: the loop body's `Q t` "declared on line 6, not run yet", the inner block's "part-way through that line"). So the check still judges each function's first stop, and its refusal now says where a later stop names another declaration: `(the line's later stops in main, in other blocks, name another `t`, but each is part-way through the line, where no change is made: to change that one, stop at a line that starts in its block)`.
- **A field change picks the same-named local the debugger shows** (#158, seventh agent evaluation, C++ `sched`). `--at 32 --set t.deadline=-30` was refused (`several locals named 't' of different types, and Peeky cannot tell which one the debugger shows`), though at line 32 only the loop's `Task t` is in scope; the other `t`, a later range-`for`'s `const Task &t`, is in another block. Set's type walk took every declaration of the name in the function.
  - Now the type is that of the declaration the debugger shows at the stop, as #140's reads judge it (`shown_declaration`: the one whose block holds the pc, the innermost where blocks nest), at the stop (`_root_type`, and the previews' `_typed`) and before the run (#149's check, `never_settable`): for each address of the `--at` line's code, the declaration whose block holds it, so the probe is refused only if no stop of the line singles one out. The build's types tell declarations apart by file and line only, so two of one name and different types declared on one line are refused (`… of different types on line 10, which Peeky cannot tell apart`).
  - A plain name selects the one the code names: where an inner block's `t` shadows an outer one, LLDB lists both by where they are declared (`t @ same.c:22`, `t @ same.c:20`), and `t` was not found; it now reads and changes the inner (the worker marks that row `selected_as: "t"`), and `t @ same.c:20` still names the outer. Not where another listed `t` is no automatic local or parameter (a block's `thread_local`), nor where the stop's declaration cannot be singled out.
  - Review of PR #159: a plain `t` chose by the debugger's blocks alone, so it named locals the code does not. clang gives a C++ `while`'s or `switch`'s condition variable (`while (long t = next(n))`, `switch (short t = …; t)`) no block of its own, so after the statement `t` read, and `--set t=500` changed, that dead variable instead of the outer `int t` the code reads (macOS, Linux lldb-dap 18 and 20). Now `_named_where` reads each candidate's scope from the source (`Statements.scope_lines`: to the `}` of its block, or to the end of the statement whose header declares it, its body and an `if`'s `else` included): one declared below the line names nothing yet (before `long t = 20;`, `t` is the outer one: it was withheld as declared below), and a header's ends with its statement. Where a scope cannot be read, nothing is chosen. A parameter shadowed by a block's local is a candidate too (`t @ param.c:3` was unmatched, so `t` was not found).
  - Caller frames are judged where the call is: their address less one, as LLDB reads them, not the return address, which lies past the call's block when the call is its last statement (`get t --frame 1` read the outer `int t = 10`, not the block's `long t = 20`). So are #140's which-declaration-is-shown, #121's jump checks (a `goto` past `long t = 7;` into a block whose last statement is the call showed `t`'s leftovers in the caller frame; on main too), and the frame's function. The rules about where the frame resumes (a declaration still running, one on the line) keep the return address.
  - Before the run, only each function's breakpoint location on the line is judged: its lowest address that runs before the function returns. Code after the function's last epilogue row (clang's exception landing pads and cleanups) is never stopped at: a `}` line whose only code is one (`closing.cpp:11`) ran to `NO_STOP`; it is refused before the run again, as on main.
  - Review of PR #159 (round 2): a header a macro writes (`#define WHILE_DECL(decl) while (decl)`, `WHILE_DECL(long t = next_val(n)) {…}`) was read from the raw source as a plain statement, so its variable's scope ran to the block's `}`, and after the loop `t` read, and `--set t=500` changed, the dead `long t` (macOS, Linux). Scopes are now read from the translation unit as the compiler preprocessed it (#144's `Preprocessed`), mapped back to the source's lines; where a unit cannot be preprocessed, or the units holding a header disagree, nothing is chosen. And a frame a signal interrupted (just above `_sigtramp`, `__restore_rt` or the arm64 vDSO's `__kernel_rt_sigreturn`) stopped at the instruction itself, not at a return address, so it is matched as it is, as LLDB reads it: less one, `main` was judged in the block before its loop and withheld its `t`.
  - Review of PR #159 (round 3): on `long t = 11; while (t > 20) t--;` the scope was read from the header, the last statement on the line naming `t` (which #121's rules use: a later end only withholds more), so `t`'s scope was that line, and on the next lines the outer `int t` was chosen and changed (`set t @ twostmt.c:3: 1 -> 9` while the program printed `B=12`; any later `while`, `if`, `switch`, `for`, `do` or block naming it, C and C++, clang and GCC). `Statements.declaration(..., declaring=True)` now gives the one statement that declares the name (a declarator introduces it; else the only one naming it, as a structured binding's), and none where two might: the scope, the jump checks (a label after it on the line counts) and the `#include` check read that one. And a function of the program named like a signal trampoline (`sigtramp`, `__restore`) is no trampoline: only a frame of that name outside the program's sources is.
  - Review of PR #159 (round 4): a C++ declaration that is a `while`'s, a `do`'s or a `switch`'s whole body, without braces (`while (n++ < 2) long t = 8;`), has no block from clang either, and was read as in scope to the enclosing `}`: after it `t` read, and `--set t=4242` changed, the dead one (and with a struct outer `t`, `--set t.x=50` was refused before the run). A declaration after a header's `)`, `else`, `do` or a label there now ends with its own `;` (`Statements._sole_statement`, walking back to where its statement begins). A C++ structured binding (`auto [t, u]`, a range-`for`'s, an `if`'s) now hides an outer local of its name as #144's block declarations do: clang 18 on Linux describes none in a range-`for` over pairs, so LLDB listed only the outer `t`, which read 1 (and changed) where the program printed 5 and 7 (on main too); the outer row is withheld (`not what the code reads`). And in a caller frame only the declaration the name means is judged by #140's still-running rule: another block's `long t;` with no code of its own withheld the outer `t` (on main too).
  - Review of PR #159 (round 5): the binding scan read every token of a statement, so a lambda's `auto [t, u]` (in `auto lam = [&]() { … };`) hid the function's `t` for the rest of the function, withheld as "names the structured binding" while the program read 1. What braces in a statement hold (a lambda's body, an initializer) is a scope of its own: neither its bindings nor its classes count. And a binding with a use on its line (`{ auto [t, u] = …; use(t); }`) is now read as the declaration it is, so a binding shadowing a parameter is chosen for `t` (it was not found).
  - `describe`'s `mutable:` line names such locals in C and C++ (`Locals of one name with different types (main: t): a change to a field or element follows the one the debugger shows at the stop …`). The guide and COOKBOOK 7.1 give the rule.
- **What is read through a noted local carries its note** (#148, found by the review of #145). A C/C++ local Peeky shows but cannot vouch for (#140: a `do` body's first line, below a `setjmp` call) carried its `note`, but its fields and elements read as plain values: with `struct pt q = { n, n + 1 };` on a `do` body's first line, `q.x` read `1` at hit 1 (a leftover: `n` was 0), in probe captures, `session get q`'s children and so the evidence; below a `setjmp` call, `q.x` and `arr[1]` likewise.
  - The worker keeps each noted local's note with its expansion handle (`self.notes`, as #108's taints are kept), so everything listed through that handle carries it, at any depth: fields, elements, a pointer's pointee (`sp[0]`, `sp[0].y`), and the elements of a value view's decoded children (a #106 preview, and whatever another preview lists through a handle). Each says what it is read through: ``read through `q`: may be leftovers, or …``. A probe gives one warning for a local and what is read through it (`q, q.x, sp[0].y: May be …`); `session get` marks such children `[note]` under the local's own `note:` line, and JSON rows and children carry `note`, which the evidence takes from the rows.
  - Where the local's note refuses a change (its declaration may still run), none of them is `[settable]`, and `--set q.x=5` is refused as `q`'s is (it already was, through the root's rule).
- **A block's `static` or `extern` no longer passes for the outer local** (found by the review of PR #140; on main too). Inside `{ extern long gcount; total += use(gcount); }`, `get gcount` gave the outer local's `6 [settable]` while the code read the global (1); so did `{ static long q = 2; use(q); }` with an outer `long q = 1` (macOS and Linux). lldb-dap lists neither: clang declares a block's static in the function's scope (listed among globals) and a block's `extern` not at all, so the outer automatic stood under the name.
  - Now the source (`Statements.block_storage`) is read for a `static`, `extern`, `thread_local` or C++ `using` declaration of the shown local's name in a block around the stop, after the local's own declaration and up to the stop's line: the row says `not what the code reads` and `--set` is refused. A macro that may declare one (`macro_hazards`' new `declares`) given that name, or one without arguments, or a directive there, gives `may not be what the code reads`, withheld too. Nothing changes outside those blocks.
  - Review of PR #144: still shown under the hidden name were a block opening on the stop line (`{ extern long gcount; total += use(gcount); }`), a declarator after an initializer's braces (`static long a[2] = {1, 2}, q = 3;`, which the cheap pre-read stopped at), and after a type's body (`static struct { long v; } q = { 5 };`); and an enumerator (`enum { q = 7 };`) and a block's function declaration (`long use2(long);`), now treated as hiding too. No longer claimed: a static an `#if 0` leaves out (skipped), one under another conditional (`may not be what the code reads`), a prototype's parameter (`extern long helper(long q);`), and a macro declaring a static whose expansion names another (`ONCE;`). A `thread_local` is listed by the debugger apart (`q @ s4.cpp:38`), and the row now says so.
  - Review of PR #144 (round 2): four more shapes still showed the outer local, settable: an enum nested in a struct's body (`struct S { enum { q = 3 } kind; long v; } s;`; C gives its enumerators the block's scope), and three macros (`MAKE_ENUM;` expanding to `enum { q = 7 }`, `DECL_ENUM(q);`, `PROTO(use3);` expanding to `long use3(long)`). Rather than guess at more macros, Peeky now reads the translation unit as the build's compiler preprocesses it: the same compile with `-E`, its line markers mapped back to the program's files and lines (`c_source.Preprocessed`; each run of a system header's lines one `#include` line), once a session for each unit holding the stop's file (`-MD` says which). Macros, conditionals and included fragments are then exactly what the compiler saw: a static an `#ifdef` of something defined keeps is named, one under an undefined `#ifdef` is not (both were `may not be what the code reads`). Where the stop's or the local's line is not singled out (a file included twice), a system header is included in the block, the units disagree, or preprocessing fails, the row is withheld (`may not be what the code reads`). Also: C enumerators of an enum anywhere in a block's statements (a struct's body, a cast) hide the local; a C++ class or enumeration a block within the local's declares by its name (`struct q { ... };`) hides it (in the local's own block the variable hides the class); and a brace opening on the stop line counts as a block only where it opens statements (a block, `({`), not a type's body or an initializer. Preprocessing costs about 0.025 s for a C file including `<stdio.h>` and 0.1 s for the `stock` evaluation's C++ file (libc++ `<map>` and `<string>`: 2.96 MB, 62,602 lines, read back in 0.02 s), once a session for each unit.
  - A stop on a `setjmp` call's own line (review of PR #144, Low) no longer gives locals declared below it the `longjmp` note: a `longjmp` returns past the call, never to the line's first instruction, so they are withheld as below the stop.
  - `setjmp` (review of PR #140, Low): the note marked every local of a program using setjmp, `helper`'s and `before`/`volatile tries` above the call included, and changes there were refused as "cannot place the stop". Now every function is judged as usual; only in a function that calls setjmp (its family's names, or a macro expanding to one, also from headers outside the program: `returning_twice`), below the call, is a local declared at or below the stop shown with a note (`may be leftovers, or its value from before a longjmp`) instead of withheld, and a change to it refused for that reason. Where the function's calls cannot be read (a directive in it), every line counts as below one. Merged with #141, which refused every change in a program using setjmp ("no value can be changed in this program", `unsettable_everywhere` in `inspect --json`) because it had no declarations: such a program now has them, so that reason applies only where the debug information could not be read, and per-local refusals below a setjmp call come from this rule.
- **A C/C++ local declared below the stop is withheld whatever else its line runs** (sixth agent evaluation, C++ `stock`). At `--at 22` on `std::string name = trim_left(entry.substr(0, colon));`, `long qty` (line 23) showed `6712214884 [settable]` at hit 1 and the last pass's `3` at hit 2, and `--set qty=99` reported a change line 23 then overwrote.
  - Cause: the declared-below rule (#74) withheld a local only when all of the line's code came before the declaration's, and otherwise showed it. clang attributes the exception handler that destroys line 22's `std::string` temporaries to line 22, placed after line 23's code, so the rule gave up and showed the leftovers: a fail-open path.
  - Fix: it fails closed. A stop whose address comes before the declaration's first code is proven above it (`declared at line 23, below this stop`), whatever else the line runs; where Peeky cannot place the stop (code of the line after the declaration's, as a cleanup or a struct's member initializer has; a label, where a `goto` can return above it; a caller frame's declaration line with no code), the local is withheld all the same, saying why it may not be initialized. A local declared on the stop line, at its first instruction, in a function with a label is withheld likewise. Both were shown before: the first pass of a backward `goto` read leftovers.
  - Sweep (real builds, macOS and the Linux `cpp`/`cpp20` lanes): declarations below lines with temporaries, in `try` blocks, range-`for` bodies, `while` bodies with a condition variable, below a `cleanup` variable and a compound literal, and after a label: every local below the stop is withheld and every one above read. Before, the temporaries, `try`, compound-literal and label shapes showed leftovers. Unchanged, by earlier decisions: a line a loop jumps back to (a `do` body's first line), programs with `setjmp`, and optimized builds.
  - Review of PR #140: a local declared below the stop still showed leftovers, `[settable]`, when another declaration shared its name: an earlier loop's `qty`, a static `qty` (clang declares it in the function's scope), the loop's `n`, a lambda's parameter `b` on the stop line. The rule gave up unless every declaration of the name in every function with code on the line was below it. It now judges only the declaration the debugger shows (`shown_declaration`): an automatic local or parameter of the function running the stop's address whose block holds it, the innermost one; statics are listed among globals. Where that cannot be singled out (the address unknown, two in one block), any declaration of the name below withholds it. The stop line's own declaration (#92) is judged the same way. A declaration line with no code whose statement's end is unknown now says the local may not be initialized.
  - Notes on rows (PR #140 follow-up): Peeky shows those values but now says on the row it cannot vouch for them (`note` in JSON; `note:` under the row in `inspect` and `get`, `[note]` with one warning per note in a probe). A local declared on a line a loop returns to, at its first instruction, where its declaration has not run on this pass: `may be the last pass's value, or leftovers on the first pass: this line is reached again by the loop` (or Peeky cannot tell whether a loop returns there), and `--set` there is refused, as its declaration runs after the stop (it was allowed, and overwritten). Locals declared above the loop are live there and get no note. In a program that uses `setjmp` (or whose debug information could not be read), each automatic local (not a parameter or static) says Peeky does not judge its initialization. Optimized builds already warned once per output (probe, session start, `inspect`, `get`); `inspect --json` now carries that `warning` too (a `get --json` row carries `optimized`).
- **A reference into a dropped Box or Vec shows only its pointer** (#110, found by the review of #103). `let r = &*b; drop(b);` left `r.x` reading freed memory through the address `b`'s own row hid. In the innermost frame of an unoptimized build, a reference now reads while the code still uses it (the MIR's liveness: its borrow keeps the target), or when every path back from the stop reaches a use with nothing since that can move, drop or write (no call, drop, partial write, or write to or end of a borrowed local); otherwise it shows only its address. At a line breakpoint only where the line starts counts: LLDB's lowest address in each lexical block. The stop's column picks the statements whose code it can be, and a block is passed over where every block branching there ends with the line's own call or branch in the same lexical block, which the IR shows by order in its IR block (rustc lays a block out after the block first branching to it). A call LLVM inlined (`#[inline(always)]`, even at opt-level 0) leaves its result's store after its body, which counts too; code of the line no statement explains counts anywhere in its block. At a step or a pause any statement of the line counts. Two review rounds showed that proving a dead reference's target unchanged needs the borrow checker itself (interior mutability, `&mut` aliases, loops, drop glue), so it is not attempted. A raw pointer (also `NonNull`, `AtomicPtr`) keeps no borrow and shows only its address. A reference into a shared reference parameter's target, one at an enclosing scope's variable initialized at the stop, a parameter never reassigned that holds references (by value: where initialized), and a local only ever assigned constants read, unless borrowed `&mut` or raw. A write through a reference is not a reassignment of it. A stop in another of the crate's files gates a borrowing binding; with `#[collapse_debuginfo]` in the crate every statement of the line's blocks counts. A name bound twice that the stop cannot tell apart reads only if every binding does.
- **A macro's own breakpoint stops are told apart** (#115). `println!`, `assert_eq!` and other macros that declare variables of their own get a DWARF lexical block per binding scope, and LLDB stops once in each, all at the macro's column; #110 judged each stop as any of them, so `let k = r.x; println!("k = {}", k);` showed only `r`'s address at every stop, the first too. The stop's pc now names its block: `application_symbols` also records each function's lexical blocks (address ranges, the names each declares; `rust_symbols.code_blocks`, from the unit dump Peeky already reads), and `stop_block` finds the innermost one holding the innermost frame's pc (`_unslid`). Blocks are named by the chain of names they and the blocks around them declare. `ir_functions` also reads each IR instruction's lexical block, named the same way (a block that declares nothing has no DWARF block of its own), so a stop's block is told apart only where the DWARF, the IR and the MIR have as many blocks with its chain and just one of the line's MIR scopes and IR blocks has it (`IrFunction.agrees`, `Body._in_block`); then only the line's places in that scope count, and code of the line no statement explains always counts. At a line breakpoint (the line's lowest address in the block) the stop is at or before the IR's first instruction of the line there that surely leaves code (a call, a store of bytes, a branch on a value); its place is known up to the calls around it, so places past the MIR block of the IR's next call go (`Body._cut`; a call LLVM inlined widens that by one call). Two scopes declaring the same names (two `for item in items` loops) are told apart by the line's places (`stop_scope`), which also picks the binding lldb-dap shows. Only innermost frames use it, and not `#[inline(always)]` frames. On the 27 fixture crates, of 207,439 verdicts at 14,028 stops, 1,036 of the 33,961 gated verdicts now read (605 of 11,004 at breakpoints, 431 of 22,957 at steps), 3 unknown ones are proven, and none became stricter. In all 302 functions the IR's blocks match the DWARF's by chain.
- **Setting strings to a text** (#137 part 2). The sixth evaluation's C++ agent could not set `name` to `"apple"` in `stock`, only shorten `colon` and restore it. A `--set`, `--set-at` or `session set` value in double quotes is now a text (JSON's string syntax), written where it is provably safe and refused otherwise, nothing written (`src/peeky/text_sets.py`):
  - C `char[N]`: the text and a NUL when both fit in N (from the type the debug information gives); the bytes after the NUL stay. C++ `std::string`: the bytes, a NUL and the new size, in its own short buffer or its heap buffer, within its current capacity, never reallocating. libc++ (macOS): where the text is held, its size and capacity come from the debugger's `__s`/`__l` members (bit fields included), and the short buffer must lie inside the string; `__cap_` less two is the room counted on a heap buffer, as both of libc++'s layouts keep at least that. libstdc++: `_M_dataplus`, `_M_string_length` and the 16-byte buffer, the data pointer read from memory (clang leaves `_Alloc_hider` incomplete) and checked against the members, so the short buffer is written only when the string uses it. Rust `String`: the bytes and `len`, within `cap`, from the build's DWARF layout, only where the MIR proves the local initialized (#108: a moved String's buffer may be freed). Java: java-debug makes a new String (the launcher now allows a quoted value for a variable declared `java.lang.String` only, marked `peekyTextSet`; an older bundle says to rebuild). Python already evaluated string literals.
  - Refused: `char *`, `&str`, references, fields and elements (C, C++, Rust: copy into a local), a text longer than the storage, a NUL in a C array's text, a layout that does not check out (a string not yet constructed). Before the run, a Rust text for a `&str` or a non-String local, and any C/C++/Rust text for a field, are refused (#138), and a Rust `String` takes no integer.
  - Debuggers: lldb-dap 21 (macOS) writes memory (`writeMemory`), so every case works. lldb-dap 18 and 20 cannot: an array's (or a libstdc++ short buffer's) printable-ASCII text is written one element at a time through `setVariable`, a libstdc++ string needs lldb-dap 20's `readMemory` to tell where its text is, and heap text (a long `std::string`, every Rust `String`) is refused.
  - Every byte and the new size are read back (memory where the debugger reads it, else each element); anything else closes the session. All `--set` rules apply first (#133 statement start, #140 not yet declared, #105 memory, optimized builds). Rows of text locals Peeky can change say `[settable]` (a `std::string` or Rust `String` only where the debugger writes memory).
  - Tests: real builds in `tests/native/test_native_text_sets.py` (fits, exactly full, too long, `char *`, a field, NUL, non-ASCII, `session set`), `tests/cpp/test_cpp_text_sets.py` (SSO, heap, growth within capacity, too long, a reference; libc++ on macOS, libstdc++ in the `cpp`/`cpp20` lanes), the `stock` case (`name="apple"` gives `STOCK:apple=7,pear=3`) in `tests/cpp/test_cpp_eval6.py`, `tests/rust/test_rust_text_sets.py` (within and beyond capacity, `&str`, a moved String) and `tests/java/test_java_text_sets.py`; `tests/unit/test_text_sets.py` covers each layout against a fake debugger.
  - Review 1 (PR #164): (H1) a Rust text set left live `&str`s holding new bytes at old lengths (`head = &s[..2]` read `[61, c3]`: not UTF-8, undefined behavior); a String is now changed only where no local that may point into it is used after the stop (`OwnedLocals.borrow_live`: borrows, what is built from them, and what a call given one may store it into), or Peeky cannot tell. (M1) `const std::string` was changed and marked `[settable]`: const and volatile strings and arrays are refused, judged from the debug information under any typedef, which also lets a typedef's string or array (`using Name = std::string`) be changed. (M2) A `std::string_view` taken before a change keeps the old size: every `std::string` change says so (`note`, a warning); Rust's borrows are refused (H1). (L1) Java marks followed the type of the value held: `Object o = "x"` was `[settable]`; declared types now come from the class files (locals, fields, an array's own type). (L2) javac's constant locals (`final int fk = 5`, `final String fc = "const"`) were "changed" while the program printed the constants: a Java local the code never reads (no load of its slot in its scope) is refused and unmarked. Nits: a libc++ heap string's room is its `capacity()` exactly (`__cap_` is the allocation, or half of it in the standard layout, told by where the short buffer lies); a libstdc++ string in its own buffer is marked `[settable]` with lldb-dap 20.
  - Review 2 (PR #164): (H1) the MIR prints a trait object without its lifetime, so a `Box<dyn Fn + '_>`, `Vec<Box<dyn Fn + '_>>`, `Box<dyn Show + '_>`, `Box<dyn Iterator + '_>` or `Pin<Box<dyn Future + '_>>` holding `&s[2..]` counted as holding no borrow; the test is inverted: a local holds no borrow only where its type provably holds no pointer (numbers, `String`, arrays, tuples and standard containers of those), and a pointer copied out of a local's Box (`vec![…]`) points into it. (H2) A guard whose `Drop` reads its `&str` was used only by its drop: a `drop` now counts as a use where the local may still hold a value at the stop (a may-be-initialized dataflow, so a Box moved into `drop(b)` before the stop is not counted again at the scope's end). (L1) A Java local a lambda captured before the stop was changed and the lambda kept its copy: Peeky follows the method's bytecode (branches, switches, handlers) from where the stop's line begins and refuses a local no path reads again before storing into it (`does not read it after this stop`), unmarked; where it cannot follow the code, the change carries a note. Review 3 (PR #164): (L1) an address cast to an integer (`s.as_ptr() as usize`) held a pointer no type showed, and `unsafe` code read invalid UTF-8 through it after a change: a crate whose sources use `unsafe` anywhere (a token scan outside comments and literals) now takes no Rust text at all, which is simpler and sounder than following every pointer-to-integer route (casts, `addr()`, `expose_provenance`, `transmute`). (Over-refusals) A crate type without type parameters whose fields the DWARF shows hold no pointer (`Span { start: u32, end: u32 }`, recursively; a type that holds itself is not assumed) no longer counts as holding a borrow (`Vec<Span>` from `parse(&text)`); and a drop counts as a use only where it may run code that reads the borrow (a crate type, a trait object, a closure, an unknown type), not for `Vec<&str>` and the standard containers and views of references. Review 4 (PR #164): (M1) a shebang line (`#!/usr/bin/env run-it "`) opened a string in the scan for `unsafe` and hid an `unsafe` block, and the change left invalid UTF-8: the scan is now #163's (`rust_lexer.scan` and `rust.unsafe_source`, taken whole at #163's merge, 8cf32ba): a shebang is skipped as rustc skips it, a source Peeky cannot read as rustc does counts as unsafe, as do `#[no_mangle]`, the `asm!` macros and a nightly feature, in every file of the crate; a text set in such a crate is refused with #163's reason. (Over-refusals) A crate type holding a `Box<u32>` (a pointer in rustc's DWARF) or a C-like enum counted as holding a borrow: `free_types` now follows a `Box` to what it owns, and takes enums and unions. (Nit) `Rec { next: Option<Box<Rec>> }` was allowed only because an `Option`'s type argument (on its `Some` variant in DWARF) was missed, against the docstring: `free_types` is now a reachability check (free unless a reference, raw pointer, trait object or unknown type is reachable), which judges a type that holds itself by everything else it holds, as the docstring now says. Merged with #160 (3ff55f9): a stop at a later one of a line's breakpoint locations in its function (`place` 2 of 2, a loop header's) is judged at every place of the line, not only before the line's first code. Merged with #166 (782407a): one Java bytecode decoder, `java_bytecode.decode` (lengths, branches, switches, the slot each instruction reads or writes), serves both #161's loop headers and the reads of a local after a stop; the class reader keeps each method's code and exception handlers for both. Merged with #163 (8cf32ba): its `rust_lexer` and `unsafe_source` replace the copies review 4 took (every crate file scanned, nightly features counted, only `r#unsafe` skipped), and the text refusal quotes its reason. Merged with #176 (2b498e9): the text rules sit in its Common cases (7.1 to 7.3) and section bodies as list items, and AGENT_GUIDE's `What:` line names them.
- **Several interventions at their own lines and hits in one probe** (#137, parts 1 and 3; part 2 above). The sixth evaluation's C++ agent shortened `colon` at `stock.cpp:22` and restored it at line 23, so `std::stol` still read the quantity, in a session of 14 calls: one `--set` at line 22 made line 23 throw.
  - `probe --set-at [PATH:]LINE[@HIT] name=value` (repeatable; in the `--at` file) changes a value at hit HIT (default 1) of LINE, counting that line's own stops, captured or not. The probe sets a breakpoint at each such line too, captures only at `--at`, and keeps each breakpoint until its line's changes are made, past `--hits` if need be (`--until hits` ends once both are done). Changes at one stop are made in the order given; one at a captured stop gets after-set rows. `--set`/`--set-hit` are unchanged, and both count toward the 8 changes a probe may make.
  - A change whose stop never comes is reported (a warning and `unapplied` in JSON), never dropped silently. Each change follows `--set`'s rules at its own stop, and one refusal fails the probe, naming the stop and how many changes were already made (half a linked change is not the experiment asked for). A `--set-at` line the debugger moves (no code of its own) is refused, never applied at the next line. A candidate edit maps the lines like `--at`'s; remote workers take `set_at` too. C/C++ previews (#136) are read where values are captured, after-set rows included, and not at a `--set-at` stop that captures nothing.
  - Evidence (schema 10): `runs.sets` lists a `--set-at` as `[name, value, "LINE@HIT"]` (so `queries diff`'s `(sets)` row tells runs apart by them); `interventions` gains `line` (where it was made) and `capture_hit` (the captured hits before it; older runs' changes were at their captured stop); `queries set_effect` lists changes in the order made, with `line`, a change at an uncaptured stop as its own row; `queries wide` has a `sets` column naming the changes made since the previous row's values; `max_pause_ms` counts every stop. With #138's refusal before the run (PR #149, merged first), each change is judged against its own line's functions: a `--set` at the `--at` line, a `--set-at` at its own (`offset` at `scale`'s line, a parameter kept in a register, is refused naming that line; a `--set s.end=5` at main's line beside a `--set-at` in `scale` is no longer judged where `scale` has no `s`).
  - Review 1 (PR #151): `@HIT` counts every stop of the line, in any function, so a one-line lambda's stops count with its caller's (`auto f = [](long v) { return v + 1; }; total += f(v);`: `6@2` changed the lambda's `v`, and nothing said so; Python too). Each change now names the function whose variable it changed (`(--set-at line 6@2, in main::$_0::operator())`; `function` in JSON and in `interventions`, schema 11), and a `--set-at` line that stopped in several functions gets a warning listing each one's stops. A requested change that never happened (`--set-at`, or a `--set` whose `--set-hit` never came) makes the probe exit with 4 (`status: completed (1 requested change not made)`, `unapplied`): its values are evidence, but the experiment did not happen; a `--hits` shortfall alone still exits 0.
  - Review 2 (PR #151): a `--set` whose `--set-hit` never came beside a `--set-at` that was made said "No intervention was applied"; the warning now names the `--set` (`--set b=5 was not applied: the program stopped 4 time(s) at line 12, before hit 9`). A Rust generic's instances, whose frames differ only in a symbol hash, counted as one function (no warning for `tf::<A>` and `tf::<B>` on one line): the stops are now kept per frame, and the worker names an instance with its type arguments from the IR (`gen::tf<gen::A>`, `instance` on a change), in warnings, changes and refusals before the run. When `--at` is also a `--set-at` line, one warning covers it. `peeky-probe` exits as `peeky probe` does (4 for a change never made). A refusal before the run names the functions sharing a reason together, with the reason and its advice once (`in fc and fd, `a.pp` is a pointer: …`).
  - Review 3 (PR #151): instance names cut at 300 characters are marked with `…`, and two that then read alike are told apart by their symbol hashes, in changes and warnings alike; Rust closures are named as the build's debug information names them (`cl::main::{closure#1}`, where LLDB shows escaped `{{closure}}` symbols), in changes, warnings, refusals at a stop and the table's function column. Python's debugger names two lambdas on one line alike, with no column to tell them apart, so the warning says their stops are counted together and a change says it was in one of them. A multi-function warning lists 8 functions and says how many more. `interventions.instance` (schema 12) keeps the label, and `queries wide`'s `sets` uses it.
  - Review 4 (PR #151): a probe table showed `cz::main::{closure#0}` and `{closure#1}`, but its note sent agents to `short_function(function)`, which gives both `cz::main::{{closure}}` (and one name for every instance of a generic), and the store kept no label for captured values. `captures.instance` (schema 13, also in the `evidence` view) keeps the label the table shows; the note gives `instance = '…'` when the table shows one; `sql --schema` and the COOKBOOK say that `short_function` merges closures and generic instances, not only overloads. `queries wide`, `join`, `hits` and `values` do not group by function, so they are unchanged.
  - Tests: real builds in C (a change and its restore at two lines, an unreached change, a refusal at one stop after another applied, a moved line, the evidence, `diff` against a run without them, a candidate edit, a remote worker), C++ (the evaluation's `stock`, `STOCK:apple=7,pear=3`), Rust (a struct field changed and restored); unit tests for parsing, bounds, the stop loop and the schema upgrade.
- **Evidence: one row per hit, and two runs joined by a value** (#139, sixth agent evaluation). The agents wrote three-way self-joins on `captures` for one row per hit (`now`, `w.start`, `width`), and a correlated subquery to link run 3 to run 4 by `v`; `queries diff` pairs only by hit number.
  - `peeky queries wide [--param run=N] [--param phase=observed|after-set]`: one row per hit and phase, a column per selector in capture order, named exactly as captured (`w.start`, `bins[3].hi`; one named like a fixed column becomes `line (selector)`). Cells are the stored text. A cell with no value is NULL and named in `no_value` with why; a selector not captured at that hit is NULL and not named; `caveats` names cells a view or a note qualifies, so a caveated value never reads as a plain one.
  - `peeky queries join --param key=v [--param a=3 --param b=4] [--param phase=…]`: each pair of hits, one per run, whose key has the same stored text, with each run's other selectors (`a:<selector>`, `b:<selector>`); a key value at several hits pairs every hit with every hit (all pairs, documented), then each run's hits the other never matched, or with no key value (`matched: only in run a`). Both runs must have captured the key.
  - Both generate their SQL per run (selectors inlined as quoted literals and identifiers), run it through the bounded read-only `query()`, and return it as `sql` in `--json`, so `peeky sql` can refine it. At most 100 columns: a wider run is refused, never cut short.
  - Tests: the store's unit tests (column order and quoting, NULL cells versus missing ones, views, phases, collisions, the column bound, repeated keys, unmatched and keyless hits, errors) and a real C program probed twice, then `queries wide`, `queries join` and the returned SQL through `peeky sql`.
  - Review 1 (PR #145): three qualified values showed as plain cells, each now named in `caveats` (a JSON object, `{"k":"note"}`; `no_value` is one too, as reasons contain `; `). (1) A C/C++ row's `note` (a `do` body's first line, `setjmp`) was never stored: `captures.note` (schema 8; `queries run` and the `evidence` view show it) keeps every row's note, and a noted cell is named. (2) A moved Rust local's `h.o` (`Some(40)`, stale with the `active-variant` view) was stored with the view alone: stale now wins in `view`, and every view but `memory` and `active-variant` is a caveat. (3) `x` and `X` were one column to SQLite (join's `a:X:1` held x's values): a column name SQLite would take for an earlier one gets ` (2)`. A join key whose values carry addresses says so (`notes`), and `--param masked=1` matches them masked.
  - Review 2 (PR #145): `diff`, `hits` and `values` still read noted and stale leftovers as plain values (a C `do` body's first line: `k 1927662298 vs 547973539` came first in `diff`, note NULL, though both runs computed and printed the same). `diff` and `compare` now name a value a view or a note qualifies in either run (`qualified (run a: note, run b: note): not evidence by itself`) and `diff` ranks those after real divergences and intervention-only ones; `hits` has a `caveat` column and leaves `changed` NULL next to a qualified value; `values` counts `qualified` sightings and orders by the others; `set_effect` names both sides' caveats. `no_addresses` masks only outside quoted text (`masked=1` had paired a `char[32]` `"0x00000001deadbeef"` with `"0x00000002cafebabe"`, and `diff` called such text an address-only difference); a Java String value is never masked. `runs.notes_kept` (schema 9) marks runs saved with their notes: each query showing values of an earlier run says they may be unmarked.
  - Review 3 (PR #145): a `diff`/`compare` row said one thing, and `missing in run b`, `intervention only in run a` or `type …` won over `qualified`, so a noted leftover read plain and came first (a run with `--hits 2` against `--hits all`; an after-set `k` with `--set`). A qualified value's note now adds it to whatever else the row says (`missing in run b; qualified (run a: note)`), and `diff` ranks such rows with the qualified ones.
- **Eighth agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-21-agent-eighth-round.md)): a Java calculator, a Python title grouper, a C++ router and a Rust leaderboard, each built around a new feature (returns, `--set-at`, `std::string` sets, `Vec` fields behind `&self`); all four agents verified a general fix on their first `verify`, after `verify --baseline`, and none saw a wrong value. The eval-fixes branch (`feat/eval8-fixes`) fixes M6, M7 and most Lows (M1 is #181, M2 #182, M3-M5, L19 and L20 #183):
  - `queries diff` notes runs that stopped as often where a hit may not be the same moment in both: places (function, instance, line) that do not pair one to one, a selector's masked values lining up better at other hits (``runs 1 and 2 saw the same values of `op` in another order (run 2's hit 4 matches run 1's hit 5: -)``), or values differing at most hits, naming `queries join` with a value the edit leaves alone; `diff` and `compare` name the runs' labels; every query drops a `stop` column no row fills.
  - Sessions: `step --out` shows the return with the state (`(return): long = 1`; `returned` in JSON); a step the session's breakpoint stops says so; every session command a hint or state names carries `--name X` for a session other than `default`.
  - Probes: `after-set` rows shown whole and equal to before, of a selector that neither is nor holds a change's target, are left out of the table, with a footer; launch warnings are shown once, with the result; a Python candidate's copy note is shorter, once per root and state directory in text, never with `--quiet`, and absent for a self-contained script (standard modules that reach no file or process, `adapters._self_contained`); the inferred Java exit warning says the exit code is unknown and nothing needs doing; after a candidate with `--set`, `compare:` says to run it without them too.
  - `verify` doesn't repeat a checker's only output line under `output:`. `describe` writes C++ standard types as code does (`std::vector<std::string>`) and leaves out `DW_AT_artificial` variables (`__range1`).
  - Docs: per-language pages (`docs/lang/`), linked from AGENT_GUIDE and COOKBOOK; ERRORS' `MUTATION_REJECTED` as lists; COOKBOOK fixes for `(output)` windows, Python returns, labels, candidate lines in `diff`, `no_addresses` grouping, macOS `/private/tmp` and session `--timeout`; `queries wide` and `session list` in the guide.
  - Not done: L14 (every `std::string` change keeps its `string_view` caveat, per #164's review), L4's output order (progress on stderr), L10 (resolved paths are the checked ones; documented).
  - Tests: `tests/unit/test_eval8.py`, `tests/unit/test_docs.py` (language pages' links), and real builds in `tests/native/test_native_eval8.py` (C sessions) and `tests/cpp/test_cpp_eval8.py` (C++ `describe`), each failing on `1ea0e6d`.
  - Review 1 (PR #189): (High) M6 missed a changed `instance`, reorders of values carrying addresses or Java object numbers, and calls left out with values changing (`[A, B, C]` against `[A, C, D]`): places now pair one to one across hits with the instance, values are compared masked and aligned (a longest common subsequence against the hit-by-hit matches), and values differing at most hits get a plain note; a renamed function or moved lines that pair the same way at every hit no longer count as divergence, and the join advice names a value the edit leaves alone. (High) An `after-set` row of an aggregate whose preview hid the change (`s` after `--set s.ninth=2`) was left out: only whole scalars and texts are, never a change target's ancestor. Language pages: Python identities hold within a hit, Java `object_ref` within a run, the copy note inside a repository above, a range `for`'s 1, 2, 2, 2, Rust's `no value yet`, and `step --out`'s return.
  - Review 2 (PR #189): (High) the fallback compared each hit's values as one multiset, so `left=A, right=B` against `left=B, right=A` gave no note, and a missing value equalled the text `None`: values now compare selector by selector, a missing one equal to no text. (High) a Java String set through an alias (`b = a`, then `a[0]="x"`) is a new object with the same text, so `b[0]`'s after-set row was left out: Java rows other than primitives always stay, and a Python row stays when its identity changed. `step --out` docs: an exception, `longjmp`, panic or throw gives a withheld row; nothing only where another stop interrupted the step.
  - Review 3 (PR #189): a selector only one run captured counted as a difference at every hit and could fire the fallback note; the fallback now compares only selectors both runs captured (`diff` lists the others once).
- **Seventh agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-20-agent-seventh-round.md)): a C ring buffer, a C++ scheduler and a Rust moving average; all three agents verified a general fix on their first `verify`, after `verify --baseline`. The eval-fixes branch (`feat/eval7-fixes`) fixes findings 5 and 8-22 (1-4, 7 and 16's same-named locals are #155-#158):
  - `session continue --to LINE` says when another stop came first and exits 3 (`note: stopped at the session's breakpoint (line 30, hit 2) before reaching line 17; `session continue --to 17` again`; `the program exited without reaching line 18`), as `--count` and `--hit` do. No option skips the session's breakpoint: repeating `--to` keeps every stop counted.
  - The note on short frame names (with #151's `instance = '…'` part) shows once per state directory (`$PEEKY_STATE_DIR/notes`), never with `--quiet`; `peeky sql --help`, `--schema` and query tables keep it.
  - `queries diff`: an `after-set` value equal to its run's `observed` value at that hit (a `--set`'s, or a `--set-at`'s at a captured stop) is left out (the intervention did not touch it); one selector's address-only rows are one row (`addresses only, at 4 hits (one row per selector: its first hit)`); runs that stopped a different number of times get a note naming `queries join`, which pairs hits by a value.
  - `verify`'s `reason` (text and JSON) is the checker's last non-empty output line when it only exited with a status (`FAIL [...]`, not `SystemExit: 1`); `checker.error` and `checker exit` keep the status.
  - After a candidate probe, `compare: peeky queries diff --param a=4 --param b=5` names the newest saved run that is the same probe without the edit (same target, `--at`, `--capture`, arguments; no edit, no `--set` or `--set-at`), or says how to make one (`evidence.compare`).
  - `MUTATION_REJECTED` for a field through a C pointer parameter names the function's one call, read from the program's preprocessed translation units (every mention of the name must be the definition, a file-scope prototype or a call by name inside a function the debug information places there; a macro used twice is two calls; an address taken, a member call or a declaration in a function leaves the callers unknown): `push has one caller: ring.c:36 in main, which passes `&r`, where `r.tail` is settable: …`. It says settable only where `set`'s rules hold at a breakpoint on that line (the type walk and storage, the root declared and its statement done above the line, the line starting its statement at its lowest address, not a loop header, no labels or setjmp); else it names the line alone. The advice is a `--set-at LINE@N` (#151), and a `--set-at` refused before the run at its own line gets it too. C++ keeps the general advice.
  - A probe refused before launch (a `--set` or a `--set-at`) prints only the error and its hint (no empty table, status or timing) and exits 2, as any refusal; the `peeky-probe` script too (`probe_exit_code`: 2 refused, 1 not completed, 4 a change not made, #151).
  - `session get` of a selector that does not resolve names the stop (`note: this stop is in main at sched.cpp:30`; the row's `location` in JSON).
  - `describe`: a C struct, union or enum no typedef names is written with its tag (`r (struct ring *)`; C++ and typedef names unchanged), and the `mutable:` line names the struct, tuple and array locals with an integer field or element `set` may change (`Locals with such fields or elements: main: r`; JSON `settable_parts`), judged by `settable_member` over direct fields, an array field's element and a struct field's fields, in the frame's memory (Rust: the IR keeps it there).
  - Rust fields and elements show their Rust primitive (`self.k  usize`, not `unsigned long`) from the base type the application units' debug information declares along the path (through what a reference points at, as LLDB lists it), where LLDB's name spells it; `debugger_type` keeps LLDB's.
  - Docs: `session wait` and program arguments (`--`, `--arg`) in AGENT_GUIDE's command table, a step landing back on an `if`'s line (COOKBOOK 1.1), comparing by value (3.1, 3.5), the exit statuses (ERRORS.md, COOKBOOK 2.7).
  - Tests: `tests/unit/test_eval7.py`, and real builds in `tests/native/test_native_eval7.py` (C), `tests/cpp/test_cpp_eval7.py` and `tests/rust/test_rust_eval7.py`, each failing on `6040934`.
  - Review 1 (PR #165): (13) a call whose line or statement changes the field first (`r.tail = 1; total += push(&r, 1);`, `push(&r, r.tail = 3)`) was called settable; now only a call in its line's first statement, whose statement neither writes the root nor takes its address, is. A call on a statement's second line names the statement's first, and one in another file is advised as a probe `--at other.c:6 --set ... --set-hit N` (`--set-at` names the probe's own file), one in a header gets none. (12) `compare:` matched runs of another build or of the source before an edit on disk: it now needs the same target identity and the unedited source's hash. (11) the reason is the line that says what failed (`FAIL`, an `…Error`, `assert`, a traceback's exception, with up to two indented lines), stdout's before stderr's. AGENT_GUIDE's exit 3 covers a session's `--to`, `--count` and `--hit`; a previewed Rust field keeps `debugger_type`.
  - Review 2 (PR #165): (13) settable was still claimed where the call's statement or a pointer changes the field first (`((r).tail = 2, push(&r, 1))`, `push(&r, RESET(r))`, `a = &r` earlier with `push(&r, (a->tail = 5))`, `saved = &r` with `push(&r, touch())`). The statement is now read preprocessed, and the claim needs no assignment but a plain name's at its start, no `++`, `--` or `&`, library calls only (none for a path through an array, whose pointers need no `&`), and no address of the root taken elsewhere in the caller but `&r` passed whole to a function of the file that only uses fields through it (`pop(&r, &v)`, so the ring keeps its claim). (12) `compare:` paired runs across an on-disk change to another file: the stored target identity now carries a digest of every source the build read (the build manifest's inputs before the edit; Python's `.py` files under the target), and runs saved before it are not named; no schema change. (11) the reason is the first line that starts like a failure (anchored: `ok: … ValueError` and `FAILOVER` no longer match), else the last line, stdout before stderr.
  - Review 3 (PR #165): (13) settable was still claimed where a call in the arguments writes through a pointer into the root (`push(&r, ops[0](r.buf))`, `(*fp)(r.buf)`, `v.fn(r.buf)`, with `buf` the first field; `memset(r.buf, …)`) or the statement first assigns the root (`r = q, push(&r, 1);`). Rather than more cases, the claim now needs the statement to be exactly `push(ARGS);`, `NAME = push(ARGS);` (or `op=`, or a declaration's `int NAME =`), `return push(ARGS);` or `if (push(ARGS) ...)`, NAME not the root, with ARGS and the rest of the `if`'s condition only reading (no assignment, `++`, `--`, `&` or call of any kind; the root only as the `&r` passed and in reads of its integer fields). Library calls lose the claim, the ring's `atoi` among them; the `_lent` exception and the no-other-address rule stay.
  - Merged with #160 (#157, schema 14): where both runs label their stops, `diff` and `compare` pair a hit with the other run's hit at the same stop (another stop is two rows, each naming its stop), address-only rows collapse per selector and stop, and an after-set value is compared with its own stop's observed one; where the stops part at a hit both reached, `diff`'s note and the candidate's `compare:` line name `join` instead of a hit-by-hit diff. The per-pass warning is said every time, the short-name note once per state directory (finding 8). No schema change of this branch's own.
  - Merged with #159 (#158, same-named C/C++ locals): finding 13's caller advice still claims settable only for a root the caller declares once (`declared_through` keeps `declaration`'s default, the later end); `describe` writes C tags within #159's alternatives (`t (struct a or struct b, …)`); #159's probes refused before launch exit 2 (finding 14).
  - Merged with #162 (#155, return values): `diff` pairs a `(return)` row (no stop of its own) by its hit's stop, from that hit's observed rows, so returns of calls that began at different stops are not compared; where `--capture return` alone leaves a hit no observed row on a line that stops several times per pass, the note says pairing by hit cannot be checked and names `join`. #162's Python refusal before launch exits 2 (finding 14).
  - Finding 6 (docs, `docs/agent-first-pass`): AGENT_GUIDE now opens with "First 5 minutes" and 15 "Rules you will hit" (from evals 1-7, each with its COOKBOOK section), keeps a one-line command table and the agent facts (timeouts, `--json`, exit codes, `session wait`, probes versus sessions), and leaves edge-case lists to the COOKBOOK (20.2 KB). The COOKBOOK gains a table of contents with summaries, "Common cases" in its dense sections, lists instead of paragraphs over about 150 words, and 2.9 for the second 2.8; ERRORS.md leads each code with its next step. Checked: every fact of the old guide (a keyed checklist) and every word of the old COOKBOOK still appear.
- **Sixth agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-20-agent-sixth-round.md)): a Rust rate limiter, a C++ stock map and a C histogram; all three agents verified a general fix on their first `verify`. The eval-fixes branch (`feat/eval6-fixes`) fixes findings 5-9, 11-14 and 19-21 (1 and 15-18 are fixed in their own branches; 2, 3, 4 and 10 are #136-#139):
  - `peeky verify --baseline` builds and checks the unedited program like any verify (`baseline: true`, no diff), so an agent sees the checker fail on the bug before trusting `passed: True`; `verify --help` shows `--old`/`--new` as required unless `--baseline`, and a refused edit says which condition failed (`--new equals --old`, `--old matched N times`).
  - `[settable]` on fields and elements: probe rows, `session get` listings and a #106 preview's fields carry it where `set` would change them, judged by the rules `set` applies (`validate_member`; what holds for the root once per listing), never through a pointer, reference or Box, nor in a caller frame. Verdicts are kept per place (the stack's functions and pcs; Rust's stopped frame alone), so a loop's later hits reuse them: with `--hits all` (100 hits, 8 field selectors) Rust takes 4.97 s against `a4e3c77`'s 4.85 s (+2.5%), C 4.39 s against 4.31 s (+2%).
  - `session inspect` says why nothing is settable where a rule decides it (`note: nothing here is [settable]: the stop is not at the first instruction of the statement on line 8 ...`; JSON `unsettable`).
  - `queries diff` puts the run-level rows (`(status)`, `(output)`, `(edit)`, `(source)`, `(line)`, `(args)`, `(sets)`) first, then the first divergence; selectors only one run captured come last.
  - Rust `describe` leaves out a macro's own bindings (`println!`'s `args`): a name the build's MIR spans in another crate's file, declared where the program's sources bind no such name on that line.
  - A Rust reference parameter's type is its Rust type from the build's IR (`&mut limiter::Window`, not `limiter::Window *`), the instantiation's own in a generic body.
  - `session report` no longer prints the worker's request count as `operations: N`.
  - Docs: quick starts at the top of COOKBOOK 7.1 and 7.2, the densest paragraphs as lists, a `MUTATION_REJECTED` hint naming the one action per rule, `--name` on every session command, a `std::map` read by key in a session, `describe`'s lines versus stepping, and `--baseline` in the guide.
  - Review 1: `describe` kept a `#[collapse_debuginfo(yes)]` macro's own bindings out (the debug information moves them to the invoking line, and `args`/`kind` are names std's macros bind too): a name is now hidden only where the function's own MIR body places every binding of it in the program's sources at a declaration of its own. The inspect note took one root's reason for the whole stop (stepped onto `+ w.start;`, `n` was in a temporary while `w.start` could be set): it now names the locals each reason holds for, and appears only when no field or element is settable either. With `--hits all`, marks cost a walk and a MIR proof per selector per hit (Rust 7.63 s against 4.85 s); they are now kept per place. The advice `session step --out` returned part-way through the caller's line, where a change is refused again: the refusals now advise a stop on the caller's line before the call (`continue --to LINE`), checked on the limiter. A `&u8`, `&i8` or `&char` parameter (LLDB's `unsigned char *`, `char32_t *`) showed LLDB's C-string reading under its Rust type; it shows its address. `verify --baseline` says the checker rejects the program only where it ran to an assertion or a nonzero exit.
  - Review 2: `session continue --to` the call's line, the advice for a value reached through a reference, stops before the next call, not the current one (the limiter, stopped in `allow` at `now=10`, got `now=11`): the refusals now advise a probe `--at` the call's line with `--set-hit N`, or `session start --at` it with `--hit N`, and say a stop inside the call cannot change what it already received. In a program using setjmp no function's statements are read, so no stop allows a change; the inspect note and `set` advised `continue --to LINE` there, and now give that reason, with no advice to stop elsewhere.
  - Review 3: `--set-hit N` counts the call line's own stops, not the called function's; they differ when it is called from several lines (stopped in `allow` at its hit 4, the call on `w` with `now=1`, `--set-hit 4` at the call line changed the call with `now=11`). The advice now says so, with how to find N (`--capture <argument> --hits all` at the call line) and `--hits N` beside `--set-hit N`; it no longer says a stop inside the call cannot change what the call received (it received an address): Peeky does not write through a reference or pointer.
