  - Review (Linux `rust` lane, lldb-dap 18, no previews): a raw field of a std type (`counts.len` of an undecoded `Vec`) carried `settable: false`, which an old test said no field row would. Every root, field and element row a change could name now carries `true` or `false`, a decoded `Vec`'s elements too; the std internals reachable raw (a `Vec`'s or `String`'s `len` and `buf`, a Box's pointee) were all `false`, and `set` refuses each.
- **Rust struct and tuple previews** (#106, fifth agent evaluation). `inspect` showed a struct local raw (`s: spans::Span @ 0x…`, a reference parameter only its address) while a `Vec<Span>` read `[Span { start: 1, end: 10 }]`, so agents captured each field. A tuple or struct whose fields are qualified scalars (the composite rules of #98, from the build's DWARF: plain aligned fields, the crate's own structs or tuples, a unit struct, and no struct sharing its path) now previews as derived `Debug` prints it (`Span { start: 2, end: 3 }`, `Point(1, 2)`, `(7,)`, `Marker`), read from memory with `[memory]`, its fields selectable children with Rust types. It needs the containers' proofs: the MIR's (innermost frame) for a local or by-value parameter, the IR's for a reference parameter (shared ones in caller frames too); the MIR's declared type picks the layout. Moved or unproven ones keep their `[stale]` view (#108); nested structs, reference locals and optimized builds stay raw. On a 50-local fixture (40 `Span`s, 5 sixteen-field structs, 5 `Vec<Span>`) all 50 read in one inspection (55 of the 128 reads and fetches one may make), at the same inspect latency as before (about 20 ms per `session inspect` on Apple LLDB). Its first review found that a generic body's MIR types can name a type parameter whatever its name: in `fn show<i8>(p: Pair<i8>, t: (i8, i8))` called with chars, `p` previewed `Pair { a: 65, b: 0 }` and `t` `(67, 0)` from `Pair<i8>`'s layout, and the same held for a `Vec<Pair<i8>>` there (#98), a scalar's MIR type name (#79: `u64` for a usize) and #108's taint (`fn keep<i64>(h: i64)` holding a Box read the freed Box). The IR's template parameters now name each instantiation's type parameters: a MIR type naming one is never the frame's, a primitive named like one may own memory, and #110's aliasing treats it as a pointer. LLDB's name must also spell the layout's type exactly (C names for a generic's primitive arguments, the DWARF's for a tuple). Packed structs are never previewed, and a source naming a field `__0` leaves the crate's tuple structs unread (its DWARF is a tuple struct's). Review 2: any `__0` identifier counts (a macro given `__0` declared such fields; `Num(1, 2)` showed for `Num { __0: 1, __1: 2 }`); a const parameter, which rustc names in the instantiation (`sum<3>`) but never lists, is a value, so const-generic functions keep their previews and Rust types (checked on 1.96, for functions, impl methods and their closures); and a body no function is matched to (a macro's twins) has unknown type parameters, so its `u64` may be a pointer: a twin's `*w = new` through `w: u64` made from `v` had let `v` read the dropped Vec (`[Res { x: 4358 }]`). Of the 18 drop checks on unmatched bodies across the Rust tests, only that one changed. Review 3: a macro can build `__0` with `${concat(__, "0")}` (Peeky's builds allow `#![feature]`), so a tuple struct now previews only where the crate's MIR builds it as one (its aggregate `Point(…)`; `Num { __0: … }` builds one with named fields), the `__0` token rule staying as a backstop; a char const argument (`ch<{CONST#27d96aba666aa2f7}>`) counts as a value; a name that cannot be read is a generic not understood; and an alias graph built while a body's type parameters were unknown is built again once they are known. Review 4: the MIR names a type by its crate-relative path where its name is not unique (`m::Point` beside `n::Point`, `m::Wrapping` beside std's, `b::Num` beside a root `Num`), so constructions are matched by path to the DWARF's (a name alone: the root struct's, else the one struct's of that name); matching names alone had left `vec![m::Wrapping(1)]` raw (main read it) and proved `b::Num { __0: … }` a tuple struct by the root `Num(…)`. Review 5: the MIR in fact names a type from its nearest uniquely named ancestor (`shapes::Point` for `geo::shapes::Point` beside `other::Point`, `Color::Red` for the variant `a::Color::Red`), so the construction proof left `vec![geo::shapes::Point(6, 7)]` raw (main read it) and an enum variant proved `fn Color`'s own `Red { __0, __1 }` a tuple struct. It is dropped: on stable Rust (checked on 1.96) an identifier `__0` needs its token in the source (`concat_idents!` is gone, a binary cannot use a procedural macro it defines, the sysroot's crates need `rustc_private`, and Peeky refuses dependencies, a library target, build.rs, Cargo configuration and RUSTFLAGS; rustc's dependency files bound the sources read), so tuple structs read as on main unless a source (tests included) has an identifier `__0` or a feature gate (`feature(` outside comments and strings, as in `cfg_attr` or `r#feature`), or does not lex. A local's MIR type is matched as a path suffix too (the crate-relative path where several match), and `r#` is dropped (`r#type`, `r#match::Kw` previewed raw). Review 6: `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock` are inputs the compiler may read too, and a TOML comment line is Rust (`#[doc = ""] fields!(__0, __1); ignore! {`, closed by `#}` below the tables, then `include!("../Cargo.toml")`), which declared `Num { __0, __1 }` and previewed `Num(1, 2)`; they are now searched for `__0` as plain text (they need not lex as Rust). A feature gate is only ever the crate root's (checked on 1.96: an `include!`d file may hold no inner attribute, and a `#[path]` module's `#![feature]` is ignored), so that search stays the Rust sources'.
- **A moved or dropped Rust struct, tuple or array reads nothing through its pointers** (#108, found by the review of #103). After `drop(h)` of `struct Holder { b: Box<Pt>, v: Vec<i64>, n: i64 }` (or `consume(h)`, `let g = h;`, `let d = h.b;`), `h.b.x` read the allocator's reuse of the freed Box (777 from a later allocation); so did a tuple's `t.__0.x`, an array's `arr[1].b.x`, a `Box<str>` field's text, and `--frame 1` in a caller that moved `h` into the call. A top-level local LLDB shows with children whose MIR type may own memory (anything but primitives, references, raw or fn pointers and tuples or arrays of them) is now judged by the owned-local proof: proven initialized in the innermost frame of an unoptimized build, it reads whole; proven on a caller frame's call line, its fields read and its pointers show only their address; otherwise it is `[stale]` (`stale` in JSON, with a note), its fields show the moved-from bytes, marked, and every pointer inside it at any depth shows only its address (`[unproven]`). The worker carries the verdict on each expansion handle, so selectors (`h.b.x`), `session get h` listings and nested values (`h.v.len`, `arr[0].b`) follow it. Showing the local as `[unproven]` with no children (as a Box) was measured against this: on the existing Rust fixtures, 267 aggregate locals were shown, 57 were not proven fresh (7 caller frames proven on the call line, 32 moved, dropped or not yet assigned, up to 18 possibly live but unprovable: macro twins, closure and async captures, `println!`'s `args`, optimized builds). Hiding them whole would have lost every field of up to 25 live ones; marking them hides only their pointers, none of which a live fixture read through. Data-carrying enums other than `Option<i32>` were already opaque. Its review found that a field reassigned in place (`h.b = new`, `h.s = ..`, `h.v = ..`) marked the whole struct stale: the drop of the old field ended the local and the store of the new one never made it whole again. Such a drop and the store to the very same place right after it (past storage markers and drop flags; also an element, `arr[i] = new`) now pair as a Box's drop-and-replace does: the store makes the local whole only if it was whole before the drop, and any move or drop of the local in between forgets that. On the review's 10 crates (41 stop lines, about 420 values) only the two reassignment stops changed, both to the right values. A field moved out and stored again (`let d = h.b; h.b = new`) stays stale: that needs per-field tracking.
- **A Rust local the MIR shows unassigned is withheld; so is an async poll's copy after an `.await`** (#132, found by the review of #130). In an `async fn` resumed after an `.await`, `session inspect` showed the poll's own copy of a scalar parameter as stack garbage (`n @ main.rs:14: u32 = 2880154539`, 0xABABABAB, `evaluable`) beside the future's field (`n = 5`), and a local the future does not keep across the `.await` showed a leftover (`tmp = 1`; the first poll set 105). The MIR proofs only gated values Peeky reads through a pointer (#53, #101, #108, #110); a scalar's own bytes were shown raw. The positive proof those use (`OwnedLocals.proven`) is wrong for this: it fails closed on what it does not understand (an async state's field or a closure's capture, a name bound twice, macro twins, collapsed macro lines), so on the sweep below it would have refused 204 more of 12,293 innermost scalar rows than this change withholds, 114 of them in async or closure bodies. Instead a second dataflow follows only whether a local was assigned since its storage began (a `StorageLive`, a loop's next pass or a resumed poll, ends that; a move or drop does not, since #108 marks moved values; across a suspension only what the state saves stays), and a top-level row of a type that does not borrow (references keep #110's gate) is withheld (`unavailable`, `withheld`, a reason; `session set` refuses it) only where some path to a place the stop can be leaves it unassigned: a `let late;` before its assignment, an `if let` binding while its scrutinee's call runs, a poll's copies after an `.await`. Places are narrowed by what lldb-dap shows (every row it lists has the pc inside its binding's lexical block), by the stop's own lexical block where #115 tells it apart, and, after a call or drop of the line, by the edge it returns on (else a cleanup block also entered from an earlier unwind would withhold 13 `Vec`s at `main`'s closing brace); a caller frame counts its line's calls. lldb-dap lists an async parameter's field (the function's own scope) before its copy (a lexical block): the copy is withheld and names the field's row, which reads; where a `mut` parameter's reassignment can have run before the stop, the field is withheld instead (it holds what the caller passed). A main-versus-branch sweep, re-measured on main with #115 (the Rust test programs, 41 with their macro files, not the one whose `env!` needs a build variable; the example crate; two async crates; a line breakpoint at every line, hits 1 and 2, and 25 steps from each function's first line, the innermost and up to three caller frames: 5,569 stops, 48,682 rows), withheld 961 rows main showed, 397 of them in #132's own async tests. In the other fixtures that is 206 rows: 205 unassigned there, all leftovers on main (the deferred `label`, a `let k;`, `if let Poll::Ready(value)` and `if let Some(b)` bindings while the scrutinee's call runs, as addresses and stale numbers), and one `k = 777` at a step on `let r = if flag {`, whose place on the line a step does not tell. In the async crates: 218 unassigned rows (all garbage on main), 27 field rows after a `mut` reassignment (wrong on main), and 113 copies and unkept locals, 53 garbage on main and 60 holding the right value, all on lines the first poll and a resumed one both run (an `.await` line, a loop around it, a callee of the awaited future): the MIR cannot tell which poll is stopped, so the first poll's values are withheld too; a parameter's field row still reads. Before #115's block and the rows' blocks were used, 4 such `k = 777` rows (the line with the `eprintln!` column included) and 85 such async rows were withheld; no row shown since holds garbage. Cost: the dataflow takes a block at a time (each block's storage starts and assignments are read once, as kill and gen masks, and a place's state is its block's entry with each local's last event before it), and what a frame's rows share (the stop's places, its lexical block's scopes, the source's lines) is worked out once per stop; #115's per-row recomputation of which scopes declare a variable is now cached per body, which the reference gate shares. On a 1,511-line function the first `inspect` took 1.16 s (at line 1,504) and 2.48 s (at line 800) before this, 0.57 s and 1.09 s on main, and 0.10 s and 0.11 s now, 7–11% over the gate switched off; on one with 40 more locals (44 and 49 rows), 33 s and 36 s on main and 0.13 s now, 11% over. Not done: telling the first poll from a resumed one (the state's discriminant at run time); macro-twin and unanalyzed bodies are not judged (as before). `gen` blocks are unstable on 1.96 and were not checked.
- **A `--set` the build rules out is refused before the program runs, and rows say which locals are never settable** (#138, sixth evaluation, Rust `limiter`). `probe … --at src/main.rs:10 --set width=9 --set-hit 3` ran to hit 3, then failed with `MUTATION_REJECTED: 'width' … is kept in a register … settable here: none`, and the run's output was lost; the only earlier sign was a missing `[settable]` tag. By-value scalar parameters (`now`, `width`, `limit`) and `let`s the code never borrows or reassigns live only in registers even at opt-level 0 (the IR declares them through `.dbg.spill` copies). The worker now judges a probe's `--set` names after the build and before the debugger starts, from facts no stop changes, in every function that can stop at the `--at` line (code of its own there, an inlined copy, or a call LLVM inlined there): any change in an optimized build (with, for C and C++, whether the debug information gives the local no stack slot there); for Rust, the IR's memory proof (#77) for the root local, its MIR type for a root (only `i16`…`u64`, `isize`, `usize` change), and for a field or element #105's walk of the debug information's types (through a reference, a `Box`, a std type, a packed struct...) and whether the root is in the frame's memory; for C and C++ the same walk for a field or element (a pointer, a union, a bitfield, a root that is not an automatic or parameter in the frame). It refuses only where the set fails in every such function (a closure on the line, a generic's instances, macro twins, an inlined copy): `MUTATION_REJECTED` naming each function's rule (or `NOT_FOUND` where none of them has the local), the `black_box` edit for a register (for a parameter, `mut width` in the signature and `std::hint::black_box(&mut width);` first, since a shadowing `let` leaves the name only partly in memory), and the locals kept in memory in scope there (`settable here: factor, total`); `refused_before_launch: true`, no hits, no output, and no run saved. A line with no code of its own (the debugger moves the breakpoint) or no function's, a crate with its own `#[collapse_debuginfo]` macro (another file's function may stop on the line) and an IR too large to read are left to the stop; so is everything a stop decides (a statement under way, initialization, #132's unassigned locals, #140's declarations). Rows of a Rust integer local (or a field of a local) the IR keeps in a register say `not_settable: "register"` (`[not settable: register]` in probe, `inspect` and `get` text), never with `[settable]`; `describe`'s `mutable:` line lists each function's integer locals kept in a register (`limiter::allow: limit, now, width`). C and C++ parameters are on the stack at -O0 and are still set. Cost: the check reuses what `prepare` read (the IR's memory proof, the MIR bodies, C's declarations and type graph): under 1 ms per name for a Rust root or a C field; a Rust field's first check reads the application units' field types (28-36 ms for the test crates), which the stop reuses. Real builds: the limiter (refused with no run, rows marked, `describe`, and the advised edit setting `width` in the same probe), a crate with a closure sharing a line, a reference field, an unknown name and a local settable at other stops (left to the stop), and C/C++ at -O1 (a local in registers) and -O0 (a field through a pointer refused, a parameter set); each fails on main.
  - Review (PR #149): an index that is not plain digits was refused before the run though the stop applies it: `sp[-1].end=9` and `sp[01].end=9` change `sp[1].end` at the stop (its walk counts `-1` from the array's end and reads `01` as 1), but the pre-launch check gave the selector's own steps to #105's type walk, which takes the debugger's names (`[1]`), and refused ("`sp` is an array: select an element as `sp[0]`"), in Rust and C alike. The check now reads each index as the walk does (`fields.debugger_steps`): leading zeros dropped, a negative index counted from a fixed-size array's length, and nothing judged where that length is not fixed or the index is past the start. A local an async body keeps across an `.await` (in the future's state, which the poll reaches through a pointer at every stop) was refused as "kept in a register" with `black_box` advice that cannot help; the refusal, the row (`[not settable: async state]`), `describe` and `session set` now say so, and advise changing what it is computed from, or copying it after the `.await` into a local the poll keeps itself (`let mut k2 = k; std::hint::black_box(&mut k2);`, then using and setting `k2`), which the test does.
  - Review round 2 (PR #149): "kept in a register" was blamed on the binding asked for when the IR's proof (per name) failed for another binding of the name, or for an inlined copy: `--set 'x @ main.rs:6=9'`, black-boxed, was told to add `black_box(&mut x)` because the `x` of line 4 was a `.dbg.spill` copy, and `y` of an `#[inline(always)]` function got the same advice, which cannot help. The IR's declarations are now kept per name with their line and kind (a slot, a copy, an inlined copy), and the refusal before the run, the row's mark (`another binding in a register (line 4)`, `inlined`) and the stop's refusal name the binding and what to black-box, or say that inlining prevents the change. `settable here:` before the run lists selectors as LLDB names them (`z @ main.rs:12, z @ main.rs:14` where two are in scope) and says fields may be settable (`no root locals (fields where rows mark them [settable])`). A final index that is negative or has leading zeros (`arr[03]`, `sh[-1]`, `np.arr[-1]`, `gr.g[1][-4]` in C and C++, `arr[-1]`, `nested[1][-1]` in Rust) was marked `[settable]` by reads but refused by `set` ("a negative index can be changed only in a Java array", "'[03]' is not a field"); `set` now changes the element the read of the selector shows (and marks), the debugger's `[N]`, where the debugger states the length, and refuses as the read does where it does not.
  - With #146 (C and C++ struct previews) merged: the fields of a preview read from memory keep their marks, judged as `set` judges them, and say why no stop changes them where that is a build fact. A Rust struct parameter in a register (`m: Meters`) is previewed, and its fields (`m.v`) now read `[not settable: register]`, as the check before the run refuses them; a nested preview's fields keep their root's reason. In C and C++, the fields of a root the debug information gives no stack slot in the frame's function (a register or a location list) read `[not settable: register]`, and a static's `[not settable: static]`, the roots `set` refuses for their fields before the run and at the stop; nothing is marked in an optimized build, where every change is refused. Clang keeps every local on the stack at -O0 (and lldb-dap lists no function's static among its locals), so these C marks are covered by a unit test; real builds check that a preview's fields are marked as `set` treats them (a field through a pointer refused before the run, the frame's own struct's `[settable]` and changed), and the Rust parameter's fields, which fail on the branch before this merge.
  - Review round 3 (PR #149): `describe`'s `mutable:` line still called every never-settable name "kept in a register", with `black_box` advice, after the rows and both refusals had learned the real reasons: an always-inlined function's locals (`far`'s `a`, `y`, which already has `black_box`) and a name whose other binding is the one in a register (`x`, black-boxed on line 6, blocked by line 4's). It now gives each name its rows' reason, grouped: `register`; `another binding in a register (line N)` (`bind::shadow: x (line 4)`: black-box that `let`); `inlined` (no edit to it helps; change the value in the caller); `async state`. A generic's instances that disagree give the reason no edit to the binding helps first. Unit tests (the reasons, the text) and the bind crate's `describe` fail on the branch before this fix.
- **Rust `Vec`, `String`, `&str` and `Box<str>` fields read from memory** (#156, seventh evaluation, Rust `movavg`). In `fn push(&mut self, v: i64)`, `self.values` (a `Vec<i64>` behind `&mut self`) showed only LLDB's summary (`alloc::vec::Vec<long, …> @ 0x…`), with a warning about an owned local that may be moved (#53), and `self.values[0]` was "not found … Capture values.len"; in `main`, `w.values` stayed raw beside `w.sum [settable]`. A field (or element) of one of these types now reads as an owned container does, under the proof of what holds it, reached through plain struct and tuple fields and fixed-size array elements only (`fields.held_container`, on the build's DWARF types, never LLDB's names): in what a reference parameter the IR proves live points at (`&self` in every frame, `&mut self` only in the innermost; not where #110 shows its target dropped), and in a local (or what its `Box` holds) the MIR proves initialized at the stop, in the innermost frame (`w.values`); a moved, dropped or partly moved struct stays `[stale]` (#108). The field's DWARF type picks the layout (`Vec<i64>`, which LLDB names `Vec<long>`), so a generic's instances read as themselves (#130). Nothing is read through a pointer, an enum, a union, a packed struct or a standard-library type (a `RefCell`'s contents change behind `&self`). A `&str` field of an owned local reads where #110 shows its target standing (a struct with no lifetime holds only `&'static str`); through a reference parameter it outlives the call. `self.values[0]`, `[-1]` and `.len` select from it. A field or owned `Vec`/`String` local left raw carries the real reason (`` `self` is a `&mut` parameter of a caller frame ``, `` `self.cell` is a standard-library type ``, `` `w` is a local of a caller frame ``, `the debugger gives no memory address for it` on lldb-dap 18), in its note, in `session get` listings and in the index refusal, which names the whole selector (`Capture self.values.len`). The Low: `v` of `for v in args[2].split(',')` (a `&str` into `args`) was `<not shown: may point at freed memory>` past its last use and a call. #110 now also reads a shared reference made only from shared borrows of variables nothing changes: through reborrows, copies, calls whose borrowing operands are such borrows (`index`, `deref`, `split`, `into_iter`) and `Iterator::next` on an iterator only `next` borrows `&mut` (std's: a crate naming anything else `Iterator` gets none of this), each variable's scope enclosing the reference's, assigned once, never moved, partly assigned or borrowed `&mut` or raw, initialized at the stop, of a type with no interior mutability or references (`Vec<String>`, `String`, integers, arrays and tuples of them), or a shared reference argument to one. A `&str` or slice local #110 shows standing now reads its text (`v` is `"3"`); one into a `String` changed since (`push_str`) still shows only its address. Cost, measured on this Mac under load (five runs each, branch against main): the movavg stop capturing `self.values` paused 77-84 ms against main's 85-91 (raw); `session get self` on a struct of seven containers took 145-170 ms the first time (which reads the application units' DWARF once, shared with field changes, #105) against 149-159, and 101-104 against 94-97 after; `v` at line 29 79-97 against 83-94 ms: within the machine's noise. Real builds: the movavg case (`[3]`, `[3, 6]`, `self.values[-1]`, `.len`; `w.values` in `main`), a struct of every kind (through `&mut self`, a nested struct, a tuple, composite elements, a `Box<str>`, a `Box`'s contents, a generic's two instances, a RefCell left raw), a moved and a partly moved struct (`[stale]`), caller frames (`&self` reads, `&mut self` and `main`'s local stay raw with the reason), and `v` (read) beside a borrow of a changed `String` (gated), each asserting the raw reason where the debugger reads no memory (lldb-dap 18); each fails on main. Tests that captured a `Vec`'s or `String`'s internals as raw fields (`h.v.buf`, `w.s.vec.len`) read the view where memory reads.
  - Review (PR #163): sound `unsafe` code freed a field's buffer where the MIR still showed its struct initialized (`ptr::read(&w.values)` with the copy dropped before `mem::forget(w)`; `ptr::drop_in_place(&mut self.values)` before a `ptr::write`), and the field read the freed memory (`[3, 4356, 0]`). A function that takes a raw pointer to a local or through it (`&raw`, a cast to `*const`/`*mut`, a call returning one; followed back through the reference temps and calls it is made from, so `self` too; the MIR's own Box derefs excepted) now reads nothing that local owns: its container fields, an owned `Vec`/`String` local, a Box's contents (raw, with the reason), and the pointers inside it (address only). The same pointer unfreezes the #110 frozen owners (a write through `&s as *const String as *mut String`, undefined behaviour, moved what `v` pointed into). Calls that unwind to `continue` (no cleanup of their own) were not read, so a borrow into a shared reference argument (`fn by_ref(args: &Vec<String>)`) stayed unproven; their header's short type names (`&Vec<String>`) are trusted where the crate names no other `Vec`, `String`, `Box` or scalar. Cost: the frozen owners and the iterators only `next` borrows are found once per function (statements indexed once, owners' initialization memoized per stop): a 3,000-branch function with 30 `&str` locals took 4.0 s for its first `inspect` and 1.35 s for each later one; now 2.90-2.99 s against main's 2.85-2.97, and 0.09-0.11 s against 0.09-0.11.
  - Review round 2 (PR #163): the per-function raw-pointer rule missed unsafe code elsewhere (in a closure, an `unsafe fn` callee, through a pointer written to an out-parameter or a slot, after a move or a rebinding, in a caller of `look(w: &W)`), which still freed what was read; and it hid safe code's `println!("{:p}", v.as_ptr())`. It is replaced by the crate's rule: where the crate's own sources have unsafe code anywhere (an `unsafe` block, function, impl, trait or extern block, an `#[unsafe(...)]` attribute, or before the 2024 edition `#[no_mangle]`, `#[export_name]`, `#[link_section]`; read without comments and strings, `macro_rules!` bodies included, other files the build reads as plain text), no `Vec`/`String`/`&str`/`Box<str>` field, no `&str` or slice local and no owned `Vec`/`String` local is read (each raw, with the reason), and no borrow is frozen. The last fixes main's own reads, which read freed memory in the same cases (a local freed in a closure or a callee, or after a move); a crate with unsafe code pays for it. In a crate without unsafe code nothing can free such a buffer, so they read, and the raw-pointer rule is gone. The standard library is taken as sound. A short type name a `macro_rules!` body names (`decl!(Vec<T>);` declaring the crate's own `Vec`) is no longer trusted as the standard library's. Not covered, as on main: a reference parameter's contents and a Box's contents read in a crate with unsafe code.
  - Review round 3 (PR #163): the crate rule now covers every read Peeky makes from memory itself, which narrows main's reads for a crate with unsafe code: a reference parameter's contents (`&Vec<T>`, `&String`, `&[T]`, `&str`, a struct preview through `&S`: a caller may have freed the buffer of what it lends) stay raw with the reason, the Box gate gates every Box (contents freed through a second Box from `Box::from_raw`, the first forgotten), and the pointers inside a value (a Box or reference field, also behind a reference parameter) show only their address. LLDB's own scalars stay: a scalar local, a struct's scalar fields in the frame or behind a reference parameter (valid for the call, as Rust requires of a reference argument), a by-value struct's preview, an enum's variant. The `[unproven]` note names unsafe code among its reasons. Tests that read live Boxes in a crate with `#[unsafe(no_mangle)]` functions read them in its copy without the attribute, and check the crate with it gates them.
  - Review (PR #163, at 86347ca): the scan for unsafe code read a shebang line as code, so `#!/usr/bin/env -S echo "` opened a string up to a comment's `"` and hid `unsafe { drop(ptr::read(&w.values)) }` (the freed buffer read); frontmatter (`---` with `x = "`) did the same. The lexer now skips a shebang as rustc does (`#!` at the start that whitespace does not part from `[`), and a source it cannot be sure it reads as rustc does counts as having unsafe code, with its own reason ("Peeky cannot rule out unsafe code in src/main.rs (…)"): a literal or comment that does not end, brackets that do not pair up once literals are blanked (rustc's tokens are trees, so a literal was misread), a comment after the `#!`, frontmatter. `global_asm!`, which needs no `unsafe` but can define `free`, counts (and `asm!`, `naked_asm!`). The check for type names a crate gives its own items searched each macro call's arguments to the next `;`, per name: 8,000 calls in one statement took 13.7 s, and the first `session inspect` ran out of view time, its `&str` showing `<Rust enum view budget exhausted>`. It is now one pass over the code (8 ms; the same verdicts as before on every Rust source in the tests, and over 400,000 random token strings never a name the old search distrusted); the first inspection takes 0.11 s against 14.6. A spent view budget names the limit (`128 debugger reads`, or the time, with how much of it the debugger's reads took) and no longer says `enum`. Real builds (each fails on 86347ca): a shebang and a frontmatter crate (raw, with the reasons), `global_asm!` (raw), and the 8,000 macro calls (`v` reads `"a,b"`).
  - Review (PR #163, round 4, at 5603c83): four ways freed memory still read (`[3, 4356, 0]`) with no note. Leaving out files rustc's dependency file does not list for the binary (to let `unsafe` only in `tests/` pass) read that file inexactly: `$$` unescaped as make's (`#[path = "a$$b.rs"]` scanned a decoy `src/a$b.rs`), and a U+2028 in a file name split its line, dropping the modules after it; every declared file is scanned again, `tests/` too. `#![feature(custom_mir)]` (Peeky's MIR builds set `RUSTC_BOOTSTRAP`) frees through a `&Vec` with `Drop(*v, ...)` and no `unsafe`: any nightly feature (`#![feature(...)]`, also under `#![cfg_attr(...)]`) now counts, "the crate enables nightly features (in src/main.rs), …". `run!(#unsafe { ... })` expands to an `unsafe` block, which the scan skipped as it skipped any `#unsafe`; only `r#unsafe` is a name now. rustc's #25860 (safe code outliving a borrow, as `cve-rs` does) is a limit of trusting safe crates, documented in RUST_AGENT. Real builds (each fails on 5603c83): `$$` and U+2028 module names, unsafe code only in `tests/`, custom MIR, `#unsafe` (all raw, with the reason).
- **Optimized Rust builds never read a Box** (#111, found by the review of #103). At opt-level 1 or 2 the Box gate was skipped (no MIR), so a dropped Box read freed memory. Optimized builds now emit the same MIR, which says what each binding is; nothing is ever proven there, so a Box (and a pointer the MIR cannot vouch for) shows only its address, while references read.
- **A moved or dropped Rust Box shows only its pointer** (#101, found by the review of #100). After `consume(bp)` or `drop(bi)`, LLDB read the freed allocation as the Box's contents (`bp.x` showed `2` for a Box made with `x: 1`; a dropped `Box<i32>` showed a later Box's 555). Now a top-level value LLDB shows as a data pointer, or an array of them (its C-style type parsed as a declarator), is read through only when the build's MIR says it is a reference, raw pointer or fn pointer, or proves it initialized at the stop line in the innermost frame (as owned `Vec`s are, #53). A Box (in any spelling, also a wide `Box<str>`, an array of Boxes, or a generic `T` holding one) elsewhere shows only its address, `[unproven]`, with a note. Only the bindings of a name in scope at the stop count (the MIR's scopes, all of them on a line that may run macro code). The review took 24 rows to close the gaps: parameters, closure and async captures, unmangled functions, generics, arrays, LLDB's string summaries and declarator shapes, scope narrowing across macros, multi-line calls and other files, and quoted IR labels that had hidden live Boxes. A struct that owns a Box was #108.
- **Fifth agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-21-agent-fifth-round.md)): a Rust `Vec` of structs, a C `unsigned` sum, a Python mutable default. All three agents verified a general fix on their first `verify` (24, 24 and 30 calls).
  - Fixed: Python candidate probes (`probe --old/--new` runs a private copy of the target root with the edit, so imports, data files and child processes all see it); Rust `describe` on a crate whose unit holds megabytes of std generics; `queries diff` shows a selector-only row's first value, a type change, and a `(source)` row for runs of different source text; a field `--set` outside Java is refused before any build, with how to add a settable local; `describe --quiet`; `runs.set_hit` is NULL without `--set`; `session inspect` marks `(Return Value)` `[not selectable]`; docs for Python `--set`, `diff --param`, `verify --keep` and the return value.
- **`describe` gives the lines of locals declared in nested blocks** (#107, fifth evaluation). `i (int)` was listed among `average`'s names though only its `for` loop sees it. A local declared only inside nested lexical blocks now shows their lines (`i (int, lines 4-6)`; `spans` in `--json`), from the function's own line-table rows within each block's code. Rust's `let` blocks show where each binding is visible.
- **Caller frames, Python globals and object identity** (#104, from the fifth evaluation).
  - `session inspect --frame N` and `session get --frame N ...` read frame N as `inspect --json` lists `frames` (0 is the innermost, at most 10); the worker's `inspect` takes `frame` (an index into that same stack trace) and refuses one past it with `INVALID_REQUEST`, naming how many frames there are. Snapshots carry `frame` (and `innermost`, where the program stopped, for N > 0); rows carry `frame` and `location`; text says `frame 1: tags (app.py:16)`. Every frame check stays: frames outside the program's sources, provenance, and Rust's MIR proofs, which hold only in the innermost frame (a real-build test reads a caller's live `Vec` raw), while a caller's references are judged by the #110 gate as the stopped frame's are, anywhere on the call's line (`r` into a dropped Box read 777 through `--frame 1`). New for callers: a C/C++ local is withheld while its declaration's code is unfinished (declared on the calling line, or above it with code at or after the frame's return address), since `long result = twice(seed);` stores `result` only after the call returns (`--frame 1` showed `6712212928`); so is a local whose declaration line has no code of its own (an array of structs, a nested struct or a 2-D array initialized element by element; in the innermost frame that is #121), and one whose declaration statement, scanned in the source to its first `;` outside brackets with comments and literals blanked (`c_source`), still runs on the calling line (`struct trio t = { seed,` / `{ twice(3), twice(4) } };` showed `t.p.x = 20905`), or whose end the source does not give (also where the compiler may read the text differently: a directive, line splice, `<::` or unsafe macro between, a trigraph or `#line` in the file; digraphs are brackets, C has no raw strings, and lines break at `\r\n`, a lone `\r` or `\n` as in Clang's line table, where `\n\r` is two), or which is declared in another file than the frame's line (an initializer's `#include`d rows: `ps[1].x = 429835611285028900` at table.h:2); a caller's values are never settable, and the worker refuses to change them.
  - `session inspect --globals` lists debugpy's `Globals` after `Locals`, read-only, bounded like other scopes, and not at module level (the same dictionary); the worker's `inspect` lists them only on request (`globals`), since listing a value runs its repr in the program. A scope cut at its bound says so (`truncated`, `total`), and names a request asks for (`names`) are listed past it. `get` reads a name missing from the locals from the globals only when `symtable`, run on the frame's source, proves it is not a local, cell or free variable of the function (nor bound by its comprehensions or an enclosing function); only then are the globals listed. `def f(): print(x); x = 1` never shows the global `x`, and each refusal names what binds the name; a name the function reads from its globals stays a global whatever a comprehension or generator expression in it binds under that name. An unparsable source, an unidentified function, or a file changed since the program started (mtime or ctime), or whose listed locals it does not bind, reads nothing, with a `NOT_FOUND` message that says the name may be such a global. Each source is indexed once (function spans and bindings, no syntax tree), in a cache bounded by size.
  - Members debugpy files under `special variables` and the other groups are selected by name (`normalize.__defaults__`); a dict's or set's groups never are (their entries are named by their keys' reprs). Groups do not count toward the four-level depth bound.
  - Python values with a debugpy reference carry `identity` (an integer; `[id 7]` in text) in `inspect`, `get` and probe rows, and in `captures.identity`, beside `captures.scope` (`globals` for a module global, `unknown` for a row an early schema 6 stored; evidence schema 7, older stores upgrade in place, including an early schema 6 with a text `identity`). The number alone identifies the object, which keeps it if its `__class__` is reassigned. The number is debugpy's per-object number, not `id()` itself: pydevd numbers objects by `id(value)` and keeps them alive while stopped. At one stop equal means the same object; across stops different means different objects and equal may also be a reused address. Scalars and debugpy's grouping and paging nodes have none.
- **The stopped C/C++ frame withholds what is being built, or skipped** (#121, found by the review of #104).
  - A multi-line initializer read leftovers at a stop inside it: `struct outer o = {` over lines 17-22 at line 20 (`o.in.y = 20905`), an array of structs, a 2-D array, and `struct trio t = { seed, {` whose first line has code. Now, in the innermost frame, a local declared above the stop is withheld while the stop's line is within its declaration (`c_source`'s `declared_through`, the caller-frame scan made header-aware: a variable a `for`, `if`, `while`, `switch` or `catch` header declares has run with its part of the header, so loop bodies still read their counters), or when its end is unknown, also for a declaration in another file (an `#include`d initializer). `declared_below` places a declaration line without code by its statement's elements, so `ps` and `grid` below the stop are withheld too.
  - A jump past an initialization: `case 1` skipped `struct pair p = { 7, 8 };`, and `p` read the stack. A local is withheld below a label a jump can reach between its declaration and the stop: a `case` or `default` of the switch whose body holds it, or a label a `goto` before it names (or any, with a computed `goto` or a macro that may `goto`); a macro that may expand to a label, or a block that closes first, is unknown. This holds in caller frames too.
  - `this` while its object is built: the stopped `Widget::Widget` showed `this.a = 20908`. `this` is withheld in a constructor (the debug information's class and member, or the frame's name `C::C(`), in any frame a constructor called, and in a lambda defined inside a local's declaration or a constructor that may still be running (by the frames listed below it). A bare member name in such a frame says why `this` has no value.
  - Lexical scopes now pick which local of a name the stop reads, so a sibling block's local never decides.
  - Review round 1 (all with real-build tests): a local declared on the stop line is withheld part-way through it while the line still has code to run (`step --out` back into `long r = twice(3) + twice(4);` showed `r = 20911 [settable]`), except a header's variable, live mid-line in a one-line loop; a local declared in another file than the stop is withheld when that file may `#include` code after it in its block (`case 1:` in `cases.h` skipped `p`); a frame running a line outside its function's own (a default member initializer: `Cfg cfg{};` showed `cfg.b = 20907` at the member's line, also as frame 1) withholds its class, struct and array locals; a `goto` back into a block from after it reaches its labels (C11 6.2.4). Over-hiding fixed: C's trigraphs are translated (one `"huh??!"` hid every local above the stop) and C++17 has none; a macro's `;` inside its brackets (a statement expression) is harmless; a lambda's defining frame and constructors below a frame are looked for in up to 64 frames, not the ten listed, and past that `this` is withheld.
  - Review round 2: a header's variable is live mid-line only when a `for`, `if` or `switch` sets it with no call (`for (long i = twice(1); ...)` showed garbage `[settable]` after `step --out`, and a `while`'s `v` the last pass's value); a local an `#include`d file declares counts as declared at that `#include` (`--at 16` above `#include "decls.h"` showed `later`'s leftovers); a stop on another file's line, or outside the function's own lines, runs every rule from the own line that brings it in, else from the last own line before it (`after = 20911 [settable]` at `cfg.hpp:4`), and withholds all but parameters when it cannot be placed; an included file with no label, jump or unbalanced brace is no longer a jump hazard; a `goto` counts only within its function's DWARF lines, not a namespace's braces.
  - Review round 3: a caller frame on its own file's line outside its function's own lines is placed on the line running it as the stopped frame is (`run()` as frame 1 on `Cfg::b`'s initializer showed `x = 20908`); a class defined inside the function (its line from the debug information, its extent from the braces; a lambda's closure is not one) is outside the function's own lines (`Local::b`'s initializer showed `loc.b = 4366860288`), and where its extent cannot be found, all but parameters are withheld.
  - Review round 4: a lambda whose closure a class's scope holds (a default member initializer's: `long b = [this] { ... }();` in `Agg`, which has no constructor) withholds its `this` (`this.this.b` was a leftover, stopped and as frame 1); an unnamed struct's constructor is one (an artificial member with no return type, or `$_N::$_N(` and `(anonymous struct)::(anonymous struct)(` frame names): `nested(long)::$_0::$_0()` showed `this.t = 2`.
- **`--set` on scalar fields and elements in Rust, C and C++** (#105, fifth evaluation, Rust `spans`). At `last.end = s.end;` nothing could be set (`--set s.end=10`: "fields and array elements can be changed only in Java").
  - `--set`/`session set` now change an integer field or fixed-size array element of a local the stopped frame holds itself (`s.end`, `s.in.lo`, `s.arr[2]`, `grid[1][0]`, `t.__0`), only where the write provably reaches the program, and read it back.
  - The path is decided from the build's debug information (`fields.py`: each function's locals with their type and storage, and a graph of the types they hold by value, not through pointers), never from the debugger's type names, which hide a typedef'd pointer and a bitfield's width. Only fields of plain records and elements of arrays with a known size; refused: a pointer (`ps.end`, `this.count`, a `Box`) or reference anywhere on the path, a union, a Rust enum (`variant_part`), a packed record (a misaligned field, or a Rust `DW_AT_alignment` below a field's), a standard-library type (`std`, `core`, `alloc`), an incomplete type, a base class or anonymous member, and a target that is const, volatile, a bitfield, a character, a boolean, an enum, a float or not 16 to 64 bits. The value must fit (`f.tiny=40000`: outside short's range); the debugger's view of the target must agree with the declared type and show a number.
  - C/C++ roots: one automatic or parameter in the frame's memory (`DW_OP_fbreg`/`DW_OP_breg`; not a static, register or location list; several locals of one name must share a type), not withheld by #120/#121 (declared below, a running initializer, a jump past it, a constructor's `this`). Rust roots: kept in memory by the IR's proof, and initialized at the stop by the MIR's (`drop(gone)`, `let later: Span;` before its assignment, and a `.dbg.spill` copy of a by-value parameter are refused). C/C++ types are read when the session starts; Rust's on the first field change, from the application units.
  - The write is lldb-dap's `setVariable` on the parent's reference; the frame, its scopes and the path are then walked again and the field re-read. Only the value asked counts: the old value is `MUTATION_REJECTED` (nothing changed), anything else, or no read-back, closes the session. Caller frames are refused (a call in progress may hold a copy: `p.x + f()` loads `p.x` first), and so are optimized builds.
  - Real builds (C, C++, Rust) check each change in the program's own output after `continue`, and each refusal by its reason; unit tests cover the type walk and the worker's read-back. Fields carry no `[settable]` mark; Python still refuses fields before the program runs.
  - Review (PR #133): a statement under way kept the old value while `set` reported the change (`let t = s.a + f();`, `step --out` from `f`, `set s.a=50`: `tempout 1001 50`; also root integers, on main). C, C++ and Rust, locals and fields alike, now refuse a change part-way through a statement, keeping the session; `inspect` drops `[settable]` there. Rust: the build's MIR shows no temporary that may hold a value read from the local (a copy, a read through a pointer, what a call given a pointer to it returns) used from where the stop can be: before the line's first code in its lexical block at a breakpoint or a step that ends at the line's lowest address, else at the start of a MIR block (back from a call). C/C++: the stop at its line's lowest address, on one of its function's own lines, and the line starting a statement (`Statements.begins`); unknown refuses. The docs say what a change makes true: the storage holds the new value and later reads see it; earlier copies keep the old value, and the stop's line may overwrite it.
  - Also: shadowed roots are selected as LLDB names them (`s @ main.rs:81.a`, `p @ s1.c:38.x`), each judged by its own declaration line; an element of an array of more than 512 is fetched alone (`a[257]` exhausted the budget); `core::`/`alloc::` are C++ program namespaces (only `std`, `__gnu_cxx`, `__gnu_debug`, `__cxxabiv1` are the library's there); a VLA is refused as one; `session set --help` names every language.
  - Review round 2 (PR #133): stops that looked like a statement's start while it was under way still took a change the program missed, so one conservative rule per language replaces the per-shape checks: a change is allowed only where Peeky proves the stop is at a statement's first instruction, and refused anywhere else. C/C++: the pc must be the lowest address of every row the function has on the lines of the statements starting at the stop line (`Statements.statement_span`; clang emitted `p.x + g();` before line 10's `t =` store, and the set reported `1 -> 50` while the program printed `A 1001`); on a function's first line only the first row after the prologue (a one-line function or lambda after `step --out` printed `E 1002`/`L 1002`); `begins` requires every bracket up to the function's body to be a block of statements (`)` + `{` only after `if`/`for`/`while`/`switch`/`catch` or lambda parameters, `]` + `{` only after a lambda introducer, never inside `({ … })`: `({ if (c) { q = g(); } })`, `new long[1]{`, `decltype(W{}){`, `(long)(struct W){`) and no directive between the line and the previous token (`#if 0` / `;` / `#endif`). Rust: a step stop must be at its line's lowest address (a step back onto `let arr = [s.a,` after `k,` and `s.b];` stops at the array's construction, after `s.a` was read: `arr [1, 7, 2] 50`; also call arguments and tuples over lines), any other stop reason is refused, and the MIR is judged from the line's first code in the stop's block.
  - Also: shadowed root integers are changed through the same names (`x @ main.rs:18=500`, `n@sh1.c:15=99`, which said "only … long; has type 'long'"); an element past 512 is fetched alone at any step of a path (`es[699].a`, `g2[599][2]`, by `get` too); `get` refuses a shadowed name two locals of one line share; a multi-statement macro (`TWICE(p);`) no longer disables the rest of its block, nor `[[likely]]` the line after its block, nor an in-class method its closing `}`; `format_args!`'s constructors keep the pointers they are given (no copy); the refusal names the loop header's later pass among its causes. Still refused, conservatively: a loop header's later passes, a statement whose first line holds no code of its own before a later line's (`long u =` / `p.x + g();`), K&R definitions, and a Rust step that lands mid-line.
  - Review round 3 (PR #133), each the conservative fix: a statement making a call the compiler inlined (`always_inline`, `flatten`) is refused (its inlined code carries the callee's lines and ran before the statement's lowest row: `t = inl(&p) + p.y;` printed `A 1003` after `set p.x=50`; `t = getx(&p) + g();`, `B 1050`; root integers lost `[settable]` there too); any `({` around a line refuses, and a brace after something like parameters is a function's body only at file scope or in a class's or namespace's body (`REPEAT(k, 1) {` inside `({` read as one: `F 1001`); a function's first line is judged by the statements after its body's `{` and the lowest of their rows past the entry (a step onto `long t =`'s store under `p.x + g();` counted as the first row after the prologue: `E 1001`); K&R definitions are refused as such. Rust: `format_args!`'s exception applies only where the crate names nothing `core` or `std` (its own `mod core { pub mod fmt { ... } }` printed like the real one: `1001`), and a breakpoint's stop needs its column (the line's first MIR place may emit no code). Documented, not changed: a `step` onto `} else if (...) {` on the else path is refused (the line's lowest address is the `if` block's closing jump, where a breakpoint on the line never hits on that path either), and Rust's pointer tracking refuses an unrelated `u` mid-statement.
  - Review round 4 (PR #133): the inlined-call refusal names what inlines at opt-level 0 (`always_inline`, `flatten`, both read from the debug information); a function-try-block (`long tryfun(P p) try { … } catch (…) { … }`) and its handlers are function bodies, where `t = p.x + g();` was refused as continuing a statement; documented over-refusals: an inlined function's own line, and a statement after a body's `{` on the line under its signature.
  - With #130 (#106) merged, a struct or tuple local previewed from memory (`Span { start: 1, end: 2 } [memory]`) refused every field change as "inside a value Peeky decodes itself" (6 Rust set tests failed). A preview read with a `composite` struct layout (`RustSession.view_writable`) is the local's own storage: its worker handle keeps where the debugger lists the local, the change walks and writes that under every #105 rule (`validate_member`'s DWARF walk, the MIR's, the statement start), and the preview is read again after the write and must show the value the debugger read back, else the session closes. Containers' contents (`Vec`, `String`, `Box`) and enum variants stay refused; no memory read is cached, and a change revokes every handle, so the next `get` or `inspect` reads again.
  - With #132 merged, a Rust root #132 withholds (`let later: Span;` before its assignment, an async body's local not kept across an `.await`) is refused before these checks run: `inspect` shows it with no value, and the refusal gives #132's reason. The check for a statement under way searched forward from every place of the line for each temporary that may copy the local, so one `inspect` of a 1,500-line function took 1.8 s, or 7.3 s with 44 integer locals (each such row is checked for `[settable]`). The copies are now found by following only what mentions each one (`copies_of`), and which temporaries are used ahead comes from one backward dataflow per body (`live_ahead`). Both give the same answers as before: for every local of the unit-test bodies, and for all 3,261 `copied_in_progress` verdicts of three crates. The same inspects now take 0.08 s and 0.11 s.
- **C/C++ pointers: `p[0]` is the pointee** (fifth evaluation's dry run). `values[0]` on `const int *values` said only "children: *values", and the pointee was `[not selectable]`. It now reads `*values`, `session get values` lists it as `values[0]`, and `values[1]` says a pointer carries no length, with where to index the array instead.
- **C/C++ pointers to arrays, containers and structs index as C does** (#102). The debugger lists such a pointer's pointee elements as its own children, so `parr[1]` on `int (*parr)[3]` read `(*parr)[1]` where C means the next row, and `parr[0][0]` failed. Now `parr[0]` is the pointee (`int[3]`; a vector's summary such as `size=3`), `parr[0][1]` its element, its children list as `parr[0][N]`, and `parr[1]`/`parr[-1]` are refused with the selector that reads the element.
- **C++ maps read by key; `[#N]` is an entry's position** (#119, found while verifying #117). LLDB lists a map's or set's entries as `[0]`, `[1]`, ... in iteration order, so for `std::map<int, int> m{{2, 20}, {1, 10}, {5, 50}}` `m[1].second` read `20`, where C++'s `m[1]` is `10` (libc++ and libstdc++; also unordered maps, sets and multimaps).
  - `m[K]` on `std::map`/`std::unordered_map` is the value for key K, as in C++: the one entry whose `first` the debugger shows as exactly K, for integer keys in the key type's range and `std::string` keys of plain printable ASCII (`m["al"]`). A missing key is `not found` with the keys there are; other key types, and a key of the other kind, are refused.
  - `m[#N]` is the Nth entry in iteration order (new selector step). Sets, multisets and multimaps, which have no `[]` in C++, refuse `s[N]` and name `s[#N]`.
  - `session get m` lists `m[1]: int = 10`, ... when the keys compare, else `m[#0]`, ..., with a note; never `m[N]` by position. Pointers list `p[0][K]`, and `p[1]` names `p[0][1]` as key 1's value.
  - Containers are told by LLDB's type name (`std::map`, `std::__1::map`, `std::__debug::set`, through `const`/`&`) or, for a typedef, by the class the build's debug information gives it (`typedef_kinds` now records it; `aliased_type`, and `pointee_aliased_type` behind a pointer). An unresolved typedef listing pairs with a const key refuses `[N]` (it could be a key or a position).
  - A `std::map` with integer keys in the standard order and more than 20 entries is bisected (read entry by entry, a 300-entry map needs more than one inspection's 512 nodes); other maps are read entry by entry and refused up front when too big for one inspection. Vectors, deques and arrays are unchanged. Rust's `HashMap`/`BTreeMap` are unaffected: without Rust's LLDB formatters they show raw fields. Tests: `tests/unit/test_associative.py` and four real builds in `test_native_worker.py` (three fail on `96f57e4`; the sequences guard passes on both), on macOS (libc++) and Linux (libstdc++, Docker).
  - Review (PR #127): on Linux a `std::string` key matched a longer string with a NUL (`sso["a"]` read the `"a\0b"` entry's 3; libstdc++'s summary ends at the NUL), now checked against `_M_string_length` (libc++'s summary is whole; the worker says which); a map with its own comparator or hash said `not found ... would insert` where C++ reads an equivalent key, now only under `std::less`/`std::greater`/`std::hash`+`std::equal_to`; a set behind a class's member typedef (`Holder<T>::S`) indexed by position, now `typedef_kinds` keeps the class every instance names (or `?`, refused); keys a selector cannot write (`ids[1234567]`) are listed by position, labels never repeat, and `const char *` keys are named so. Four real builds fail on `0e8369c` (the NUL one on Linux); checked with lldb-dap 18 and 20.
- **libstdc++ strings with a NUL byte** (#128, found while fixing #119). On Linux, LLDB's summary of a libstdc++ `std::string` ends at the first NUL (`"a\0b"` showed as `"a"`, with no note) and, for a value LLDB names `std::string`, fails outright (`error: summary string parsing error`, shown as the value). The worker now checks each libstdc++ string against its `_M_string_length`: with lldb-dap 20 it reads the bytes and shows them as LLDB shows a libc++ string (`"a\0b"  [memory]`, also `std::wstring`, `u16string`, `u32string`, by value, reference or pointer); with lldb-dap 18 it keeps the text with a note (`"v" (shown up to a NUL at byte 1 of 3)`), or no value where the summary failed. Covers locals, members, vector elements, map values and keys (a key at the depth limit now matches on Linux too), probe rows and the evidence store; macOS is unchanged. Real builds in `tests/cpp/test_cpp_strings.py` (all four fail on `daa5ceb` with lldb-dap 18 and 20); the Docker lanes gain `cpp-llvm20` with `PEEKY_EXPECT_READ_MEMORY`.
  - Review of PR #131: a string under a typedef's name (`typedef std::basic_string<char> Bytes`, `std::__cxx11::basic_string<char>` by an alias, `const Bytes &`, `Bytes *`, `bp[0]`, a struct member) was still cut at its NUL with no note, and `using Name = std::string` showed (and the evidence store saved) `error: summary string parsing error` as its value. Strings are now known through typedefs (the build's debug information) or, under any name, by their libstdc++ members, and LLDB's failed-summary text is never a value for any C/C++ type. A NUL before a digit (`"n\064"`, read by C as `"n4"`) and an invalid byte before a hex digit (`"\xffa"`) are spelled `"n\00064"`, `"\377a"`, LLDB's libc++ text on macOS included; values stay within 2000 characters, cut between characters with `...`; a string in its own buffer is read in one request. Four more real builds in `tests/cpp/test_cpp_strings.py`: all fail on `b6f37d6` with lldb-dap 18, three with 20 (whose memory reads were already bounded), two on macOS.
- **Sixth evaluation, C++ findings 15-18** (branch `fix/eval6-cpp`; [NATIVE_INTERNALS.md](NATIVE_INTERNALS.md)). (15) `session get name` listed a `std::string`'s library members (`[not selectable]: std::basic_string<char>::(anonymous struct) = … @ 0x…` on libc++; `_M_dataplus`, whose `_M_p` LLDB cuts at a NUL, on libstdc++): a standard string shown as its text now has no handle, so nothing is listed or offered under it, and selecting into one says to compare its text; a pointer's `p[0]` is still the string. (16) The `function` column showed `trim_left(std::__1::basic_string<char, std::__1::char_traits<char>, std::__1::allocator<char>> const&)`: tables show `trim_left` (overloads in one table keep their parameters), session text `trim_left(std::string const&)`, and JSON, the store and SQL the whole name, with `short_function()` in SQL. (17) `describe` gave `name (lines 22-35)` for an `if` block's local: C++ puts a block's exception cleanup on the function's closing line, so a block's lines now split where code outside it runs between (`name (lines 22-25, 35)`), never narrower than where the local is visible. (18) COOKBOOK 4.7: a C++ reference's value starts with its address; take the text after it to join with plain strings. Real builds in `tests/cpp/test_cpp_eval6.py`, all three failing on `a4e3c77`.
  - Review round 1 (PR #142): `ptr[00]` on a pointer to a string was refused as past the pointee (the new path compared with `[0]`); a probe's table that shows short names now carries the note on matching them in SQL, whose example is `short_function`'s value (`'twice'`, not `'twice(int)'`), and the docs say `short_function` merges overloads; a block's later span starts right after the last line of code outside it (`i (lines 66, 70-71)`: `let s = twice!(i);` had been left out).
- **C/C++ struct and array previews** (#136, sixth evaluation's `histo`). A struct, class or fixed-size array showed only `bin[4] @ 0x16bb9f278`, so the agent listed eight selectors by hand. Probes, `inspect` and `session get` now show `[{lo = 0, hi = 2, count = 0}, …]` and `{lo = 6, hi = 7, count = 0}`, written from the debugger's children in LLDB's C-like form (`native_previews`): 8 items per level, two levels, 40 characters per member, 240 in all, within a preview budget of their own (256 `variables` requests, 3 s, per inspection or expansion). Unions (named by the build's debug information, `union_names`, or anonymous) are never previewed (`<union>` inside); pointers and references inside show addresses only; members with no value show `<no value>`; base classes show by their class's name, the vptr is left out; `std::` values keep their own views; withheld locals have no preview. Probes preview only the roots they capture, and a selector's field or element alone (the worker's `preview` operation), so capturing scalars costs nothing: on a fixture with 40 nested struct locals and a 1000-element struct array (macOS, medians of repeated runs), a 100-hit probe of two ints took 1.96 s (main 1.99 s, the same 20 ms longest pause); `session inspect` took about 25 ms more than main (129 `variables` requests, all 41 aggregates previewed); a probe capturing two of the structs and a field paused about 10 to 15 ms longer per hit. Real builds in `tests/native/test_native_previews.py` (C, with the evaluation's `histo.c`) and `tests/cpp/test_cpp_previews.py`, all failing on `3c8a378`.
  - Review round 1 (PR #146): previews guessed where main showed only `T @ 0x…`: a variable-length array read another's length (clang `a = [10, 11, 12, 0, -1882931200, 1]`) or a wrong one (GCC); `Hold<int[1]> hs[5]` one element (the name's first `[1]`); `npp[0]` on a pointer to a pointer previewed the struct where the pointer belongs (stored so in the evidence); `op[0]` on an incomplete type `{}`; optimized builds values not stored yet (GCC `t = {x = 64, y = 0, z = 0}`); char-typed bitfields LLDB's unsigned characters (`'\x05'` for -3), wide arrays text ended at the first NUL. Previews now take every type from the build's debug information (`fields.type_graph`, now with bases, bit widths and one level of pointees; `native_previews.Typed`), matching the debugger's children to it by name, and make none when unsure: VLAs, incomplete types, values inside `std::` containers, programs using `setjmp`, optimized builds. Char bitfields show their numbers (member rows too), wide arrays element by element, LLDB's padding bitfields are left out, typedef'd arrays read `[1, 2, 3]`. `union_names` is gone (unions come from the same types). Real builds with clang and GCC (VLAs, optimized -O1/-O2) all fail on `84124ba`.
  - Review round 2: a wide character bitfield (`char16_t w : 9` holding u'a') previewed `u'̍'` (LLDB reads it at other bits): it has no value, in previews (`<bitfield>`) and its own row (`not shown: …`); `struct B2 : A2 { int A2; }` previewed the base as the `int` member: a name a base and a member share means no preview; a noted local's previewed fields and children carried no note: #148's notes (merged as #150; ``read through `late`: …``) now carry it with the preview's text, in captures, `session get` children and the evidence. Real builds fail on `84c99fe`.
- **A line that stops several times per pass says so** (#157, seventh evaluation's Rust `movavg`; branch `feat/multi-stop-lines`). `out.push(format!("{:.1}", mean));` stopped 3 times per loop pass, so `--hits all` gave 12 hits for 4 values with no word, and `--hits`/`--set-hit` counted stops where an agent counted passes. LLDB places a line breakpoint at the first code of each lexical block on the line, so a line has several locations in one function wherever a block of its own starts there: a macro's (`format!`, `println!`, a C `do { int t_ ...; } while (0)`), a one-line loop's body, a C++ range `for` header (its range once, its element each pass); Rust's `for` header has two, both before the loop. The worker now asks lldb-dap where it placed the session's breakpoints (`breakpoint list`, read-only, through the command escape) after every `setBreakpoints`, groups the locations by the function holding them (each inlined copy apart: two copies are two calls), and the launch warns before the first stop (`Line 29 stops up to 3 times per pass in movavg::main (3 breakpoint locations): --hits and --set-hit count stops, not passes; ...`). Each stop's pc names its location: rows carry `stop` (1 to N, address order) and `stops`, the table a `stop` column (`2/3`), session state lines `stop 2 of 3`, and the evidence a `captures.stop` column (schema 14, after #151's 10 to 13; `queries run` and `queries wide` show it). Locations placed before the program loaded (unslid, lldb-dap 18) are asked again once at the first stop that names none. No `--first-stop`: breakpoints cannot tell where a pass starts (a loop header's first location runs once, a branch skips one, a call returning between two looks the same); COOKBOOK 2.2 shows `WHERE stop = 1` and when it is right. Java (java-debug binds a line's first location only) and Python (one stop per line per pass) cannot stop more than once per pass in one function, so they get neither; a C or C++ `for`/`while` header with one location stops once, before the loop, and Rust's `for` twice, which COOKBOOK now says. Real builds (Rust `movavg`'s `format!` and `for` lines, a C macro and one-line `for`, a C++ range `for`) fail on `5cddb2c`; lines with one location (two statements, a C `for` header, a lambda's line in main) keep no warning and no `stop`.
  - Review round 1 (PR #160): a function inlined into two callers (`format!` in an `#[inline(always)]` function) stopped 3 times per call with no warning, since each location was taken for a copy of its own: LLDB's text gives each copy's own offset (`uses_inl + 72 [inlined] fmt_inl + 12`), so locations are grouped by copy, and copies that read alike warn once. Two instances of a generic warned alike (`r1::gener` twice): warnings name them as tables do (#151's `r1::gener<i32>`), else by the whole name. Locations listed before the program loads (`address = m[0x…], unresolved`) were left out, so the first stop never asked again: they are read. Warnings say `--set-at`'s @HIT counts stops too. Tests fail on `d6470de`.
  - Review round 2: a `--set-at 9@2` on a `format!` line said only `9@2`, though its hit was the first pass's second stop: changes now record and show it (`--set-at line 9@2, stop 2 of 3`, JSON `stop`/`stops`, `interventions.stop` in schema 14, `queries wide`'s `sets`, `set_effect`'s `stop`), and #151's "stopped in N functions" warning names `--set-at`'s @HIT. Locations listed before the program loaded keep the module's addresses, which a slid program's pc may match by chance: they are marked, and no stop is labelled from them (the first stop asks again). Tests fail on `2710e17`.
- **A loop header that stops only before its loop says so** (#161, found while fixing #157; branch `feat/loop-header-note`, on #160). A breakpoint binds a line's first code, and a header's first code often runs once, before the loop: `i = 0` of a C, C++ or Java `for`, a jump into the loop (C/C++ `while`, Rust's `while`), a Java for-each's array copy, Rust's `for` at both its locations. `--at` there with `--hits all` gave one row where an agent expected one per pass. Probes and `session start` now say so before the first stop (`Line 4 is a loop header: it stops once each time its loop starts, before the first pass, never at the passes; put --at (or --set-at) on the first line of the loop's body for one stop per pass`), for `--at` and `--set-at` lines alike, and for Rust's `for` it replaces #157's per-pass warning. Decided from the build's code, never the source's shape (`loop_headers`): LLDB's `disassemble` and `source info` (read-only, through the command escape, before the program runs) or the class file's bytecode give the function's control flow; its natural loops come from dominators (an indirect jump or irreducible flow gives no answer); the line's loops are those whose header holds its code; the note needs every breakpoint location of the line (java-debug's: the line's first bytecode in the first method holding it) to be outside them and to lead into one. Headers whose first code is the condition the loop returns to stop at each pass and get none: Java `while`, `for (; j < n; j++)`, `do`'s `while`, C++'s range `for`, Rust's `while let`. GCC gives `for (;;)` and `do {` no code, so LLDB moves them into the body (no note). `describe` does not mark them (C, C++ and Rust need the debugger's locations). Real builds in C (clang and GCC), C++, Rust and Java fail on `d6470de`.
  - Review round 1 (PR #166): the note was false on lines that run at each pass of a loop around them, as each location had only to be outside the line's loop and able to reach it: a loop entered under a condition (`if (r == 3) for (...) {`: 6 stops for 5 passes, said "twice each time its loop starts"), a line holding a whole loop (`FILL(...)`, a `do { for ... } while (0)` macro, `for (...) total += i;`), a statement and a loop on one line (Rust, Java too), where it also replaced #157's correct warning. Now every path from each location must pass through the loop's header before coming back to it or returning, the loop must hold other lines' code, and the line's code in it must be the loop's own (condition, increment, a for-each's binding), within the header's columns. A loop counts as the line's only where its header holds the line's code, so a `goto` into a loop (and `for (;;)`, `do {`, Rust's `loop {`) gets none. The debugger is asked at launch only: the file's line table once (`source info -l 1`), each function's disassembly once, lines naming no loop and lines in more than 8 places skipped; re-arming after a `--set-at` no longer analyses while the program waits (its pause on a 7,800-instruction function from 881 to 594 ms, on 240 inlined copies from 639 to 73 ms). Tests fail on `0bbb9a3`.
  - Review round 2: a location that runs only under a condition on the line (`if (z) { int w = 5; ... } for (...) {`, the block's own location) made "stops 3 times" stop twice. Each location stops at most once per start; the count is exact only where every location dominates the loop's header, else the note says `up to 3 times` (`at most once`). Tests fail on `1f8d058`.
- **C++ standard containers preview their elements** (#181, eighth evaluation's C++ `router`; branch `feat/std-container-previews`). At `router.cpp:31`, `have` (`std::vector<std::string>`) showed only `size=3`, and the bug's evidence (`have[2] = ""`) took a session, which `--hits all` could not replace (some hits hold two elements). Probes, `inspect` and `session get` now show `size=3 ["users", "42", ""]`: a `std::vector`, `std::array`, `std::deque` or `std::list` as `size=N [e0, …]`, a `std::map` or `std::unordered_map` as `size=N {k: v, …}` in iteration order, a set as `size=N {e, …}`, on libc++ and libstdc++ (lldb-dap 18, 20 and Xcode's), with #136's bounds and forms.
  - Typed by the debug information: `fields.type_graph` records a class template's arguments (`_Tp`, `_Key`) and walks them for these containers, whose elements sit behind pointers; each element shows as that type says (a struct as its preview, a string as #128 checks it), and an element row (`vp[0]`, `mp[1]`, `named["a"]`) is now typed, so previewed, too. The elements are the debugger's children, which its formatter reads from the container's own storage, `size()` of them; any other listing keeps the summary. A `std::array`, which LLDB lists by its C array, shows that array once the debug information says it holds `_Size` of `_Tp`.
  - Withheld as before: not yet constructed (#121, #140), optimized builds (#136). A moved-from container is empty in both libraries and shows `size=0 []`. New: a container a standard-library call may be changing keeps its summary, and its elements no preview of their own (`Noisy @ 0x…`). During `o.items.clear()`, a stop in the second element's destructor showed `main`'s `o.items[1]` (destroyed already, its string freed) as `{name = "\U00000003\0\0…"}` once elements were typed; now a program running more than one thread (another may be inside a `push_back` on a shared container) and a frame below a library function (a frame named in `std::`/`__gnu_cxx::`) show no container's elements, and a container reached through a pointer or reference, or a static local, shows them only where no frame of the stack (read to 64) is a library function's.
  - `session get m` lists a map's values with their previews (`named["a"]: Pt = {x = 1, y = 2}`); #119's entry count reads `size=N` before a preview. Tests in `tests/cpp/test_cpp_containers.py` fail on `1ea0e6d`; `test_cpp_previews.py` and `test_native_worker.py` expect the previews. Gaps: a container four levels below a local is past the inspection's depth; `std::forward_list`, `std::multimap`, adaptors and `std::pair`/`std::tuple` keep the debugger's views; a destroyed element's own row (`o.items[1].name`) still shows LLDB's reading, as on main.
  - Review round 1 (PR #186): (H) clang types `items` of `auto &[items, dropped] = *owner` as the vector itself at `DW_OP_fbreg -24, DW_OP_deref`, so it was taken for the frame's own and previewed `size=2 [{value = 1, …}, {value = -99, …}]` in the second destructor `clear()` ran: a local whose location is not one frame offset (`fields.referred_locals`) now counts as reached through a pointer. (M-H) `slot->~E(); ::new (slot) E(3);` in `main`, stopped in `E::~E()`, previewed `main`'s `v` as `size=2 [{x = -7}, {x = 2}]` with no library frame on the stack: a constructor, destructor or assignment operator below the container's frame withholds it too (anywhere on the stack for one reached through a pointer or reference). (Risk) two `threads` replies that both said one thread proved nothing: the threads are now those both replies, LLDB's `thread list` and every `thread` started event name together, and any source that does not answer withholds. Also: an element through a reference or pointer to a container (`rv[0]`) was not taken for one. Real-build tests for the first two fail on `302ceb4`; the thread fix's unit test does (every lldb-dap tested, Xcode's, 18 and 20, answers the second `threads` request in full, so no real build there shows it), and a real build holds a worker thread inside `emplace_back`'s relocation.

By-value bindings and by-value parameters the ABI passes through a hidden pointer (the call's own copy: `o.in.hi` of a 40-byte struct parameter on arm64) change as before. Tests fail on `5b0a2ed`.
- **Rows read through a container a library call is changing are withheld** (#187, found while building #181; branch `fix/container-element-rows`, on #186). A stop in an element's destructor during `o.items.clear()` showed `main`'s `o.items[1].name` as the freed string of an element already destroyed (`"\U00000003\0\0…"`), with no note; #181 had stopped previewing such a container, but its element rows and their members still read. Every row read through a standard container's element is now `not shown` with why, where a standard-library function, or a constructor, destructor or assignment operator (#186's review), runs between the stop and the container's frame, or anywhere on the stack where the container is reached through a pointer or reference (`this.owner[0].items[1].name`, `items[1].name` through a reference parameter); such rows have no handle, so selectors below them and a map's key lookup refuse with the reason. #181's type walk missed elements reached through a reference or pointer to a container (`rv[0]`), fixed on #186; the stack is read to 1,024 frames (was 64) for both. Which container the call changes is not told, so the frame's other containers' elements are withheld too (`o.tags[1]`). Tests fail on `f3dea4b`.
  - Review round 1 (PR #200): rows were withheld for library calls only, never for another thread (`shared[0].x` read the moved-from `-1` while a worker thread relocated the vector): they now need exactly one thread, proven, as previews do; a child whose name or type passes 2,000 characters was shown raw before the check ran (`value="FREED"`), and is now withheld as any row; and a partial `stackTrace` page (64 frames with `totalFrames: 2000`, or the ten listed short of theirs) was taken for the whole stack: replies now keep whether they are whole, and a partial one withholds. Tests fail on `33894b5`.
- **Rust tuple and struct elements** (#90, second part).
  - `Vec<T>`, `&[T]` and `&Vec<T>` are read when `T` is a tuple, or a struct the crate defines, of qualified scalars: `[('a', 3), ('b', 1)]`, `[Interval { start: 1, end: 10, open: false }]`, `[Point(1, 2)]`. The build's debug information must show plain, aligned fields within the element's size.
  - The compiler's own type picks the layout (the MIR's for owned locals, the IR's for parameters). LLDB's names only confirm it, since they collide: LLDB names `Vec<Pair<i8>>` `Vec<Pair<char>>`, the DWARF name of `Vec<Pair<char>>`.
  - Fields are selectable (`runs[0].__0`, `spans[1].start`), with Rust types; `runs[0].0` gets a hint to write `__0`.
  - Anything else (an enum, a `String` field, a standard-library struct such as `Range`, which prints as `1..5`) keeps LLDB's summary with a note that says which elements are read. Two structs that share a path (a function's own `Token` and `mod parse`'s) make a container of either opaque.
- **Plain Cargo crates** (#89, found by the fourth evaluation's dry run).
  - A `cargo new` binary crate works as is: edition 2021 or 2024, Cargo's default targets (`src/main.rs`, `tests/*.rs`), empty `[dependencies]`, package metadata. Before, only a manifest with explicit `[[bin]]` and `[[test]]` targets and every discovery switch off was accepted, with errors such as "Rust sources and tests must be nonempty immutable tuples".
  - A crate without an integration test is verified by its checker alone. Anything else Cargo would build (`src/lib.rs`, `src/bin`, `build.rs`, dependencies, a second test) is refused with the reason.
  - Rust `char` values print as Rust's `{:?}` does (`'b'`, `'\n'`, `'\u{200d}'`), not LLDB's `U+0062 U+0062 U'b'`.
  - A Vec whose elements Peeky does not read gets a note: its value is LLDB's summary, not the contents.
- **Java scopes in `describe`** (#91).
