  - `describe` lists each Java method's line span and the names it can see (`this`, parameters, locals), as it does for Python, C, C++ and Rust: `names in BalanceBug.assign (demo/BalanceBug.java:19-32): workers, tasks, order, task, best, w`.
  - The names come from the class files' LocalVariableTable (launched builds use `javac -g`; attached JVMs use their own classes), read by the same bounded class reader. Classes compiled without local names say so (`scopes_unavailable`).
- **Fourth agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-19-agent-fourth-round.md)): Java aliasing, C integer overflow, a plain Rust crate. All three agents reached a general verified fix on their first `verify` (33, 27 and 29 calls).
  - Fixed: `object_ref(value)` for Java identity; `(args)`/`(sets)` rows in `diff`; `verify --timeout`, `--quiet` and `seconds`; `job wait --json` for any job; `ArrayList` children are elements; a `->` selector hint; a guide that fits one read; the checker descriptor in the cookbook.
  - Filed: #90 (Rust char/tuple elements), #91 (Java describe scopes), #92 (C declaration-line stops), #93 (smaller evidence and describe gaps).
- **Java fields and array elements take `--set`** (#86).
  - `--set current.end=10`, `this.count=0`, `arr[0]=1` and `arr[-1]=9` change scalar fields and array elements reached from the stopped method's locals, in a probe or with `session set`.
  - Final and static fields, Strings, boxed values and an `ArrayList`'s elements are refused (`MUTATION_REJECTED`), and so are map entries in any language. Captured fields and elements carry `settable`.
  - The worker locates the parent again from its frame and scope and writes through the parent's own reference. It then reads the value back from the object; if the new value cannot be confirmed, the session closes.
  - Peeky's Java launcher refuses a field write whose name a hiding static field or a second field shares: java-debug writes by `fieldByName`, which would pick a field other than the one listed. Bundles mark this with `peekyFieldSet`; older bundles change only locals and say to rebuild.
  - Other languages refuse a field or element `--set` at launch, before the program runs.
  - Example: `examples/java/demo/MergeBug.java` (cookbook 7.3).
- **Settable locals up front** (#77).
  - Probe rows carry `settable` (`[settable]` in text) for root locals, so the observation probe tells which interventions will work.
  - A refused `--set` lists what can be changed at that stop (`settable here: …`).
  - A C/C++ local declared below the stop is MUTATION_REJECTED.
- **Rust scalars by their Rust types** (#79).
  - Locals and parameters take theirs from the build's MIR: `usize`, `u64`, `f64` and `char`, where LLDB says `unsigned long`, `double` or `char32_t`.
  - Fields take the one primitive LLDB's name can be.
  - `u8`/`i8` values print as integers, not C character literals.
  - LLDB's name is kept as `debugger_type`.
- **Several edits per candidate** (#76): `verify` and candidate probes take repeated `--old`/`--new` (or `-file`) pairs, each quoting the original file, up to 32 and never overlapping. They are built as one edit with their combined effect, so workers, hashes and diffs are unchanged; a candidate probe maps `--at` through every edit. The HTTP probe's `edit` takes a list.
- **Exact integers in the evidence store** (#75): schema 4 makes `captures.num` NUMERIC, so `600` stays `600` and 64-bit integers are exact. The table is rebuilt in capture order, and older stores are upgraded in place.
- **C/C++ locals declared on the stop line** (#92, found by the fourth evaluation).
  - At a breakpoint on `long long us = ms * 1000;`, `us` showed the previous call's value, `[settable]`. It is now withheld (`declared on line 3, which has not run yet at this stop`) and refused by `--set`, exactly when the stop is at the line's first instruction in its function: the instruction pointer, less the executable's load slide (`__TEXT` on macOS, the header segment on Linux), against the build's line table.
  - Where control enters the line is found per block: the line's first instruction in the block that declares the local (a braced one-line loop body has its own), and after the prologue on a function's first line, where its breakpoint is.
  - An address a loop jumps back to is never withheld: Peeky disassembles the function once per session (DAP `disassemble`) and leaves an address alone when a later jump returns to it. Neither is a line's first row reached from a later line's code (a `for (` or `switch (` header over several lines). That covers x86-64, where `while ((x = next()) > 0)` tests at the line's first instruction with `x` live, and a `do` body's first line, whose last-pass value is shown with a note on its row.
  - Mid-line stops (a return from a call) still show the value. Unoptimized arm64 and x86-64 builds only; anything unknown (a load address or jump in an unrecognized form, a jump through a register) withholds nothing.
- **C/C++ locals declared below the stop are withheld** (#74).
  - `no value yet: declared at line 18, below this stop`. They are never settable or expanded.
  - The rule comes from DWARF declaration lines and the line table. It applies only when:
    - every same-named automatic local in the functions running code on that line is declared below;
    - and that code comes before the declaration's.
  - It never applies with `setjmp` (in the executable, the sources or a header's `__builtin_setjmp`; rows carry a note saying so), statics or parameters. With a `goto` label it now withholds as unproven (sixth agent evaluation).
  - Four review rounds found ten cases where a live local was withheld, plus a misleading note on the stop line; all are fixed with real-build regressions (the note was dropped).
  - Line tables now resolve each file once per table: an STL-heavy C++ probe went from 1.45 s to 1.31 s.
- **Third agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-18-agent-third-round.md)): Rust, C and Java bugs, each fixed and verified on the first `verify`, all three after a candidate probe. The eval-fixes PR fixes the findings:
  - `queries hits`/`run` keep error text out of `value`;
  - owned `Vec`s read at a loop head after `vec!` (std macro code counts only on lines that can invoke a macro);
  - Rust `f64`/`f32` scalars print as Rust does;
  - failed probes keep their output, and `NO_STOP` gives the exit code and names Peeky options placed after `--`;
  - `diff` gets a `(line)` row and gives line and column.
  - From its review rounds:
    - the macro-line premise is qualified per toolchain (`test_rust_macro_lines.py`);
    - functions using `debug_assert!` can be proven;
    - Rust builds with many diagnostics are `BUILD_FAILED` with rustc's errors;
    - programs using `HashMap` no longer exceed the 2 MiB DWARF cap.
  - Issues filed: #74 to #79.
- **#73 (`f8e5efa`):** Rust containers of every scalar element type (`Vec<T>`, `&[T]`, `&mut [T]`, `&Vec<T>` for i8–i64, u8–u64, isize, usize, bool, f32, f64), with floats printed exactly as Rust's `{:?}`.
- **#71 (`146ab4f`): owned Rust `Vec<i32>`/`String` locals** are read from memory where the build's MIR proves them initialized at the stop line (closes #53).
  - The binary crate is compiled through a rustc wrapper: `-Zmir-opt-level=0`, a `PreCodegen` MIR dump, legacy mangling, and `RUSTC_BOOTSTRAP` for that crate only.
  - `rust_mir` runs a definite-initialization dataflow, checked at every place code of the stop line can be.
  - Five review rounds found five false proofs (merged codegen blocks, matching by elimination, a stop in another file, two kinds of macro twins), each fixed with a real-build regression.
- **#70 (`ea6c728`):** the Java adapter test infers a dropped exit the way the worker does (#27, observation 3).
- **#27 root cause found and patched; lost events fail fast** (branch `fix/java-lost-stop`, PR #123). java-debug sometimes never reported a stop on an attached JVM (Venice; twice in the attach tests under heavy load), and the probe or session waited out its timeout. See [JAVA_ATTACH.md](JAVA_ATTACH.md#lost-stop-events).
  - **Root cause** (found by the second review round, which reproduced it naturally): java-debug's "Event Hub" thread built `logger.fine("JDI Event: " + event)` for every JDI event even with FINE off. Naming a short-lived thread the JVM had already collected threw `ObjectCollectedException`, which killed the thread, and no JVM event was processed again (stops, steps, threads, exits; probably #29's lost exits too).
  - **Patch:** `scripts/setup_java_adapter.py` patches the pinned `EventHub.java` (`eventhub-log-guard`: log only when FINE is on, catch `RuntimeException` around it). The patch is idempotent, fails loudly if its target moved, and is listed in `adapter.json`. Peeky proves it from the compiled `EventHub.class` (it calls `Logger.isLoggable`) and refuses unpatched bundles (`UNSUPPORTED`), so rebuild local and Venice bundles. The CI lanes build fresh bundles, so they need no change. The review's churn runs: 18 of 18 completed with the patch.
  - **Dead event thread → `DEBUGGER_ERROR` at once:** the adapter's stderr is kept (last 16 KiB, `debugger_events.stderr`). `Exception in thread "Event Hub"` there ends any wait immediately (launched and attached JVMs). The session is closed and kept as a failed one: it repeats its error and its `report` keeps the stderr tail.
  - **Review round 3** (no High or Medium). Its lows are fixed:
    - **Stderr reader.** It owns its descriptor and stops on request. Before, on Linux, a descendant holding the pipe made `close()` wait 3 s and leave a reader that stole a later file's bytes; now close takes 0.1 s on that repro.
    - **Patch check.** The patch is proven from the compiled class; a forged `adapter.json` record no longer passes.
    - **Stderr kept on failure.** The tail survives the event-thread error, in the probe result and the session's report.
    - **Scanning.** The pattern is found in overlong lines, split writes, a last line without a newline, and from an adapter that exits right after it.
  - **Review round 4** (one older Medium, fixed). With no client waiting, the pause guard saw neither a lost stop nor a dead event thread when the breakpoint was pending at launch (a `suspend=y` attach, as with Gradle's `--debug-jvm`). Only `wait` took the `breakpoint` event that verifies such a breakpoint, and the guard never looked at `dap.fatal`.
    - The guard now closes a session whose adapter is fatal first, with or without a pause budget. With no stop pending, it takes the `breakpoint` events that come before any stop or exit, never the stop or exit itself, before polling.
    - Real-JDK tests: the review's repro now fails fast in about 7 s (it held the JVM for 15 s); the dead event thread ends the session in 0.5 s with the guard alone; beside a guard ticking every 10 ms, a waiting client receives every stop exactly once (hits 1, 2 and 3, each `stop_id` once).
  - **Review round 5** (no High or Medium):
    - **Regression fixed.** With `--max-pause`, any session's adapter crash reached the client as `NO_SESSION` instead of `DEBUGGER_ERROR`, because the guard's drain met the dead connection and closed the session. The guard now drains only for attached JVMs, leaves a failed connection to the client's next request, and keeps a failed session for any failure it meets itself.
    - **Docs.** They now say that the guard's own `wait` may take a stop that the client's next `wait` then reports once.
  - The first version (`7d3ad07`) served such a stop as recovered. Its adversarial review found wrong results (repros in the workspace's `work/vr27/`, outside the repository):
    - java-debug's class-load requests suspend every thread, so a breakpoint line that never ran (a thread blocked entering `synchronized`) was served as a hit;
    - its late-report bookkeeping turned lost step reports into hits, served stale stops, and could leave a thread suspended past `--max-pause`.
  - Now a stop is never served. While waiting, the worker polls `threads` and one-frame `stackTrace`. Evidence is exactly one suspended thread with no step in flight, at a verified session breakpoint's exact source and line, while every other thread checked runs. The checks cover the JVM's own threads before and after the rest, a window of up to 256 threads, and two rechecks of the suspect, each followed by the JVM's own threads. The same sighting must hold in 3 consecutive polls spanning 4 s, with no report in between.
  - On that evidence, the wait ends with `DEBUGGER_ERROR` (`Lost stop: …`) naming the thread, location, breakpoint and unreported time. Peeky records the diagnosis (history `lost_stop`, `debugger_events`) and detaches, so the JVM runs on.
  - The failed session repeats the error, reports its final state and closes cleanly. Polls start 2 s after a resume, run at most every 2 s within 1 s each (40–90 ms with 300 threads), and stay out of the DAP trace. Launched JVMs are not polled.
  - Real-JDK tests: a probe fails in about 6.6 s and the JVM completes unchanged; a session fails after a served hit; a lost step report on a breakpoint line never errs; and the review's class-load storm (the waiter seen at the breakpoint line in every poll) ends normally with 0 hits. The churn scenario reports its stop with the patched adapter; with `PEEKY_JAVA_ADAPTER_UNPATCHED`, the old adapter's dead event thread ends the session in under 5 s.
  - Round-2 lows: a sighting followed by the program's exit is reported as a stop never reported (`unreported_stop`; a probe fails with `DEBUGGER_ERROR`), never `NO_STOP`. The JVM-thread checks are Reference Handler, Finalizer and Common-Cleaner (Signal Dispatcher has no Java frames), and a lost stop holds its thread about 4–8 s regardless of `--max-pause`.
- **#143: a failed or timed-out launch no longer leaves the program running** (branch `fix/launch-timeout-orphans`, PR #147). On a macOS host where listing `~/Documents` blocked in the kernel, pydevd hung before `initialized` in every Python launch. Peeky timed out correctly but left each debuggee (`debugpy --connect …`) running under PID 1, and dozens piled up. debugpy's launcher starts the program in a process group of its own and announces its pid only after the launch, so ending the adapter's group never reached it.
  - **Every session end now ends what the session started** (`peeky/processes.py`, `DAP.close`): failed or timed-out launches, failed requests, `close`, idle workers and probes. Before `disconnect`, Peeky reads the adapter's descendants and the program the adapter announced (`process`, java-debug `processid`), the latter kept only while its start time matches. After `disconnect`, whatever of those still runs gets SIGTERM (to its whole group when it leads one), then SIGKILL after 1 s. An attached program is detached, never ended.
  - **Reported:** errors that end a session carry `cleanup` (`terminated pid N`, `nothing Peeky could identify was still running`, `detached; the attached program keeps running`, or why not), and so do `close` results and `peeky session close`. A launch whose program never reaches the debugger fails with `TIMEOUT: The debuggee did not start …`, and its hint says to check that the program can read its own path.
  - **Tests,** each failing on main:
    - fake adapters: a program that never initializes, its child, one that ignores SIGTERM, one whose adapter died after announcing it, a reused pid, a failed attach, and the HTTP payload;
    - real debugpy, hung before `initialized`;
    - lldb-dap hung after the program started;
    - C and Rust programs stopped when lldb-dap dies;
    - a launched JVM whose adapter dies, and a failed real attach that leaves the JVM running to its normal end.
  - **Review round 1** (no process Peeky did not start was touched; orphans gone in the common cases). Its two findings are fixed:
    - **Medium: a program's own children outlived its dead debugger, and `cleanup` said otherwise.** A child in a session of its own (all platforms), and on Linux lldb-dap 18 the child in the program's group too, survived `kill -9` of the adapter, and so did children a program left behind when it exited. Peeky now lists the session's processes while it runs (every second, after each stop and announcement): descendants of the adapter and of every process listed before, still the same by start time, and the members of their process groups. It ends all of them that still run, and their groups. Only a process started after the last listing, in a group of its own, whose parent has exited, cannot be identified. When the debugger was gone, `cleanup` says so (`…; the debugger was gone: a process the program started in the last N s, … may survive`). `nothing was left running` became `nothing Peeky could identify was still running`.
    - **Low: a launched JVM got no grace when its adapter died.** It shares java-debug's process group, which got SIGKILL right after the JVM's SIGTERM, so shutdown hooks never ran. The adapter's group now gets SIGTERM too, and SIGKILL only after the 1 s grace.
    - Tests, each failing on the reviewed head `cb5cbec`: fake adapters (children in the program's group and in another session after the adapter dies, a child a program left behind, a program in the adapter's group that needs its grace), the listing rules on synthetic process tables, a real C program and a real Python program whose debugger is killed with both kinds of children, and a real JVM's shutdown hook.
  - **Review round 2** (both fixes hold on macOS and Linux; nothing Peeky did not start was recorded or ended; the listing costs about 2% of a core per session). Its Low is fixed: a session that failed with no request in flight (the pause guard resuming a stop after `kill -9` of lldb-dap, a lost stop, an adapter's fatal event) kept `cleanup` only in `report`. The repeated error and `close` (`peeky session close --json`) now carry it too. The test (pause guard after the adapter dies) fails on the reviewed head `c41d980`.
  - `test_native_adapter_death_reaps_stopped_target` now passes with Apple LLDB too. Before, the stopped program outlived its debugger; now it is ended, and on macOS launchd leaves it a zombie, which the test counts as exited.
- **#155: return values, after `step --out` and in probes** (branch `feat/return-values`; the seventh evaluation rated it High). In C++ `sched`, the comparator `before()` that `std::sort` calls was the proof of the bug, but `step --out` stopped in `sort.h`, where `inspect` refused everything, and no probe could capture a return.
  - **Found on the way:** Apple's LLDB steps out past libc++'s hidden frames (its "libc++ frame recognizer") and past code without debug information (libc's `qsort`), then lists a `(Return Value)` that is not that function's. Stepping out of `before(a, b)` (it returned false) into `std::sort` listed `true`. So the old advice in COOKBOOK §11 could show a wrong value.
  - **Sessions:** at the first step, Peeky keeps LLDB's step out to one frame (it turns the libc++ recognizer and the avoidance of code without debug information off for the session), and it records where every step starts. After a step, the value the debugger lists (`(Return Value)`, java-debug's `⎯►name()`) is shown only if the stop is that call's return: the caller's frame at the instruction where the call returns, with the frames below unchanged. It is shown apart from the locals (`returned`, `[Returned]`, `session get return`), labelled with the function that returned, also when the step stops in a library (where nothing else is read).
  - **Older lldb-dap:** before LLVM 21 (the Linux lanes' 18 and 20) lldb-dap lists no `(Return Value)`; Peeky reads LLDB's own record of the step out (`thread info`, `Return value: (bool) $0 = false`) for the stopped thread, under the same checks.
  - **Probes:** `--capture return` steps out of each call after its hit and records a `(return)` row (`phase` `returned`; `function` that returned, `line`/`location` it returned to), saved in the evidence (`queries hits` and `wide` include it). Pending calls form a stack by depth, so recursive calls each get their own return. A line reached again at the same depth before the return (a loop's next pass, or a call that ended by an exception and the next one) leaves the earlier hit withheld, since the stack cannot tell them apart.
  - **Withheld, with the reason:** structs, classes, strings, references, Rust enums, `Option` and chars, values LLDB cannot read, functions returning nothing, returns in optimized builds, and a step that went past the caller. C and C++ show integers, `bool`, characters, floats and pointers; Rust its integers (u8 as `65`), `bool` and floats; Java any type (JDI's exact value). Python: not available, since debugpy shows a return only by writing `__pydevd_ret_val_dict` into the program's globals; `--capture return` is refused there (`UNSUPPORTED`).
  - **Tests,** each failing on main: the `sched` comparator's every return (C++), and the session's step out into `sort.h`; recursion, loops, a `qsort` comparator, structs, `void`, `double` and an optimized build (C); `sort_by_key`'s key function, recursion, `bool`, `u8` and `Option` (Rust); a `Comparator` the JDK calls and a session step out (Java); the attribution, withholding and pending-call rules on scripted stops (unit).
  - **Review round 1 of PR #162** (one High, two Lows, two nits; each fix has a test that fails on the reviewed head `0439407`):
    - **High: a call that ended by an exception, `longjmp` or panic was credited with a later call's value** (macOS and Linux, sessions and probes). LLDB's step out waits for the return address in the caller's frame; when the call is unwound and its caller survives, the wait stays armed, and the next call from the same place, in the same frame, ends it with that call's value. `left_function` could not tell (same name, address and line). Fix: while a step out runs, Peeky watches the caller on the stepping thread with conditional breakpoints (`$sp >=` the caller's stack pointer) on the call instruction, on every branch to the return address and on every computed jump in the caller (read from LLDB's disassembly, cached per return address). None of them can run in a frame at or above the caller's while the call is live, so a stop there means the call is gone: the step ends where the caller went on (reason `step`, no hit counted, LLDB drops the stale step out) and the value is withheld (`it never returned`). When the step does end at the return address, the caller's stack pointer must be back where the call left it, or the value is withheld; and a probe checks that each return comes back to the frames under its own hit. This holds whatever ended the call (an unwinder entry point Peeky does not know, `siglongjmp` from a signal handler): any way back to that address is watched, not the ways out of the call. Unwinder breakpoints (`__cxa_throw`, `longjmp`, `rust_panic`) were rejected: they fire for exceptions caught inside the call too, and miss other routes. Cost: about 4 ms per hit for a comparator (`sched`, 35 hits: 2.60-2.71 s before, 2.73-2.74 s after) and 5-9 ms for a recursive function (`fib`, 100 hits, one condition evaluation per hit): about 5 debugger requests per step out, plus one disassembly per call site.
    - **Low: a multi-threaded callee failed the whole probe** with session advice. A step out refused because another thread stopped too, a stop on another thread before a return, or a step that ends on a thread with no pending call now withholds those returns (`another thread stopped at a breakpoint before it returned`) and the probe goes on with every hit's rows.
    - **Low: an `always_inline` function's reason** pointed to `--capture return` inside a probe that used it. Its frame shares its caller's instruction (no call comes before it), and the row now says it was inlined.
    - **Nits:** the Python refusal is made before anything runs and keeps no run in the evidence; Java's empty return reads `java-debug listed no returned value: the method returns nothing, or it ended by an exception`.
    - **Merge of main** (`--set-at`, #151): `(return)` rows work with `--set-at` lines inside the function (the step out is interrupted, the change made, and the return still credited to its call, checked by its frames).
  - **Review round 2 of PR #162** (the High holds: no route back to the return address escapes the watch; one Medium regression, two Lows; each fix has a test that fails on the reviewed head `c2550c3`):
    - **Medium: `step --out` over a deep recursion failed the session** (`DAP control event history overflow`) at about half the depth main survived. Cause: lldb-dap sends `continued` after each of LLDB's own stops it resumes from, and a step out over a recursion through the caller makes one per level at the return address (main), plus, with the watch, one per level at the watched call. They filled the 2,000-entry history that guards the session's stops. Fixes: a run of `continued` events is now one entry (it keeps the first cursor and a count), so no number of them evicts a stop: main's own limit (it failed at 2,000 levels) is gone too. The watch is bounded inside LLDB: each watched instruction has a counter breakpoint (ignore count 128, auto-continue) whose command disables the whole watch; Peeky reads the counters when the step ends and withholds the return if the watch was dropped. Deleting the watch from Peeky while the program runs was tried and rejected: LLDB then reported a thread that had just hit the removed breakpoint as an exception. An optimized build's step out is not watched (its return is withheld anyway). Measured on macOS (`deep/d.c`, `session step --out` from the outermost call): depth 100: 0.35 s main, 0.77 s now (value shown); 1,000: 3.24 s main, 3.79 s now (withheld); 1,900: 6.21 s, 6.72 s; 2,000: main fails, 3,000: 10.27 s now. A probe at depth 1,000 completes with its rows. Per-hit cost for the `sched` comparator and `fib` is unchanged from round 1.
    - **Low: a large caller withheld every return** ("no call instruction comes before where it returns"): LLDB refuses to disassemble a function of about 10,000 instructions unless forced, and Peeky disassembled again at each call site. The caller's disassembly is now forced, parsed once per function and reused for its other call sites; a failure is cached and named in the reason.
    - **Low: probes did not follow mutual recursion** (`f` → `c` → `f`: only the innermost hit got a value). After a return, a probe now steps out until the next pending call's own frame returns, and checks each return against its hit's frames.
    - **Pre-existing on main, fixed:** a step a breakpoint interrupted stayed in LLDB, and a later `continue` stopped with reason `step` where it would have ended. When a stop interrupts a step, Peeky now discards what is left of it on that thread (`thread plan discard`), so `continue` runs to the next breakpoint.
  - **Review round 3 of PR #162** (return values clean: `deep`, `big` and `rec` correct, the counters reset, a dropped watch always withheld, the merged `continued` events safe, the discard working). One Low and one Nit, each with a test that fails on the reviewed head `fcac74a`:
    - **Low (pre-existing): a step another thread's stop caught mid-way was reported as ended.** In `th/t2.c` (2 of 4 runs, on the PR and on main alike), lldb-dap sent two `stopped` events for one stop: `step` for the stepping thread, caught inside `usleep`, then `breakpoint` for the other thread. Peeky took the first for the step's end, kept LLDB's step, and a later `continue` stopped with reason `step` at line 24. Peeky now discards every stepping thread's step at every stop (a step that ended left nothing, so that is a no-op) and, when LLDB still had the reported thread's step, reports the stop as `interrupted` (`the step was interrupted here: another thread stopped at the same time; session continue serves its stop`); a probe withholds that call's return and goes on. The test drives the worker with lldb-dap's two events, so it does not depend on the threads' timing; `th/t2.c` itself now shows the interrupted step, then the other thread's hit, then the exit.
    - **Nit: a probe that timed out gave its pending hits no `(return)` row.** They now get `the probe timed out before it returned`.
    - **Docs:** `--capture return` through a deep or wide mutual recursion costs about 7.5 ms per nested call on macOS (`wide/w.c` at depth 13: 62.6 s against 1.8 s without `return`); COOKBOOK and the agent guide say to limit `--hits` or probe a line one call reaches.
    - **Merge of main** at `3ff55f9` (#160, multi-stop lines, evidence schema 14): the conflicts were the worker's per-stop state, `by_hit` (a stop's `stop`/`stops` come from its observed rows, never a `(return)` row) and the docs; no schema change here. Then `44a3082` (#159, same-named C/C++ locals) merged without conflicts.
- **#168: the announced-pid cleanup test was flaky under load, and so was the product** (branch `fix/cleanup-flake`). `test_a_program_whose_adapter_died_is_ended_by_its_announced_pid` failed in the Docker `unit` lane on two branches while two heavy jobs ran; on `main`, 3 of 50 runs failed in the Linux image beside two lane runs.
  - **The test's timing assumption:** it expected `cleanup` to say `the debugger was gone`, which Peeky says when the adapter had exited at the final listing. But an exiting adapter closes its pipes before it exits, so Peeky could see the end of its output, list its processes while it was still exiting, and (rightly) say nothing about the debugger being gone. The 3 failures were all this. The fake adapter now leaves a holder on its output pipe until it has exited (the holder waits to be reparented), so Peeky always sees its end after it. Peeky now also checks that the adapter is running after it reads the process table, not before, so `ran` means the adapter's tree was whole for the whole read.
  - **The product race:** the listing that the announcement asks for can come only after the adapter died (it is asynchronous, at least 0.25 s after the last one, and the fake adapter dies 0.5 s after announcing). Then no ancestry led to the program any more: Peeky ended it by its announced pid alone, and its child, in its group, kept running. Fix: Peeky starts the adapter in a session of its own, and a process can only inherit a session, never join one, so every process still in the adapter's session was started from it, whatever became of its parents. The process table now carries each process's session (Linux `/proc/PID/stat`; macOS `getsid`, since `ps` gives none), and those processes count, with their groups and descendants. Peeky's own session never counts. `cleanup` now names only a process in a session of its own (`setsid`) as one that may have escaped.
  - **Tests:** the program and its child are ended when no listing ran before the adapter died (fails on `main`: the child survives), the session rule on a synthetic table (Peeky's own and unknown sessions never count), and the process table's session.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #170** (a High, rare, and a Low; each fix has a test that fails on the reviewed head `b66cce9`):
    - **High: a reused pid could make an unrelated session count as the adapter's.** The listing asked `Popen.poll()` whether the adapter ran, which reaps a dead one; until `close`, the pid was free, and a new session leader there (a shell, ssh, another adapter) made session N someone else's, whose processes `close` then ended. The reviewer showed it by wrapping the pid space in Docker. Peeky now asks with `waitid(WNOWAIT)` (the process table's state where Python lacks it, macOS before 3.13), so a dead adapter stays a zombie, which keeps its pid, group id and session id reserved, and only `close` reaps it, after its last signal. An adapter reaped by anyone else (a caller, never Peeky) proves no session and gets no group signal, unless a process Peeky recorded, still the same one, is still in that group or session: an id is never reused while it has members. A launched JVM's child in java-debug's group is still ended that way. The test checks that a dead adapter stays a zombie through several listings, and that nothing calls `poll()` on it.
    - **Low: the announcement was no longer tested, and a program that left the session kept its child.** With `Family.announce` stubbed, both tests still passed, since the session rule found the program. Now a program that calls `setsid` itself, with no listing before the adapter dies, is found only by its announcement (with it stubbed, the program and its child run on), and the announced program's process group is ended too (start-time checked), so its child no longer survives (it did on `main` as well).
  - **Review round 2 of PR #170** (no High or Medium; one Low; the tests fail on `7483124`): **an inherited ignored SIGCHLD still allowed the pid-reuse kill.** With it, the kernel reaps the adapter as it exits, behind `Popen`: `returncode` stays None and `waitid` fails with `ECHILD`, so the guards (which asked `returncode`) did not apply. In Docker, a new session leader at the adapter's pid had itself and its child ended by `close`; on macOS, `killpg` went to a freed id. Now every command that starts adapters (`peeky`, `peeky-worker`, `peeky-probe`, `peeky-agent`) sets an ignored SIGCHLD back to the default first, and one helper (`DAP._adapter_state`) tells "reaped" wherever `returncode` was asked: `returncode` set, `waitid` failing with `ECHILD`, or (without `waitid`) the adapter missing from the process table or its pid showing another start (recorded at spawn). With no `waitid` and no table, the adapter's group is not signalled, and `cleanup` says `nothing was ended`. The reviewer's scenario with SIGCHLD ignored (pid wrap in Docker) now leaves the unrelated leader and its child running.
- **#173: block declarations and bindings in optimized builds** (branch `fix/o1-bindings`; found in PR #172's review). At `-O1`, a range-`for` binding's line showed plain `t` as the outer local (`long 1`) where the code names the binding, with only the generic optimized-build warning; so did a block's enumerator, `typedef` and `extern` at `-O1` and `-O2`. The block scan (#144, with #159/#172's bindings) ran only for a local the debug information places in the frame's memory (`DW_OP_fbreg`/`DW_OP_breg`); an optimized build keeps the outer local in a register or as a constant, so the scan skipped it. Each function now also lists those locals (neither in the frame nor a static), lldb-dap lists them all the same, and the scan judges them as at `-O0`. On a range-`for` line, an optimized build's stop cannot be told from the line's start (where the range is read with the outer local), so there the row says `may not be what the code reads`, naming the binding. Tests at `-O1` and `-O2` (C: an enumerator, a `typedef`, an `extern`; C++: a range-`for` header and a one-line loop) fail on `main`.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #175** (clean on values; one Low, over-withholding with a wrong reason; the test fails on `41a9f15`): at `-O1`, clang keeps a block's `static long t = 50` that is never changed as a constant (`DW_AT_const_value`, no address), and lldb-dap lists it as a local, `t @ oc.c:8`, which is what the code names. The scan judged that row as the outer local, hidden by its own declaration, and said the static was not listed. A `static` (or `extern`, or a thread's own) whose declarator is on the row's declaration line is now that row's own, where the name appears once on that line (otherwise Peeky cannot tell which declaration the row is, and withholds it). The outer local's row says the static is `listed separately as t @ oc.c:8` where a row of it is listed (by the frame's rows, or the debug information when they are not at hand), and `which the debugger does not list` only otherwise.
- **#180: inline assembly that can read a file is refused before building** (branch `fix/incbin`; found in PR #178's review). The assembler's `.incbin` and `.include` read a file that no compiler lists in its dependency file, so the provenance check missed it: `__asm__(".incbin \"/abs/data.bin\"")` built and ran with Apple clang, Linux clang and GCC (`blob=SECRET`), and so did Rust's `global_asm!`. Now `UNSUPPORTED` (`Inline assembly that can read a file …`), with nothing compiled or kept as evidence:
  - **C and C++:** each unit is preprocessed first (`-E`, the build's own flags, as a build command), and every `asm`/`__asm`/`__asm__` group in the whole unit (system headers too) is judged by its string literals, adjacent ones joined and escapes read (`"\x2eincbin"`). Refused: `.incbin` or `.include` (any case), an assembler macro that could spell one (`.macro`, `.irp`, `.irpc`), an operand where a directive's name is (`.inc%c0`, a statement starting `%c0`), and in C++ Clang's constant-expression templates (`asm((s()))`). GNU as and LLVM MC have no other directive that reads a file; Peeky builds no `.s`/`.S` file and passes no `-Wa,` option.
  - **Rust:** before any command, each `asm!`, `global_asm!` and `naked_asm!` (`r#asm!`, a path) is judged by its literals (template lines, `concat!` pieces, escapes, raw strings), the words of a `stringify!`, and the same operand rule (`{}`). Where a template may come from elsewhere (a `macro_rules!` argument, a renamed asm macro, a macro the caller names, `include_str!`/`env!`), such a directive in any declared file counts.
  - A `.incbin` string outside inline assembly builds as before (`printf(".incbin")`, `println!`). C23 `#embed` needs nothing: the dependency file lists it. Tests: real builds (C with clang and GCC, Rust) refused, each building on `main`, beside a plain `.incbin` string that still builds; unit cases for the scanners.
- **#191: Rust locals and parameters named in Unicode are proven as their ASCII twins** (branch `fix/rust-quoted-idents`; found while fixing #179). LLVM IR quotes a local whose name is not plain ASCII (`%"gr\C3\B6\C3\9Fe"`, its bytes in hex) and writes its debug-info name so too, and the IR's proofs matched only plain names (`%([-\w.$]+)`): a reference parameter `größe: &Vec<i64>` stayed a raw pointer (`0x16b1a2fa0`) where `values: &Vec<i64>` reads, and no such local was proven kept in memory. Both declaration syntaxes now take a quoted name, and it and the debug-info name read back from hex (`ir_string`, as #179 reads file names), so `größe` reads `[3, 4, 5]` with its Rust type, and a `black_box`ed `zähler` is proven in memory. An owned `Vec` so named read already (the MIR names it as the source does). Still: such a name is `[not selectable]` and never `[settable]`, as selectors (in every language) are ASCII names. Tests (each fails on main): the IR parser (both syntaxes, a spill copy, a parameter), and a real build (`größe` reads; `stücke`, an owned `Vec`, too).
  - Review (PR #194, round 1): a parameter's proof was revoked only by another *declared* variable of its name, but rustc declares no slot for a shadow it never stores (`let é: &Vec<i64>;`, written NFD, which rustc normalises to NFC): the IR names it (`DILocalVariable(name: "\C3\A9")`) and nothing else. ASCII names had the same gap. A proof now needs the parameter to be its function's only variable of that name (in either Unicode form) in the debug information, declared or not; an unreadable name (`\q`, `\FF`, an unclosed quote) could be any and revokes them all, an unnamed one (`_: i32`) none, and a declaration whose slot name LLVM cannot have written counts as no slot in memory. No wrong value was seen on main (lldb-dap lists both bindings past the shadow, as `w @ main.rs:1` and `w @ main.rs:3`, which no proof matches); before the shadow, `w` now shows only its pointer where main read `[3, 4, 5]`. Over rustc's IR of 159 test and repro crates (each that builds as one file), 41 of 8,642 parameter proofs went, and no memory proof changed: the two shadows, and 39 in standard-library functions (`Range::next`, `Chars::next`, `Vec::as_mut_ptr`) that rustc also inlines, whose inlined copy is a second subprogram with the same linkage name (so the same frame hash) and its own `self`. None in a crate's own functions. Tests (the shadow ones fail on 42e6536): the IR parser (an NFC parameter with an NFD shadow, ASCII, unnamed, unreadable and unplaced variables, LLVM's numbered `größe1`/`größe2` beside a natural `größe1`, dotted suffixes, `\22`/`\5C`, refused `\q`/`\FF`, a slot named for another variable), rustc's own IR of the repro, and a real build.
- **#190: every probe error before the program starts is a refusal before launch** (branch `fix/before-launch-exit`; noticed in PR #189). Only errors that said so (`before_launch`) skipped the table and exited 2; a file outside the worker root (`INVALID_REQUEST`), an outdated Java adapter (`UNSUPPORTED`), a remote source mismatch (`PROVENANCE_CHANGED`), a failed build (`BUILD_FAILED`), a preparation timeout or a debugger that never initialized printed an empty table and exited 1, and remotely were even kept as evidence runs. Now the boundary is the debugger's `launch`/`attach` request: the worker marks any launch error before it sends one, `run_probe` any error before it asks the worker to launch, and `peeky probe` any error before the probe reaches a worker (its JSON then has `status: failed` and `refused_before_launch: true`; a broken connection, whose outcome is unknown, is not one). Errors once the request is sent keep their table and exit 1: the program may have run. Tests: one per code (unit, with stub targets and a fake debugger, the Java adapter through the CLI, a worker whose root lacks the file), each failing on `main`; the C and Rust `BUILD_FAILED` candidate tests now expect exit 2.
  - **Review of PR #196** (one blocking, three Low; each test fails on `bf0072c`): a remote probe's child had the worker's 40 s launch budget while the worker's watchdog killed it at 35 s and made up a failure without `refused_before_launch` (an empty table, exit 1, a saved run). The child now gets the HTTP cap less 10 s for its launch (`--launch-budget`, hidden), so a slow build answers itself, and writes a marker just before the launch request (`--launch-marker`), so a failure the worker makes up says whether it was sent. A remote answer that is not JSON, or not a probe result, is `TRANSPORT_LOST` (outcome unknown), and an error body that is not the worker's own is not a refusal before launch. Messages and docs say the debugger's launch or attach request was not sent, not "nothing ran" (a build, or a Python candidate's interpreter, may have run), "every probe is saved" became every probe not refused before launch, and ERRORS' `BUILD_FAILED`, the guide and the C/C++ and Rust pages say a probe's failed build exits 2 unsaved, `verify`'s 1.
- **#167: C++ with g++ is refused before building** (branch `fix/gpp-refusal`). `--driver-mode=g++` is a clang option, so a C++ target with `PEEKY_NATIVE_CXX=g++` failed with `BUILD_FAILED: unrecognized command-line option`. A C++ build now asks the compiler's `--version` once per compiler file and passes the flag only to clang; any other compiler gets `UNSUPPORTED: C++ targets need clang++ …` before anything runs, and no evidence run is kept. It goes by what the compiler says, not its name (macOS's `g++` is clang). C targets still build with GCC.
  - **Assessed, not enabled: the C++ suite with g++** (GCC 13, aarch64 Docker, `tests/cpp` and the C++ cases of `tests/native`, with the refusal removed locally): 33 of 287 fail. By cause: constructors, member initializers and temporaries, whose code and lines GCC places differently (#121, #140: 12); libstdc++ strings, whose debug information is complete under GCC, so LLDB's summary works and cuts at a NUL (#128, #137: 6); frame names (`::before(const Task &, const Task &)`, `<constructor>`, lambdas: 5); libstdc++ maps and sets (#119: 4); line tables and lexical blocks (a range-`for` header's stops, a line only an exception runs, braces that are no blocks: #157, #158: 3); location forms (a `const` struct, a bitfield's structured binding: 3). Two of them would show what Peeky cannot vouch for: a lambda's `this` while its object is being built is not withheld, and an `unordered_map` with a custom hash is listed empty, so `hci["ALPHA"]` reads "not found … would insert". A `cpp-gcc` lane would take about 5 minutes; making it pass means about 3 to 4 days to withhold what GCC's debug information does not prove, or about 2 weeks to prove the rules for GCC.
- **#183: interventions' real reasons, before the run where the build shows them; settable paths and types in `describe`** (branch `fix/intervention-reasons`; eighth agent evaluation, Java `calc` and Rust `board`).
  - Java: `op='+'` on a `char` was refused as "requires a numeric or boolean scalar literal" with a C/C++/Rust hint, at the stop; only a retry gave the rule. A char now takes a char literal (`'+'`, `'\n'`, `'\u0041'`) or a code point (`43`): Peeky sends java-debug the char between single quotes, and the launcher allows that only for a variable declared `char` (`peekyCharSet`; java-debug's `CharacterFormatter` takes a three-character quoted value as its char). A refusal at a stop names the type rule before the literal's shape (an older bundle: "a char too with an adapter bundle built by this version"). Before the run, each `--set` of a plain local is judged from the class files' local variable tables in every method on its line (`JavaSession.launch_refusal`): a name none declares there (`NOT_FOUND`, listing the locals), a declared type no change reaches (`StringBuilder`), a value it cannot hold (`left='+'` on a `long`), or a local never read after the line's start; the launch's `sets` carry each value for it. `describe` gives each name its declared type (`op (char)`, `results (StringBuilder)`) and lists the settable kinds exactly.
  - Rust: `--set 'entry="zed:50"'` at the first line of a `for entry in args` body was refused as "a statement under way has already read it into a temporary (... at its second line)". The temporary was the iterator's `next()` result, made before `entry` was moved out of it and read again by the next pass's `discriminant`; #105's copies were judged flow-insensitively. `Body.copies_at` follows the MIR in the order the code runs (an assignment to the local, or its storage starting or ending, leaves no copy of its new value), and the change is made (`RANKING:bob=60,alice=60,zed=50,carol=40`). A refusal now names where the temporary was made (`at line 17`), not an example, and a line every place of which is inside such a statement (a statement's later line) is refused before the run.
  - `describe` lists the paths a change reaches (`board::main: board.entries`, `bins[*].hi`), not the locals holding them (`board`, whose `Vec`s' elements are not settable; the `&str` parameter `name`, whose length is no field to change), and for Rust the functions where nothing can change, with the candidate edit that can (COOKBOOK 6.6). Rust types read as the program spells them (`Vec<u32>`, not `alloc::vec::Vec<unsigned int, alloc::alloc::Global>`; `debugger_type` keeps LLDB's), and a `Vec<String>` field (`self.names`) says why it stays raw, in `session get self` listings too.
  - Review round 1 (PR #192): (High) copies in the program's own bindings were not counted (`_copy_parts` skipped them as the program's), so `x = match x { n => { keep(&mut x); /* stop */ n } }` reported `set x: 1 -> 9` and printed 1, as did a `move` closure called after the stop. They count now: a binding holding a direct copy (`Body.direct_copies_at`: by value, never what a call given a pointer returns) blocks a change where the program uses it and then replaces or drops the local unread (`Body.copy_undoes`); where it reads the local again, the copy is its own history and the change is made (`let o = Outer { span: s, .. }` with `s.end` read later, as #105's tests expect). Refusals name the binding. Found with it: #183's ordered flow started from bb0 only, so a block whose entry held no copy was never walked and its own copies never flowed on (a `while let`'s `then_some(x)`); every block is now walked once. (High) Java's check before the run judged every method holding the line, where java-debug stops only in the first in its class file: of overloads `f(String)` and `f(int)` on one line, `f(int)` took `x=2` and the probe ran; only the first method holding the line counts now. (Low) `describe` walks records and fixed-size arrays at any depth (`grid.cells[*].n`), and says `… and N more` where it caps.
  - Review round 2 (PR #192): (High) `copy_undoes` took any read of the local, or any use of a pointer to it, as the program seeing the change: `let y = x; /* set x.b=9 */ println!("{}", x.a); x = y;` and `let y = x; /* set x=9 */ ignore(&x); x = y;` each reported a change the program never saw. Now only a read surely covering part of the changed part counts (`_root_access`, with the selector's MIR projection from the DWARF's declaration-ordered fields): a `copy`/`move` of an overlapping place, or a borrow only formatting a primitive reads; a borrow given to anything else is unknown. Copies are judged by the part they may have taken, and an aggregate's mark gate leaves them to each member. Measured on six crates (421 marks against 21406eb): 31 newly unsettable, none newly settable; 12 are changes the program never saw (`keep(&mut x)` lines, `arr = [s.a, k, s.b]` then `s.b` unread), 19 over-refuse where only `width(&s)` or `{:?}` reads the part. (Low) the ordered copy flow walks only blocks reachable from bb0 or a resumption, so an unreachable block adds no copy to a join.
  - Review round 3 (PR #192): (High) a borrow given to `Argument::new_display::<i64>` was counted as read where it was made, though only formatting the `fmt::Arguments` reads it: `ignore(format_args!("{}", x)); x = y;` and a dropped `format_args!` reported `set x: 1 -> 50` and printed 1. A read is now counted only at a call that formats: `_print`, `_eprint` or `format` written by std's own `print!`/`println!`/`eprint!`/`eprintln!`/`format!` (the call's span is in their `macros.rs`), traced back to the borrows through locals assigned once; `write!`, panics and anything else stay unknown.
  - Review round 4 (PR #192): (High) that provenance was a path suffix and the callee a bare name: a crate's own `src/library/std/src/macros.rs` whose macro called the crate's `fn format(_: Arguments) -> String` (formatting nothing) passed for `format!`, and `set x: 1 -> 50` printed 1. The span must now be exactly this toolchain's std or alloc `macros.rs` (`rust.std_macro_files`: the sysroot's sources, or `/rustc/<commit-hash>/` from the build's `rustc -vV`; nothing under the crate or its build), and the callee its full path, `format` alone only where no function of the crate's own prints so.
- **#204: a JDK class's own fields are never offered for a change** (branch `fix/java-jdk-internals`; ninth agent evaluation, Java `rechnung`). `session get a.bezeichnung` (a `String`) listed its internals and marked `hash` and `hashIsZero` `[settable]`: a String's cached hash that disagrees with its text corrupts every `HashMap` holding it, as `list.size` would an `ArrayList`. The launcher now names each listed field's declaring class (`peekyOwner`) and refuses writing one a JDK class declares; Peeky marks none of them, nor an element of an array one holds (a String's bytes), and `set` refuses naming the class (`it is declared by java.lang.String, a JDK class`), rows say `[not settable: declared by java.lang.String (the JDK's own state)]`, and a probe's `--set` of a field of a value declared a JDK type, which no class of the program declares, is refused before the run. A bundle without the marker changes no field and says to rebuild it. A `String` and a boxed number show as values (`Integer@57 {value=1001}`, in listings too), their fields read only by name (`a.artikelNr.value`). The program's own fields keep their rules, also in a class extending a JDK class.
  - Review round 1 (PR #210): (High) an array a JDK field holds stayed writable through another name: a String's `value` taken through reflection or `Unsafe` into a local, or a field of the program's, took `set alias[0]=78`, leaving the cached hash stale. The launcher now finds, before writing an element, what refers to the array (`referringObjects`, at most 64, through the rows of an array of arrays) and refuses one a JDK class's field holds (`the array is held by java.lang.String.value, a JDK class's field`); it refuses too where it cannot tell (more than 64, a JVM without instance info). Only the write pays for it: a row's mark still goes by its path. (High) JDK classes were told by package name, so a class of the program in `javax.foo` was refused as "a JDK class": the launcher now reports whether the JDK's own class loaders (bootstrap, platform) loaded the declaring class (`peekyJdkOwner`, marker `peekyJdkOwners`), and before the run any class not in the program's class files counts. (Low) an unrelated class's `int size` let `--set items.size=0` run to the stop: only program classes that may be the value (its subtypes as far as the class files tell, or ones extending another JDK class) defer to it. An element of an array an `Object[]` holds (`ArrayList<int[]>`) is the program's again, as on main.
  - Review round 2 (PR #210): (High) `jdk.jshell`, `jdk.compiler` and `jdk.jdi` load through the application class loader, so `shell.nextKeyIndex=0` on a `JShell` was allowed: a class in a named module `java.*` or `jdk.*` (JDI's `ReferenceType.module()`) is now the JDK's, the loader rule kept for Java 8. (High) an array inside an `Object[]` was taken for the program's by its type alone, though JDK caches keep arrays in `Object[]`s: the search now goes through any array holding it, and only the backing arrays of `ArrayList`, `Vector`, `ArrayDeque` and `Arrays.asList`'s list hold the program's elements, where the list itself is the program's, judged the same way but for its own iterators and sublists (an `ArrayList<int[]>`'s rows still change, inside a `for` over it too; a `HashMap<String, Object[]>`'s, and a `HashMap<String, List<int[]>>` list's, are refused). (Low) the search had no time bound: it now runs off the request thread and waits at most 2 s, less where the pause budget leaves less (the worker sends `peekyPauseLeftMs`, keeping 1 s), refusing past that; the JVM's own search cannot be interrupted, so it runs on until it ends.
  - Review round 3 (PR #210): (High) a list's iterator or sublist was skipped as a referrer, which proves only that the view does not own the list: a list whose iterator a `HashMap` held, or whose sublist `Collections.unmodifiableList` wrapped, let its aliased rows be written. The view is now followed to whoever holds it, within the same bounds (`in a list held, through an iterator or sublist, by java.util.HashMap$Node.value`); only the view-to-list edge is passed over, so a `for` loop's own iterator still leaves the rows the program's.
- **#174: a return followed across a line's stops in one call, and an honest reason where it is not** (branch `fix/return-multistop`). With `--capture return` on a line with several breakpoint locations (#160), the call's next stop at the line withheld the earlier hit's return with `the line ran again at the same depth before this call returned (a loop's next pass, or the call ended without returning and another began)`: wrong for a later location in the same pass. And `(return)` rows stored no `stop`.
  - **Followed where proven:** a step out is watched (#162): its caller's call instruction, every branch to the return address and every computed jump, in a frame at or above the caller's. A breakpoint on the stepping thread that interrupts it, with the watch kept whole (not dropped, not stopped at a watched instruction), the stopped frame's caller at the stack pointer the call's caller had, and the same function with the same frames under it (the caller at the return address), is in the same call: another call returning there would have had to run the watched call instruction first. The probe then keeps following the return, and each of the call's hits gets it. The proof is the same for a loop's next pass in that call, so that is followed too (C `sum_to`: each pass's hit now gets 6, where only the last did). It holds only for the step that began at the call's own last stop (a `--set-at`'s stop in the call continues it; one in a call below ends it). Cost: one more stack-pointer read per interrupted step out.
  - **Withheld otherwise, saying only what the stop shows:** where the stopped function is not the call's, `… in a call of another function (the stopped function is not this call's)`; where the frames under the stop are not the call's, `the line stopped again at the same depth in another call (the frames under it are not this call's)` (C: `jump(1)` left by `longjmp`, then main called `jump(2)` from elsewhere); else `… and Peeky could not tell whether in this call (another of the line's breakpoint locations, or a loop's next pass) or in a later call after this one ended without returning`, with the stop where the line has several (`at its breakpoint location 2 of 2`). Java, with no watch, always gets the latter.
  - **`(return)` rows have their hit's stop** (`stop`/`stops` in JSON, the table and `by_hit`; `stop` in the evidence). Evidence schema 15: upgrading a store fills it in for earlier runs from the hit's other rows (every row of a hit comes from one stop), only where each has one and all agree; otherwise (`--capture return` alone, or rows that disagree) it stays NULL. `diff` pairs by the row's own stop, keeping the inference for a row without one, and its note that pairing by hit cannot be checked comes only for such rows.
  - **Tests,** each failing on `main`: C on macOS and Linux (`order`, whose `SWAP` gives one call two stops: both hits 31, at stops 1 and 2, in the evidence too; `jump`'s other call; `sum_to`'s loop), Rust (`format!`'s three stops in each call of `width`), Java (a loop's reason), the store's upgrade, and the proof and the probe's rules on scripted stops (unit).
  - **Review round 1 of PR #198** (the same-call proof sound: no wrong attribution in any case tried; one blocking issue and one wording issue, each with a test that fails on the reviewed head `6b14f50`):
    - **Blocking: the upgrade guessed a stop from contradictory evidence.** A schema-14 hit observed at stop 1 with an after-set row at stop 2 had its `(return)` row placed at stop 1 (`MIN(stop)`), `diff` paired it the same way at query time, and its note did not come, since some stop existed. A stop is now taken from a hit's other rows only where each has one and all agree (`COUNT(DISTINCT stop) = 1`, none NULL), in the upgrade and in `diff`; a `(return)` row left with none pairs with no other run's row at a stop, and `diff`'s note that pairing by hit cannot be checked comes for it.
    - **Wording:** a stop in another function with the same frames under it was said as `the frames under it are not this call's`; it now says `in a call of another function (the stopped function is not this call's)`.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #198** (`_hits_stop`, the upgrade and `diff` correct; one blocking issue, with a test that fails on the reviewed head `a2193dd`): `queries wide` still took `MIN(stop)` for each hit and phase, so `x` at stop 1 and `y` at stop 2 of one hit showed as stop 1, as did a selector captured twice alike at stops 1 and 2 (its cells collapsed), and a hit with a row at no stop showed the other's. Every aggregate of stops now takes one only where each row has one and all agree, else NULL: `wide` and its cells (`join` reads the same cells), and a probe's `by_hit` (whose rows agree by construction). No other `MIN`/`MAX` of a stop is left in `src/peeky`.
- **#202: C caller advice follows a pointer passed on to the frame that owns the struct** (branch `fix/caller-chain`; ninth agent evaluation, C `wrap`). `--set lay.width=16` in `fits(const struct layout *lay, …)` named `wrap.c:40 in wrap`, where `lay` is a pointer parameter too and a change is refused as well. The refusal now follows the calls while each function has one caller and passes its own pointer parameter on as is (same type, never pointed elsewhere: no `lay = …`, `lay++` or `&lay`), to the frame that owns the struct: `fits has one caller: wrap.c:40 in wrap, which passes it on (`lay`, its own pointer parameter); wrap has one caller: wrap.c:75 in main, which passes `&lay`, where `lay.width` is settable: a probe's `--set-at 75@N …``.
  - Settable is claimed only where the existing rules hold at the owner's call and each function on the way uses the pointer only for that call and to read integer fields through it; else the owner's call is named alone (`…, and so may wrap, on the way`). A call's arguments may now take the address of another plain name (`&out`), which runs nothing.
  - Where the calls cannot be followed (a function on the way with several or unknown callers, a pointer pointed elsewhere, kept in a local or computed), no frame is named for the change: `…, which passes it on (`lay`, its own pointer parameter, where a change is refused too), and Peeky cannot single out one call of wrap; change the value in the frame that owns the struct, …: `describe`'s `mutable:` line lists the fields each function can set`. A static or global the caller passes is said to be unchangeable, with a candidate edit as the way.
  - Tests (`tests/native/test_native_caller_chain.py`, each failing on `29728f5`): the chain to `main` and its `--set-at` followed (fits reads the new width; also at a session's stop), a writer on the way (no claim), two callers of `wrap`, a re-pointed `lay`, a kept pointer and a static owner.
  - Review round 1 (PR #211; each test fails on `c98daae`): (blocking) an inline-asm output operand (`__asm__ volatile("" : "=r"(p->width) : "0"(5))`) was read as `p->width`, a read, so the width wrap stores was called settable at `wrap(&x)`: a function on the way, a lent one or the owner with inline assembly is no longer followed or claimed (`wrap has inline assembly, which may change `p` or what it points to`). (blocking) Calls were keyed by source line, so a `static wrap` in a header that `a.c` and `b.c` include, with `b.c`'s `CLOBBER` making its copy write the width, was read from `a.c`'s copy: each unit's calls are now its own, and a function or caller defined in more than one unit gets the general advice. (blocking) AGENT_GUIDE rule 2 said to change the field at the owning call unconditionally: now only where the refusal says `settable`, else its `describe` or candidate-edit advice.
  - Review round 2 (PR #211; the test fails on `4cf3cb3`): (blocking) the owner's scan for `&x` and inline assembly read only the lines of its own file, so `keep(&x);` (keep saving the pointer, which wrap writes through) or an `asm` statement in a header included inside `main` left `x.width` called settable at `wrap(&x)`, though fits read wrap's 5. The owner's whole preprocessed body is scanned now, as the functions on the way and the lent ones already were.
  - Review round 3 (PR #211; each test fails on `825cb2f`): (blocking) `saved = __builtin_addressof(x);` in main escaped the scan for `&x`, so `x.width` was called settable at `wrap(&x)` though wrap wrote it through `saved` first; the same builtin hid an address in a lent function and on the way (`__builtin_addressof(p->width)`). A body with `__builtin_addressof`, `__builtin_frame_address` or `__builtin_stack_address` is now treated like one with inline assembly, and the owner may not name an array in the root without an index (`saved = x.pad`), which is a pointer into it. Audited: offsetof arithmetic and `memcpy(&…)` need a pointer that `&` or such an array gives.
  - Review round 4 (PR #211): `__builtin_dwarf_cfa()` also gives a frame's address with no `&`. Rather than list such builtins, every `__builtin_` now counts except those known to reach no memory (`expect`, `unreachable`, bit counts, the overflow checks, `object_size`, the fortified `__*_chk` copies, whose destination shows as usual).
- **#201: `queries diff` judges hit alignment on values no intervention changed; `join` takes several keys** (branch `fix/diff-alignment`; found by the ninth agent evaluation: wrap, roster and rechnung). `diff` warned that hits do not line up where they did, and `join` took one key.
  - **Only plain values tell:** wrap's `--set-at 75@1 lay.width=16` made `lay.width` 16 against 20 at all 8 hits, which gave `the runs' values differ at 8 of 8 hits`. Values read after a `--set` or `--set-at` in their run on a like name (the same name, a part of it, or what holds it, after the change's capture hit) no longer count toward saying the hits line up, nor toward the count of hits whose values differ. A name does not prove the same variable, so they still count where they line up better at other hits, and the note says an intervention on a like name may account for that. Values a view or a note qualifies count nowhere. After-set rows never counted.
  - **What agrees is said, never over a contradiction:** wrap's fix moved `column` (`run 3's hit 6 matches run 1's hit 7: 14`), and roster's shifted every `width` by one (`run 6's hit 1 matches run 5's hit 2: 6`), while `word` and `title` read the same at every hit. Every selector is checked on its own.
    - Where one lines up better at other hits and others read the same at every hit, the note says both, and that the values do not settle it. Peeky cannot tell which names the call.
    - Where values differ at most hits, the note says that before anything that agrees, and that the values do not show the hits are the same moments.
    - Where nothing lines up better and some values read the same at every hit, with no intervention in either run, the note says the hits line up by them (they tell the hits apart: what `join` by them would pair), or as far as they show (they read alike at some hits, which could still trade places).
    - After an intervention, the note says only what agrees, what differs and which interventions there were (wrap 2/3).
    - With nothing alike, or only constants, the warnings are as before.
  - **The first difference:** where the runs stopped a different number of times, a second note says how many of the rows at a hit come after the first hit that differs (rechnung: hit 4, then 19 of the 20). Every row is kept.
  - **`join --param key=a,b`:** several selectors, split at commas outside brackets and quotes, up to 8, with a column for each. A key value at several hits of a run that the other run has too now gets a note naming them and the rows they make. For rechnung, `key=neu.bezeichnung` made 16 rows from 3 values, and `key=p.bezeichnung,neu.bezeichnung` makes 8, with the note naming hits 1 and 7. `diff`'s notes name `key=<selector>[,<selector>…]`.
  - **Tests,** each failing on `main` 29728f5: the issue's cases cut down from the evaluation's stores (wrap 2 and 3 with the `--set-at`, wrap 1 and 3, roster 5 and 6 and the `--set-at` run, rechnung's 22 rows and its joins), a reorder among alike hits and untouched values that differ, both still warned, and key splitting.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #209** (three blocking issues and one wording issue, all claiming alignment too readily; each fix has a test that fails on the reviewed head `d43a44c`):
    - **A qualified value proved alignment:** `stale_key` 1, 2 (stale) in both runs gave ``the hits line up by `stale_key` `` over `call` A, B against B, A, and `join` paired on it. Only plain values count now, and in `join` a qualified key value matches nothing (unmatched, `caveats` says why).
    - **One value alike at every hit hid a contradiction:** `key` 1, 2 in both runs (a counter, alike by chance, or one that follows from a set value) returned early with "the hits line up by `key`" and never looked at `call`. Every selector is now checked on its own first; where one lines up better at other hits, the note says that and what reads the same, and that the values do not settle it. So wrap 1/3 and roster 5/6 now say both. After any intervention, no note says the hits line up: only what agrees, what differs and which interventions there were.
    - **A later intervention hid an earlier reorder:** `x` A, B, C against B, A, Z, set after run 2's hit 2, was left out at every hit. Only values read after the change (by `interventions.capture_hit`) are left out now; hits 1 and 2's reorder is said.
    - **Wording:** "no value lines up better at other hits" checked single selectors; it now says "no single selector's values".
  - **Review round 2 of PR #209** (`capture_hit` timing, the after-set exclusion and qualified join keys correct; two blocking issues, each with a test that fails on the reviewed head `170ab7f`):
    - **A claim before the majority check:** `key` 1, 2 alike in both runs, with `call` A, B against C, D, gave "the hits line up by `key`". The count of hits whose values differ now comes first: ``…but `call` differs at 2 of them: the values do not show that a hit is the same moment in both``.
    - **Contamination by name alone:** a `--set-at` of `helper`'s local `x` left every hit of `main`'s `x` out, and its reorder (A, B against B, A) went unsaid. Interventions keep their function (and instance), and a like name is no proof of the same variable: a value read after one may hold back a claim that the hits line up, or the count of differing hits, but never a sign that they do not line up. That sign now says an intervention on a like name (``run 2's on `x` in helper``) may account for it.
  - **Review round 3 of PR #209** (intervention handling sound; one blocking issue, with a test that fails on the reviewed head `7f6c749`): selectors were checked one at a time, so a combination could contradict a categorical claim. `key` 1 to 6 was alike and different at every hit; `p` and `q` each lined up best hit by hit and differed at only 3 of 6 hits, but `(p, q)` together paired 4 hits elsewhere against 3. Combinations are still not checked: where two or more selectors differ, the note now says the hits line up by `key` only as far as each selector alone shows, and that how they line up together was not checked.
- **Ninth agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-21-agent-ninth-round.md)): a C line wrapper, a Python session counter, a Java invoice with German names and a Rust table, on `29728f5`; all four agents verified a general fix on their first `verify`, and none saw a wrong value. The Mediums are #201 (done in #209) to #207, and #212 for docs.
- **#208: the ninth evaluation's Lows** (branch `fix/eval9-lows`), each code change with a test that fails on `1f4adc5`:
  - C: a caller frame's loop variable (`i` in a `for` or `while` header) stays withheld while the body calls, with a reason that says Peeky cannot prove the loop has run its initialization and to read it in the loop's body. A withheld local's reason names a `switch` or a `goto` only where Peeky finds the label that can reach the line; otherwise it says Peeky cannot tell. A `char[N]` LLDB shows with `...` past its 1024-byte summary limit, though its text ends at a NUL before that, loses the `...`; a probe row of a long `char[N]` shown as its text no longer carries a children error.
  - CLI: `session get` names on stderr the selectors with no value behind its exit 1; its child hints double-quote a selector with `'` in it; debugpy's grouping note is shown only where nothing else is listed; `--set-hit` past the last captured hit says how to reach it (`--hits`, or `--set-at`); a baseline verify's lone `reason:` line is said plainly; text names files below the working directory relative to it.
  - Python `describe` lists the module globals a function reads (`MAX_GAP (module global)`): names the module binds that the function's compiled code loads.
  - Evidence: `queries outputs` and `diff`'s `(sets)` row give each requested change as `[name, value, "requested at LINE@HIT"]`, a `--set`'s too, ending `, not made: that hit never came` where the probe warned so; `outputs` drops `set_hit`.
  - Docs: AGENT_GUIDE rule 4 says `--set-at` works at several hits of the `--at` line; COOKBOOK 7.3's example keeps the Java after-set row the code keeps; COOKBOOK 4.6 has a tip for testing identity.
  - Left to the open PRs: rule 2 and C's `settable here` (#211), Java's `final` checks, boxed numbers and `settable here` (#210), Rust type spellings (#205); Python reports no settability, so its refusals list none.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #214** (items 1, 3, 4, 7, 11, 12 and 16 clean; three blocking issues and one non-blocking, each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `6ad3d65`):
    - Reading a caller's `for` counter showed leftovers where a macro hides a call in the initializer (`int i = INIT`) or a `goto` jumps into the body: reverted, with the clearer reason above.
    - The same text check (`Statements._settled`) let frame 0 read a one-line loop's `i` part-way through its header, back from `INIT`'s call with `step --out`: a leftover (`1`), on `main` too. A header's variable now counts as set by no call only where its initializer names nothing (literals and operators: any name may be a macro that calls, such as a library's `errno`).
    - `outputs` and `diff` said where each change "applied", though `runs.sets` holds requests, a change whose hit never came included: now "requested at", with "not made" from the probe's warning.
    - `describe` took `globals` for a module global from `globals()["x"] = 1` and counted names in code the compiler drops (`if False:`): it now takes only names a target binds, and reads from the compiled code's global loads, running nothing.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #214** ("requested" against "made" truthful; one blocking issue and two non-blocking, each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `8f92730`):
    - A one-line loop's header variable still read as live mid-line, back from a call in its body (`step --out`), where the header sets nothing (`for (int i; …)`) or a `goto` into the body skips `int k = 0`: leftovers (`1`). Its own declarator now needs an initializer, and no label a jump can reach past the declaration may follow it in its block (as `jumped_past` judges one: a `case` of a `switch` around it, or a label any `goto` names); an `#include`, a macro that may hold a label, or a block that never closes leaves it withheld.
    - `for (int i = 0, j = 1; …)` took `j` for a name in `i`'s initializer: each declarator is judged with those before it.
    - `describe` missed globals loaded only in a generator expression (a comprehension too, before Python 3.12), compiled as code of its own: their loads count for the enclosing function.
  - **Review round 3 of PR #214** (computed gotos, nested labels, enclosing switches, includes, macros and `setjmp` handled; two blocking issues, each with a unit test and a real build that fail on the reviewed head `e050c39`):
    - In C++, `for (X j = 0, *i = nullptr; …)` runs `X::X(int)` before `i` is set; back from it, `i` read as a leftover pointer and was followed (`i.v = -170737344`). In C++ only a header's first declarator counts as set by no call; C keeps each judged with those before it.
    - A GNU `asm goto` lists its labels as operands, so no `goto inside` was seen and a one-line loop's `k` read as live mid-line. Like a computed `goto`, one anywhere in the source makes any label a jump target.
- **#205: `&str` loop items read, and Rust hints name the right length** (branch `fix/rust-str-items`; the ninth evaluation's roster). In `for cell in cells` over a `&[&str]`, `cell` (a `&&str`, LLDB's `&str *`) showed only its pointer, so the agent decoded `"Renée"` by hand; `cells`' warning said `Capture .len` (a slice's is `length`) without naming `cells`; and `names.len` on a `Vec<&str>` past its last use was refused with 900 characters about a Box.

Past that it shows its pointer, with a short reason naming it (#110: a call or drop may have come since `cell` was last used).
  - A `Vec<&str>` past its last use keeps its own fields (`names.len` reads) where the MIR proves it initialized; the pointers inside it show only their address. A `Vec` behind a reference is not one (`&mut Vec<Res>` stays `[unproven]`).

A container of `&str` says its items read where the code binds one (`for cell in cells`: `cell`).

- **#206: a namesake out of scope no longer withholds, and `describe` lists each binding** (branch `fix/rust-str-items`; the ninth evaluation's roster).   - The declaration a field read uses is the binding in scope at the stop, from the MIR's scopes (`_in_scope`), as the root's own reads were; where the MIR cannot single one out, the refusal stands.
  - A `String`, `Vec` or `&str` field reads through a reference local where #110 shows its target standing, in the innermost frame (`entry.name` → `"Renée"`); before, only through reference parameters and owned locals.
  - `describe` lists a name the function binds more than once by each binding's selector, with its own type and lines (`points @ main.rs:73 (Vec<String>, lines 74-85)`, `points @ main.rs:81 (&str *, line 82)`), and its `mutable:` line names them so (`bind::shadow: x @ main.rs:6 (line 4)`). The worker gives a row lldb-dap lists by its plain name its `declared_at` (`points @ main.rs:73`), so that selector reads it at any stop where it is in scope.
  - Tests (each fails on `main`): `entry.name`, `entry.city` and `entry.points` on line 82 over two hits, `describe`'s four entries, and `points @ main.rs:73` on line 76.
  - Review (PR #215, round 1; no blocking issue): a selector's identity is a file's name and a line, so two bindings sharing both (`let x = n + 1; let x = format!("{x}!");`, or files of one name in two directories) were one apparent selector (`x (String or usize, lines 3-6)`). `describe` now lists them as `x @ main.rs:3 (2 bindings on this line: not selectable by line)`, counting bindings, not DIEs, and leaves them out of `mutable:`; the selector is refused with that reason, where lldb-dap lists both by it and where it lists one by its plain name (`declared_at_shared`). Tests (each fails on 8b009e1): a real build (`describe`, the refusal, `n` still read), and the plain-name case (unit).
