- **#222: a deferred `format!` assignment is the line's own, and refusals name other files' lines** (branch `fix/rust-macro-line`). After `let label: String; label = format!("sum {sum}");`, line 5's first stop withheld `label` (`no value yet`) though its slot held `"sum 6"`, and refused `--set sum=7`, citing "line 113" (of std's `alloc/src/macros.rs`).
  - **Cause:** `format!`'s last call (`_3 = must_use(move _4)`, spanned in `alloc/src/macros.rs`, inlined there with its call site on line 4) returns into the MIR block of line 5's `println!`. A call from another file was taken for the line's on any line that may invoke a macro, so its return (the block's start, before `label = move _3`) was a place of line 5.
  - **Fix:** such a call counts only where the IR does not put its block's code (its own, or code inlined there, by its call site) on other lines of the file and none on this one (`IrFunction.code_lines`). An IR block it does not name, or one with no line of the file, still counts. Line 5's stop 1 now reads `"sum 6"` and takes `--set sum=7`.
  - **Messages:** a copy's line in another file is named with it (`at alloc/src/macros.rs:113`), and a statement under way at a line's start advises a stop before that statement starts (an earlier line, or an earlier stop of the line with `--set-hit`), never the breakpoint it stopped at.
  - **Tests** (each fails on `main` 2f6d707): the issue's crate (`label` read at every stop, `sum` set at stop 1), and a statement spanning two lines, whose refusal names `alloc/src/macros.rs` and advises an earlier stop.
  - **Review round 1 (PR #230; two blocking issues, each failing open):**
    - **Partial IR evidence counted as conclusive:** a block's lines dropped calls with no `!dbg`, line 0 or an unresolved `inlinedAt` chain, so a call with no location beside line-4 code read as `{4}`. Every IR instruction is now kept with where it is, or that it is unknown (`provenance`), and `call_lines` takes the MIR terminator's call (the block's last call and what follows it, or the whole block where the callee is inlined): its return leaves the line only where all of that is located and none is on it.
    - **Files compared by name alone:** `_file` read only `DIFile.filename`, so `mod.rs` in `/w/src/a` and in `/w/src/b` were one. A file is now its directory and name as one path, and a source is the program's only under the build's directory (its compile units').
    - **Tests** (each fails on a76cac7): the issue's MIR and IR, with an unlocated instruction, a line-0 site and an unresolved chain each keeping the place, and with a located call leaving it (unit); two `mod.rs` DIFiles in two directories (unit); and a real build with `src/a/mod.rs` and `src/b/mod.rs`, each with the issue's pattern (a guard: rustc names them apart already).
  - **Review round 2 (PR #230; one blocking issue):** `call_lines` took the block's last LLVM call for the MIR terminator's, so an unlocated real call followed by a located helper call and branch on line 4 read as `{4}`; and an `invoke`'s result, stored in its normal successor, was not looked at. The rule is now the simplest fail-closed one: the whole IR block, every instruction located and none on the stop line, and no `invoke` ending it. It still places #222's real case (the inlined `must_use` and its branch, all at line 4's site). Tests (each fails on e938b1d): the unlocated call before a located `memcpy` call, and an `invoke` (unit).
- **#67 (`0a1fad7`): CUDA phase 0** for #61. [ADR 0007](adr/0007-cuda-gdb-dap.md) (proposed) drives `cuda-gdb -i=dap`. `scripts/cuda/qualify.py` is the qualification to run on a GPU host.
  - Found by experiment: upstream GDB 14.2, 15.1 and 16.3 start the program at different DAP requests. One order works on all three (breakpoints, `launch`, then `configurationDone`).
  - The new `gdb-dap` lane keeps that order tested against GDB 15.1; 14.2 and 16.3 were run by hand.

- **#255: a raw pointer inside a value shows only its address** (branch `fix/rust-nested-raw-pointer`; found by the round-2 review of #253). In a live struct or tuple the MIR proves initialized, `h.p` (a `*const i64` field) listed its pointee, which safe code can free: `session get h.p` showed `*p = 777` from a reused block after `drop(b)`. A raw pointer field, tuple or array element, `NonNull`'s pointer or fat raw pointer's address now shows only its address, `[unproven]`, with a note. Its Rust type in the build's debug information tells it from a reference (both LLDB's `long *`), and the local's MIR type decides where that says nothing. A raw pointer local that #110 cannot judge (an optimized build) shows only its address too. Review: every raw pointer's note now says Peeky never follows one, because nothing proves what it points at still stands; a local's had said a reference's #110 reason, and a field's that it may be moved or dropped. An array of raw pointers local lists its elements, each its address, instead of collapsing.
- **#249: every remote source compared** (branch `fix/remote-all-sources`, stacked on #251). Protocol 2: `version` says which sources a client hashes (Python: every `.py` under the root; Java, C, C++, Rust: the trusted config's declared files); the client sends `expect_inputs`, and the worker refuses with `PROVENANCE_CHANGED` naming the first file that differs or is on one side only. The client also requires `inputs_checked` and a matching `inputs_sha256`, or discards the result. Request limit 64 KiB → 4 MiB. Review fixes: a missing `inputs_sha256` or an empty manifest is refused; compiled builds are compared again before the debugger starts; resumed remote sessions recheck protocol 2; remote Python sessions recheck their `.py` files around every operation (`wait` included) and refuse stack frames in uncompared files under the root; only virtual environments are left out; remote runs use a fresh bytecode cache; the client rehashes its own checkout after every answer; every remote Python result carries the not-compared note.
- **#252: C, C++ and Rust on a separate host** (branch `test/native-separate-host`). The remote e2e Dockerfile gains a `native` target (LLVM 18 from Debian 13's signed packages; Rust 1.96.0 through rustup-init 1.29.2, checked against its published SHA-256 per architecture, as `scripts/ci_linux` now is too; installed before Peeky, so a source change doesn't rebuild it). `tests/remote` runs, per language through the real `ssh -L` tunnel, a probe with `--set`, a session, and refusals of a changed `main` file and a changed header or module, and records the tool versions. Path binding: remote results named worker-host paths. The worker now marks, for a request with `expect_inputs`, each location and source path that is a compared file (under the root, no `..`, through no link) as `source:NAME`; the client shows its own copy only of a name it hashed that is still a regular file under its root, and marks every other path `worker:` (review of #268: prefix mapping claimed `..`, links, venvs and uncompared files). This changes protocol 2's responses, so the protocol is 3 (round 2): a client and a worker of different protocols refuse each other, the client declaring `protocol` with `expect_inputs`, and a session record from another protocol is refused. Frames and breakpoints show the user's source where the build's copy is unchanged; a candidate's edited file keeps the copy's path, with a note, since its lines are the candidate's. A refused program is named by its path under the root. Warm module 19.8 s; cold image builds 94 s locally.
- **#263: `queries diff`, `set_effect` and `join` readable** (branch `fix/queries-readability`; found by the tenth agent evaluation: outline, debounce, kitty and runoff). Text tables show a long value from 40 characters before where the pair first differs, one offset for both values, with `…` at every cut (`diff`, `compare`; `set_effect`'s values and each change in `interventions`), so outline's `level` 1 against 0 shows; `--json` keeps values whole. `diff`'s `(sets)` row shows a count and the first change (debounce's 70 effect rows went from 214 to 138 columns), and intervention rows now come before real differences. The alignment check leaves out of what agrees every selector that differs after an edit or an intervention, which may follow from it (debounce's `db.stable`, outline's candidate), and says so. Where such a selector's values line up better at other hits, the note says the change may account for it, and never that the hits line up (review: `call` A, B, C against B, A, C after an edit had given "the hits line up by `key`"). With a selector left out, the note says what pairing by number amounts to, not that the hits are the same moments. Agreement reads "agrees between the runs at each of the N hits"; `join` is named only where the captured selectors tell each run's hits apart. `join`'s text table leaves out selectors that agree at every paired hit, with a note (`--param unchanged=1` keeps them; `--json` has every column): runoff's went from 430 to 210 columns. A canned query names its default runs once (`evidence.prepare`) and reads its rows, notes and text table in one read transaction (`evidence.canned`): the store keeps SQLite's rollback journal, so a probe saving meanwhile, or evicting run 1 at the 500-run limit, waits for it instead of silencing a note; should a named run still go, a note says what could not be checked.
- **#261: a Java `[settable]` mark promises only what a write does** (branch `fix/java-settable-mark`; tenth agent evaluation, Java `runoff`). `session get ranking` marked `ranking[1] [settable]`, then `set` was refused ("the array is in a list held by java.util.Collections$UnmodifiableList.list"), naming `'1'`, with a hint that ended "rows marked [settable] can be changed here"; `counted` and `left` showed neither mark nor reason. The launcher now answers `peekyArrayHolder` (marker `peekyArrayHolders`): before Peeky marks an array's elements it runs a write's search of what refers to the array (at most 0.5 s, once per array and listing), and a held element reads `[not settable: its array is in a list held by … (the JDK's own state)]`, or why the launcher can't tell (too many referrers); where the search runs out of time, or an older bundle can't search, it reads `[maybe settable: …]` (`maybe_settable`), and `set` decides. A line breakpoint stops only its own thread, so while another thread runs no element is promised (`[maybe settable: other threads are running; set checks again]`) and no verdict is kept past one listing (review: a background thread wrapped the array's list after a `get`, which still said `[settable]`); only a verdict made with every thread suspended (`allSuspended`) lasts the stop. A change to an element suspends every thread across the launcher's check and java-debug's write, and resumes them before its reply; where that resume fails other than by a disconnect (review round 2), the launcher disconnects from the JVM (which resumes every thread), says why on stderr, and ends, so the change fails and the session closes with that reason instead of leaving an attached JVM suspended. A Java row of a type `set` changes says why it isn't marked (`not read again after this stop`, `a constant javac compiles into its uses`, `declared Object, not String`, `final`, `in a caller's frame`). Field and element refusals name the whole selector (`ranking[1]`), and a write the debugger refuses gets a hint that promises nothing of a mark. Cost: 36 hits capturing two elements, 1.12-1.23 s on main to 1.43-1.47 s (max pause 29-30 ms to 40-43 ms), about 3.5 ms per search.
- **#264: Java `describe` gives a block's local its lines** (branch `fix/java-block-lines`; tenth agent evaluation, Java `runoff`). `names in Runoff.count (app/Runoff.java:43-79): ... b (int), left (int), ranking (int[]), lowest (int), c (int)` listed `b`, `ranking` and `c` once, with no lines, though each is declared in two loops. A name not declared once for the rest of the method (one LocalVariableTable entry running to the end of the code) now shows every declaration's lines, as C, C++ and Rust show block lines (COOKBOOK 1.1): `b (int, lines 50-53, 77-79)`, `ranking (int[], lines 51-53, 78-79)`, `c (int, lines 60-66, 70-71)` (`--json`: the scope's `spans`). A line counts where its first instruction, which java-debug binds a line breakpoint to, is in the declaration's scope: never the declaring line or a `for` header, and none for a one-line block (`x (int, no breakpoint line)`, `[]`). A name declared with different types is listed once per type (`value (int, line 31), value (String, line 34)`; `--json`: `typed_spans`, every type, `spans` maybe empty). Review: the first version counted a line if any of its instructions was in scope, and a name with one declaration to the method's end lost every other declaration's lines; a real probe at each listed and unlisted line now checks `describe`.
- **#259: a reference made into a raw pointer for `ptr::eq` reads on that line** (branch `fix/rust-ptr-use`; tenth evaluation, kitty). At `if ptr::eq(f, e.payer)`, `f.name` was withheld with "(#110: a call or drop may have come since `f` was last used)". Making `f` a raw pointer was counted as its use; the line breakpoint may be past `ptr::eq`, which LLVM inlines even at opt-level 0, and any call there ended the proof. A call of `std::ptr::eq` or `addr_eq`, which only compares addresses, no longer does, where the crate names no `std`, `core` or `addr_eq` of its own; the raw pointer is still no use of `f`. Where the proof fails, the note now says why it did: a value not yet assigned, the Drop window, or the calls and drops that may have run since the last use, with their lines ("the stop may be past a call on this line" for one on the stop's own line). Rust notes and refusals, and `sql --schema`, print no issue numbers. Not changed: kitty's `payer @ main.rs:47` at line 58 is past its last use with calls since (lines 54 to 56), so it stays withheld, now saying so; reading it would need the frozen-owner proof to follow `ok_or_else`'s closure. Review (PR #272): `make_mod!(std)` declared a `mod std` whose `ptr::eq` took the Box and dropped it, dumped `std::ptr::eq` as the real one, and `f.name` read freed memory as `""`. Now a call counts only on two raw pointer locals, and only where no function in the crate's MIR dump is named `ptr::eq` or `addr_eq` (any prefix), and no macro is passed `std` or `core` but as a path's start, nor an `extern crate` renamed so.
- **#262: a session step says when its stop is in another function or depth, or past a block, and `session start` warns when it stopped at a moved breakpoint** (branch `fix/session-step-notes`, tenth agent evaluation: outline 2, runoff 2, prep 12). At a `session step`'s own end (reason `step`), its stack, read whole up to 256 frames (`totalFrames` must match), is compared with the one it started from, by depth and callers so recursion counts: ``note: left `rename`; now in `run` ``, ``now in `walk`, one call deeper, called from `walk` ``, or ``left `add`; now in `shift` ``, and `get`'s place note adds ``after a step from `rename` ``; JSON `call_change` (`left`, `deeper`, `other`) on the state and on `get`'s rows with no value. After such a step in the same function at its depth, `get` of a local whose block ended says `'ranking' was visible at line 79, earlier in this same call, but the block declaring it has ended` (Java, whose steps prove the call), or that `this stop is outside its scope` (C, C++, Rust: LLDB's step runs on to a later call when an exception or `longjmp` ends its own); from the names an earlier inspection listed, since reading every stop's locals is not cheap. `session start` warns as a probe does when it stopped at a breakpoint the debugger moved (`continue --to` already refuses a moved line). Review (PR #274): stacks are not call identities (two activations at one depth under callers at the same lines look alike), so a `continue`, or a step a breakpoint, exception or pause stopped first, gets no note (a `continue` from one recursive `f(0)` to another said "entered `f`, called from `f`"); the wording says only what the stacks show, never "returned" (a generator at `yield`, a C++ throw); no warning without a stop at the moved line; and the stack read is bounded (`levels: 0` on 20,000 frames passed the 4 MiB message limit and closed the session). Tests: `tests/unit/test_session_calls.py`, Python in `tests/integration/test_cli.py`, Java in `tests/java/test_java_cli.py`, C in `tests/native/test_native_session_calls.py`, C++ in `tests/cpp/test_cpp_session_calls.py`, each failing on `db418fe`; the review's on `38f84bc`.
- **#260: the elements of a slice or `Vec` of structs with a `String` field read** (branch `fix/rust-slice-structs`; tenth evaluation, Rust `kitty`). In `settle(friends: &mut [Friend], …)` (`Friend { name: String, paid: i64, owes: i64 }`), a live parameter, `friends[0].name` said "read it as a &Vec or &[T] parameter", which it was, and `friends.data_ptr.name` said the debug information gave `friends` no type it points at. A `Vec` or slice of tuples or crate structs now reads element by element wherever each has an integer, bool, float, char or `String` field, under the container's own proofs: a `String` field's text from memory (its layout checked as `alloc::string::String`; at most 16 texts per container, each a read of its own, a later one `not read:` with its length), and any other field `not read:` with its type and what to do, left out of the element's value (`Expense { cents: 4500, .. }`, `(_, 3)`). Wide elements show those that fit one 4096-byte read, where the whole container was `<unverified …>`. A raw slice or `&Vec` of structs is never told to be read as one; a proven container of enums or packed structs says why; a raw slice's `data_ptr` path says to select `friends[0]`; a closure's captured `&str` says Peeky does not judge a capture and where to read it; a `Vec<&str>` the line moves keeps its raw fields, `[stale]`, with the MIR's reason instead of the Box note; a standard-library type's field (`sharers.len`) is no longer marked with another binding's register; and an index into an owned `String` or a `String` field (`text[0]`) says the text is shown whole, not that it is "beyond the debugger's visible window". Tests: unit (layouts, decoding, notes, the field walk) and `tests/rust/test_rust_slice_structs.py` (the kitty crate), each failing on main.
  - Review (PR #273): in a caller frame, a `&mut Vec<T>` that `retain_mut` was changing showed `friends.len  3` with no note, and the note said to capture it for the length (on main too). A `&mut` reference to a `Vec`, `String` or slice in a caller frame (a parameter or a local, by its IR or MIR type), and a container field behind a caller's `&mut` parameter, now keep their raw fields `[stale]`, each with the reason carried from what holds it ("read through `friends`: …"), and no note offers a length a call may be changing (nor for an owned `Vec` or `String` in a caller frame); a shared one still reads.
  - Review round 2 (PR #273): in `main`'s frame during `retain_mut`, the owned `friends`' note said it may be mid-change, but `friends.len` selected alone read `3` with no note and no `[stale]`. Wherever an owned `Vec` or `String` (a local, a composite `Vec<T>`, or one in a struct field) is left raw because a call under way may be changing it, its own fields now show `[stale]` with that reason carried, as a caller's `&mut` one does; #108's rule for the other fields of a struct lent `&mut` (unmarked) is unchanged. A `String` whose bytes are not UTF-8 is withheld with that reason, never shown in part. A compiled crate's reordered generic struct (padding, a ZST, `Option<String>`, `Box<str>`, a nested struct, `char`, `u128`) checks the real layout against the program's own `{:?}`.
  - Review round 3 (PR #273): the reason said "the call under way may be changing it" also for a caller's owned `Vec` the call never took. Every caller-frame reason now says what Peeky knows: "`v` is a local of a caller frame: Peeky doesn't check whether it was lent to the call under way, which may be changing it, so its fields aren't trusted here". `[stale]` is now defined with both meanings (leftovers, or a caller's container that may be mid-change; the note says which) in rust.md, rust-cookbook.md, AGENT_GUIDE, COOKBOOK and `sql --schema`.
- **Tenth agent evaluation** ([report](evaluations/2026-10-22-agent-tenth-round.md)): a C button debouncer, a Rust trip kitty with non-ASCII names, a Java runoff count and a Python outliner, on `db418fe`, each agent given its language's cookbook as a fifth doc; all four agents verified a general fix on their first `verify`, and none saw a wrong value. Only one mentioned the docs' length, as a Low (three of four in the ninth round). The Mediums are #259 to #264 (done in #269 to #274); #265 and #266 are enhancements.
- **#267: the tenth evaluation's Lows** (branch `fix/eval10-lows`), each code change with a test that fails on `026d317`: a failed `session get`'s stderr line comes after its rows (stdout flushed first); a repeated `visible:` list says `as listed at hit N` (`as listed above` in `session get`); a long `--hits all` table points at `queries wide`; an index named by a variable (`self.items[at]`) says to capture it first; a value at the inspection's depth limit says why it lists no children; Python's field refusal prints as other languages' do (`error [MUTATION_REJECTED]:`), with a hint that names no `[settable]` rows; a refused change through a reference or pointer gets the call advice only where it is proven a parameter at the stop (Rust: the MIR's binding in scope; C, C++: the declaration that applies there), a local says no call passed it, a Rust slice item says to edit the source, and anything else names no call; Rust `describe` leaves out the `?` operator's `residual` and `val`, told by their MIR spans ending right after the `?`. Now only a pointer proven a parameter at the stop gets it; Rust picks the innermost binding declared above the line where several are in scope, and C and C++ the declaration that applies at the stop, `p @ file:line` too; same-named declarations are never pooled. A local's advice says to change what it points at only if that is a root local in some frame; a `for` item of a standard slice iterator (MIR: `std::slice::Iter(Mut)::next`, in a crate whose `std` paths are the standard library's) is told to edit the source. The `?` spans are described exactly: both end right after the `?`, and only `residual`'s is the `?` alone.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #276** (two blocking issues, one doc fix; each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `c996e75`): a parameter pointed elsewhere before the stop (Rust `p = &GLOBAL;` or a write through `&mut p`; C and C++ `p = &global;`, and in C++ `aim(p)` with `void aim(Acc *&)`) was still called a parameter, with call advice. Now Rust needs an argument local the MIR never assigns, whole or in part, nor takes by `&mut` or raw pointer; C and C++ read the body as each unit preprocesses it (`_uses`): no `p = …`, `op=`, `++`/`--`, `&p`, inline assembly or address builtin, and in C++ no mention but `p->…`, `p[…]` or `*p`. A crate's own `trait Iterator` implemented for `std::slice::Iter` is dumped `<std::slice::Iter<…> as Iterator>::next` too: a slice element now also needs `_std_iterator()`. The Rust cookbook says a local binding was not passed into the function as a parameter, not that it came from no call.
  - **Review round 3 of PR #276** (one blocking issue, with a test that fails on the reviewed head `3de4776`): Rust `asm!("…", inout(reg) p)` writes the parameter `p`, but its MIR shows only an operand (`inout(reg) copy _1 => _1`), so `p` kept the call advice. Any `asm!` in the body, or a body Peeky doesn't fully read, now leaves a pointer parameter unproven, as in C and C++.
- **#266: heap addresses compared across runs** (branch `feat/sql-addresses`; tenth evaluation, kitty). Each run's heap starts elsewhere, so kitty's agent matched the payers' raw pointers to the slots after the sort by `substr(value, -3)`, which held only because both buffers started at `…800`. The new SQL function `address(value)` is a value's first `0x` token outside quoted text (1 to 16 hex digits) as an INTEGER, else NULL: a null pointer, a string's text and a Java object have none. At or above 2^63 it is the two's complement, so two such addresses still subtract and order exactly. COOKBOOK 4.8 and rust-cookbook 4.8 join runs on each address minus a base each run has (`MIN(address(value)) OVER (PARTITION BY run_id, selector)`, or the first hit's): on kitty, 4500 at offset 0 goes to Bo, 1000 at 80 to Zoë and 900 at 40 to Chloé. No canned query: the base, and what pairs the runs, depend on the program. `sql --schema` lists the function; values are stored and shown as before. Tests: unit (each value format; both cookbooks' SQL on synthetic runs) and `tests/rust/test_rust_slice_structs.py` (rust-cookbook 4.8's SQL on two real kitty runs), each failing on `a234283`.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #277** (two blocking issues, one non-blocking; each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `b81a8b8`): the recipe's CTE filtered only `run_id`, so `observed`, `after-set` and `returned` rows were pooled for the base and the join (run a observed `0x100000000` then `0x100000028` after a change, run b `0x200000028` then `0x200000000`: they cross-paired). Both cookbooks' SQL now keeps `observed` rows and joins on `phase`, and COOKBOOK 4.8 says how to keep one `stop` location per run (`AND (run_id != :a OR stop = 2)`). `address()` had reused `no_addresses`' rule of 9 or more significant digits, so a non-PIE x86-64 `brk` heap pointer (`0x4052a0`) was NULL; it now reads any nonzero token of 1 to 16 hex digits (`0xffffffffffffffff` is -1), and `no_addresses` is unchanged. The docs say it is lexical: in `{x = 0x1234567890, p = 0x…}` it reads `x`, so call it on a pointer's own selector.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #277** (one non-blocking issue, with a test that fails on the reviewed head `603c912`): with `0xffffffffffffffff` read as -1, `MIN(address(value))` took a sentinel (`MAP_FAILED`, `usize::MAX`) as each run's base and paired two sentinel hits at offset 0, while no real pointer paired. Both recipes now keep `address(value) > 0`, which skips values that read negative; COOKBOOK 4.8 says to drop it and take a known element's address as the base to compare addresses at or above 2^63.
- **#265: `--set-at LINE@all`** (branch `feat/set-at-all`), with tests that fail on `a234283`: a change at every stop of a line, each made, checked and read back as one `LINE@N` is, up to 100 stops (the `--hits all` cap); one `@all` is one of the 8 changes. A refusal at any stop ends the probe, naming that hit; a line that never stops, or stops past the cap, is reported (`unapplied`, with `made` for the cap) and exits 4; `--until hits` is not kept running by it, and says so. The text shows 3 changes and counts the rest; JSON has each, with `"all": true`. Evidence keeps one `interventions` row per stop and `[name, value, "LINE@all"]` in `runs.sets`; `diff`'s `(sets)` counts requests, and more than 3 alike `intervention only` rows are one. Debounce, the motivating case, can't use it: line 36 is in `settle`, where `db` is a pointer, and `main`'s line 80 runs for every sample. The refusal now says `80@all` is another experiment and points to a candidate edit, the recipe COOKBOOK 2.8 gives; Java changes an eligible program-owned instance field through a local, parameter or `this` reference, so `@all` works there as is.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #278** (one blocking issue, two non-blocking; each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `eb48b86`): comparisons could hide how far an `@all` got. A run refused at hit 7, two runs refused at different hits, and a run `--until hits` ended after one change read like a run that changed every stop, since `compare` showed no status, `diff` showed one only where the statuses differed, and `(sets)` said only `requested at LINE@all`. Each probe now gives `set_outcomes` (one per requested change: `made`, `ended`, `complete`), kept in `runs.set_outcomes` (schema 16). `outputs`, `compare` and `diff` say how each went (`, made at 6 stops; refused at hit 7`, `, made at 1 stop before --until hits ended the probe`), and `diff` and `compare` get a `(status)` row, with each run's error, wherever either run didn't complete; `compare` also gets a `(sets)` row, and the `(sets)` text shows the first change that differs. Wording: AGENT_GUIDE rule 2 is scoped to C/C++ and Rust (Java can change an eligible program-owned field through a reference) and rule 4 says `@all` is each stop while the probe runs, at most 100; ERRORS.md says a capped `@all`'s run is partially intervened; the `--until hits` warning says any later execution of the line ran without the change. The decision not to write a caller's frame from a callee's stop stands.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #278** (one blocking issue; each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `e04ce55`): runs saved before schema 16 lost or overstated their `@all` outcomes. A capped one read plain `requested at 9@all`, a never-stopped one "that hit never came", and `compare` called two such runs with equal requests "the same interventions, made alike", which schema 15 can't show. The warning-based text is back for them (`, made only at the first 100 stops: the cap`, `, not made: its line never stopped`), also for an entry that is no outcome; and two runs whose requests read alike are made alike only where both have valid outcomes for every request. Otherwise `compare` and `diff` show a `(sets)` row: "the same requested interventions; how they were applied wasn't recorded for one or both older runs".
  - **Review round 3 of PR #278** (two blocking issues; each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `5b3f20e`): a schema-15 run against a schema-16 one with the same request said "the runs requested different interventions", since `(sets)` compared the texts with their outcomes before anything else. Requests are now told apart by themselves first; where they are the same and either run did not record how they went, the note is that it wasn't recorded. And an outcome was read without checking it against its request, so `[{"made": 2, "ended": "refused", "hit": 3}]` for a `9@all` read "refused at that hit" and counted as recorded. Each outcome must now match its `runs.sets` entry: every key, of its type (`made` a non-negative int, `ended` a known end, `hit` where the end needs it, `complete` a boolean that agrees), and the request's option, location, name and value. Otherwise the run's changes read as its warnings did, and it counts as unrecorded.
- **#279: a stop before a function's prologue stores its parameters withholds them** (branch `fix/gcc-entry-stop`; found by the #167 GCC survey), with tests that fail on `da1105a`: GCC marks no prologue end, so LLDB stopped a one-line function (and a one-line C++ lambda) at its first instruction, and a `step --into` stopped there too; `a` read the slot's leftovers (`a = 2` for `one(3)`) with no note, and `set` was refused as "part-way through" the statement. Each function's prologue now runs from its entry to its first `prologue_end` row, or in a build with none to its second row, as GDB reads GCC's; there, every parameter and local but a `static`, one a location list places or one passed on the caller's stack is `no value yet`, saying to read the arguments at the call or put the body on a line of its own (a multi-line function, which stops there more rarely: on a later line), and a change says the stop is before the prologue stores the parameters. Withheld rather than moved past the prologue: it covers steps and any stop there, with lldb-dap 18 and 20, without resuming the program. Clang, Apple clang and rustc mark the end after the parameters' stores (they carry no source location), so they are unaffected.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #280** (one blocking issue, two non-blocking; the fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `c436317`): a module with no `addressRange` (which a frame's `moduleId` allows) left no stop's address to place, so the prologue rule failed open and `one(3)`, `one(4)` read `65535`, `3`; a frame on a line of a GCC prologue (one with no `prologue_end`) now withholds its parameters and locals, but what the prologue leaves alone, saying Peeky cannot tell whether the stop is past the prologue and why, while clang builds are unchanged. `scripts/ci_linux/run_lanes.sh` gains `c-gcc-llvm20` (the GCC-dependent `tests/native` files with GCC and lldb-dap 20; 28 passed, 1 skipped, 33 s), and the C++ lambda test says it waits for #167.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #280** (one blocking issue, with a test that fails on the reviewed head `7b9bc38`): without the load address, only lines of GCC's unmarked prologues were withheld, but a fault or signal can stop inside a marked (clang) prologue on its function's line, with the parameter stale. Every window's lines now count, and the note says Peeky cannot tell whether the stop is before or after the prologue stores the parameters, as it cannot place the stop's instruction; clang builds lose reads only on such a line, in that degraded mode.
- **#167 (part): what LLDB misreads in GCC's C++ is withheld or said** (branch `fix/gcc-cpp-containers`; survey items A1, B1 and its notes), with tests that fail on `06a9978` under the test-only hatch: an unordered container whose LLDB count differs from its own `_M_element_count` (GCC's, read as `size=0 {}` with `um[1]` "would insert"; also clang's typedef'd one on LLDB 18) is `not listed`, in rows, previews, keys, positions, listings and the evidence; a `std::string` set takes `_M_local_buf`'s length from the debug information (LLDB reads `char[0]`), and without `writeMemory` its refusal says LLDB misreads the buffer; LLDB 18's `error: <module> 0x…: DW_TAG_…` lines on its own stderr are a warning and `debugger_diagnostics`, not `output`; and a bitfield's structured binding with no location says why no stop reads or changes it.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #281** (two blocking issues, two non-blocking; each fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `faa5086`): a count LLDB reads right did not prove its entries right, as `WeirdAlloc<double, int>` showed `ws[#0]` as the double `3.4584595208887258E-323`; a non-empty unordered container is now listed only where the type LLDB's provider decodes (its version's rule: the 4th or 5th argument's first, or the last's) is the hashtable's `_Value`, aligned where it reads (clang's `long double` read `size=1 {0}`, LLDB 18's `std::unordered_map<int, Asset>` decoded as a set); `local_buffer` gave `char[64]` in a 16-byte union, and now requires the sizes to add up; the diagnostics warning says a program line of that exact shape would be moved too; and test_cpp_cli accepts the bitfield refusal only for GCC.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #281** (one blocking issue, with a test that fails on the reviewed head `9075773`): the entry's place was proven by `_Hash_node_base`'s size, while LLDB skips the size of `_M_nxt`'s type, so an 8-byte `_M_nxt` in a 16-byte base with a 16-aligned value passed while LLDB read 8 bytes in; `_M_nxt` must now be a pointer at offset 0, the base exactly its size (the pointer's `DW_AT_byte_size`, kept in the type graph, else 8), and the value's alignment must divide that.
- **#167, part 2 (survey items A2 and B3): GCC's lambdas and implicit constructors are placed, and a C++ frame Peeky cannot place withholds its locals** (branch `fix/gcc-cpp-functions`), with tests that fail on `06a9978`: GCC gives a lambda's `operator()` and an unnamed class's `<constructor>` no file or line, so they had no entry in the function table and no rule ran in them (`x = 281474976709584` before `long x = a * 2;` ran; `this.t = 20943` in the constructor still building it), and a lambda's own parameter was judged by its enclosing function's declarations (`t` withheld as the structured binding). First, a safety net: a C++ frame on the program's lines whose function has no entry withholds every local and parameter as `not shown`, saying Peeky cannot place the function's declarations; with clang that is a function a `#line` declares in another file, as bison writes, which showed leftovers too. Then such members take their class's file and line, as clang gives them; `<constructor>` is a constructor's name, and a generic lambda's `operator()<int>` or a closure GCC folded away is a lambda's (with clang too, `main`'s locals were withheld beside a generic lambda). C++ with GCC stays refused; the test-only `PEEKY_TEST_CPP_GCC=1` hatch ships with the g++ cases of the new tests.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #282** (two blocking issues, one non-blocking; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `626bdaf`): an inlined call Peeky cannot place shares its caller's code, so `_function_at` found the caller's entry and the net never ran: a forced-inline helper a `#line` declares elsewhere showed `x = 281474976709872` in `main` (clang and g++); and without the load address, any placed function running the frame's line counted. Every function and inlined call with code is now kept, placed or not (`CodeDie`); a frame runs the deepest code holding its address (lldb-dap gives an inlined call's caller the address its code starts at, and a frame showing the call's line there is the caller), and where that is unplaced, or may be, its locals are withheld; without the address, the line must be one placed function's own code. A compiler-made member whose class names no file is no longer placed by its first row, which may be an inlined callee's; an inlined call GCC leaves no code (`low_pc` equal to `high_pc`, written as addresses) no longer holds twice its address, as it did read as an offset (at -O1 it took `main`'s frames); the cookbook says when a frame is withheld so.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #282** (one blocking issue; the fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `4f32dbf`): where every function or inlined call holding a frame's address was placed, the narrowest was taken, so an inlined call's start on its own call's line, or a caller frame at the end of an inlined call, was judged by the call's declarations. `frame_level` now gives every root the frame may run (the deepest code at its address, and at the one before it above the innermost frame, and an inlined call's caller at its start); the frame's line, or else its name, may pick one, never at such a start tied on one line; `_function_at` takes that one root's entry, and the frame is withheld where several remain. Rows a later row at their address replaces no longer count (GCC writes the call's line at an inlined call's start), and folded copies are several roots. C targets keep the narrowest entry.
- **#167: C++ built by GCC is enabled** (branch `feat/cpp-gcc`), with tests that fail on `69f5293`: a C++ target built by g++ was refused before anything ran. Clang and GCC now build C++, told apart by `--version`; only clang gets `--driver-mode=g++`, and any other compiler is refused, naming both. The test-only hatch (`PEEKY_TEST_CPP_GCC`, `tests/cpp_gcc_survey.py`) is gone. The C++ tests are compiler-aware, as C's are: a `cxx` fixture runs the cases whose lines, names or notes depend on the compiler once per compiler, with GCC's frame names (`::before(const Task &, const Task &)`, plain `operator()`), lines (a member initializer runs on the line that builds the object; a range `for` header stops once), lldb-dap 18's string summaries, lldb-dap 20's text-set refusals and the hedged notes, each output checked by hand. Found on the way: a name the function keeps in static storage (a `static` local, or a `const` aggregate GCC puts in `.rodata`, survey B4) now says so instead of a bare `not found`; a change through a const pointer (GCC's `this` is `Widget *const`) is refused as a pointer's, not as "`this` is const". B2 (captures `not found`), B5 (an aggregate caller withheld) and the eval-6 lambda's prologue refusal were rechecked: their notes are true. New lanes `cpp-gcc` and `cpp-gcc-llvm20` run every C++ case (`-m cpp tests/cpp tests/native`) with `/usr/bin/g++`, mirrored by the hosted `native-cpp-gcc` job.
- **#283: a range-`for` header that names the outer local hedges that local's note** (branch `fix/optimized-range-for-note`, PR #285), with tests that fail on `2ab3588`: on `for (const auto &[t, u] : make(t)) { use(t * u); }` at `-O1`, the outer `t` said `not what the code reads: the code here names the structured binding` though the line's first stop runs the header before the binding exists. A header ending in `{` already hedged. Now, wherever the header (its range or an init-statement) names the local (`Statements.range_header`), the row says `may not be what the code reads`: the header names `t` before that binding exists, and Peeky cannot tell whether running it reads this local. That holds in optimized builds, at `-O0`'s first stop on the line (which said the same), on every line of a header that spans lines, and in a caller frame. The value is still withheld, and a change is refused with the same doubt.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #285** (three blocking issues; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `df6f700`): a header over two lines (`for (const auto &[t, u] :` / `make(t)) { … }`) kept the unhedged claim, since only a `for` on the stop's line was read; the header holding the line is now found, and every stop on it hedged. The note said `the loop's range reads this local first`, but a mention is no read (`sizeof(t)`, `decltype(t)`, a lambda's body): it now says only that the header names `t`, and the old header hedge no longer says the range is read with it either. And `p.template t<0>()`, `p->template t<0>()` and `P::template t<0>()` counted as the local; the scan now looks past `template` for `.`, `->` or `::`. The same misreading in `Statements._declares` made the binding's own rows on such a line say `this row is the outer local` (on main too); it reads them as uses now.
- **#292: `queries diff` no longer calls a pointer's other address `addresses only`** (branch `fix/diff-pointer-rows`; found while preparing the eleventh evaluation), with tests that fail on `0a2c2fe`: a pointer into an array held element 6's address in one run and element 4's in the other, but `diff` masked both as `0x…`, said `addresses only, at 2 hits` and ranked it last, and COOKBOOK called such rows values that differ only in addresses. A row whose type is a pointer or a reference (`T *`, `T &`, `T &&`, Rust's `&T`, `*const T`), or any value of a C, C++ or Rust run (a preview shows its pointers as addresses), now says `addresses differ: it may point at another element too; compare offsets with address() (COOKBOOK 4.8)` (or `an address in it may be another element's too`), one row per selector (and stop) as before, ranked right after the real differences and before qualified values: not among them, as the stack and heap move between runs, so most pointers differ either way, and the first real difference stays the first divergence. A Python repr's address and a Java object number stay `addresses only`. `diff` and `compare` tables never cut a note, so its advice shows. No JSON key changed (the rows have no category: the note says it).
  - **Review round 1 of PR #294** (one blocking issue; the fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `ce8898f`): where the runs stopped a different number of times, the note on rows after the first difference took its first hit from every row at a hit, so a pointer's other address at hit 1 made it say `the first difference at a hit is at hit 1`, though the rows led with a real difference at hit 2 (an address-only or a qualified value did the same on main). The first is now taken from the real-difference tier alone, by its rank (`_REAL_RANK`, read from the same ranked rows as `diff`, `_DIFF_RANKS`), never from note text; with no real difference at a hit, that note is left out.
- **#286: any one-line statement whose header names the outer local hedges that local's note** (branch `fix/one-line-header-notes`, PR #287), with tests that fail on `0a2c2fe`: on `if (t) { static long t = 5; use(t); }`, the outer `t` said `not what the code reads` and that the code names the block's `static`, though the line's first stop runs the header, which names the outer `t`; #285 hedged only a range-`for`'s own binding. Now an `if`, `switch`, `while` or `for` header (also with an init-statement) that names the local before the line's declaration hides it (`Statements.header_before`, with `block_storage` read up to that name) gives the row `may not be what the code reads`: the header names `t` before that declaration, and Peeky cannot tell whether running it reads this local. As in #285, that holds in optimized builds, at `-O0`'s first stop, on a header over several lines and in a caller frame; and at every stop where a part naming it runs on each pass (a `while`'s condition, a `for`'s condition or increment) or the declaration is in the header itself (a block of a lambda in a range). Members don't count. The value is still withheld.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #287** (one blocking issue, one non-blocking; the fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `4dadbd1`): a stop past the line's first instruction was taken to be past a one-line header, but a stop back from a call in it (`if (check(t) && t > 2)`, after `step --out` from `check`) is before `t > 2` reads the outer `t`, and it got the plain note. Now a header on the line has run only where the line table's column for the stop (lldb-dap gives it with the frame) is after the header's `)`, on a line of plain ASCII without tabs that no macro changes (`NativeSession._header_ran`); a range-`for`'s own binding is judged so too, where a stop at its declaration or `:` also counts as past: they carry the loop's implicit code, all emitted after its range expression (the `begin`/`end` setup, each pass's test and binding, the range temporary's cleanup). The scan's lexical reading (a `t` the header declares, or a lambda's parameter, counts as a mention) is documented.
- **#291: a structured binding of a pair or tuple shows its element, never its address** (branch `fix/gcc-binding-reference`; found while fixing #286), with tests that fail on `0a2c2fe`: GCC describes each name of `auto [t, u] = make(i)[0];` as a variable holding a reference to its element (`std::tuple_element<0, std::pair<long, long> >::type &&`), as does Apple clang 21 (`long &&`), and lldb-dap showed that reference's address as the row's value (`t = 0x0000fffffffffb70`): with no note once the declaration ran, and with one where a loop returns to its line. Such a row now shows the element (`10`, `{x = 3, y = 4}`, `"apple"`), typed as the binding (`debugger_type` keeps LLDB's), in caller frames and optimized builds too, with lldb-dap 18 and 20; where its reference may not be set (a loop's return to the line, a `setjmp`), it is `no value yet`, and one the debugger lists no element under is `not shown`. GCC's are told by their type (`fields.element_reference`, stored as `through`, so a change is refused before the run), Apple clang's by a line that declares the name only in a binding's brackets (`Statements.binds`); clang 18 marks them artificial, and lldb-dap lists none. A pointer element is shown but not followed (`p[0]`).
  - **Review round 1 of PR #296** (two blocking issues, one non-blocking; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `8ba8f82`): a binding by reference was followed into a temporary its line destroyed (`auto &[t, u] = make(0)[0];`, then `auto poison = make(7000);`: `t = 8000, u = 1`, no note), and GCC's type alone marked `std::tuple_element<0, P>::type &r = p.first;` as a binding. `Statements.binding` now reads how the line binds and what: one by reference (`auto &`, `const auto &`, `auto &&`) is followed only into an object of the frame or a member of one (a local or by-value parameter in scope, through no reference or pointer); anything else (a call, a subscript, a range `for`'s element, `*p`, a global) is `not shown: the binding refers to an object Peeky can't prove is still alive`, `auto &&` of a call included. GCC's candidates must also be in a binding's brackets on their line (a reference of the code's own keeps its address first), and are withheld where the line can't be read. In an optimized build, a stop on a binding's own lines is `no value yet`.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #296** (one blocking issue, with tests that fail on the reviewed head `ec1ccae`): a member chain through a union was proven alive, so `auto &[a, b] = u.p;`, then `u.p.~pair(); new (&u.q) std::pair<long, long>{7, 8};`, showed `a = 7, b = 8` with no note. `_member_object` now never walks into a union (a standard library's too), and such a binding is `not shown` with the lifetime note. New integration cases also pin what the review checked by hand: a local reassigned after `auto &[e, f] = p;` reads its new values, and `auto &[g, k] = v[0]` and `for (auto &[key, value] : m)` are withheld.
- **#289: an `if constexpr` block's declaration hides the outer local** (branch `fix/if-constexpr-blocks`, PR #298), with tests that fail on `2730d91`: on `if constexpr (sizeof(long) > 1) { static long t = 5; use(t); }`, the outer `t` showed its value (3) with no note, though the code there names the block's `static` (5). `Statements._brace` read the block of `if constexpr (...)` as a function's body (its condition as parameters), and that of `if consteval` and `if !consteval` as no statement's, so a declaration in one on the stop's line hid nothing. They are statements' blocks now, and the row says `not what the code reads`, hedged where the header names the local (#286). Peeky builds C++17, where `if consteval` (C++23) doesn't compile, so only the scanner's tests cover it.
- **#288: a C++ local declared on the stop line is withheld after clang's landing pad** (branch `fix/stop-line-local-cleanup`; found while preparing the eleventh evaluation), with tests that fail on `0a2c2fe`: in a clang build, `int q = 0;` after a braced inner loop that calls a member function, in a range-`for` over strings, showed its leftovers, `[settable]`, at the line's first instruction (grades' `quiz` showed the previous student's). Clang puts the loop's exception landing pad, on a closing `}`'s line, just before the line's code at `-O0`; Peeky took it for a later line's code running into the line, as a header over two lines does. Such a first row is now `preceded`, and the stop's disassembly (`control_sources`, `runs_into`) decides: where every way in is another line's code falling or jumping into it, the value is shown; where every way in is from at or above the line (a landing pad ends in a jump), the local is withheld. Otherwise, or where the disassembly can't be read, the row is shown with a note and a change is refused. GCC puts its landing pads after the function's code, and was right already. `describe`'s extra closing line for block locals is a separate, documented rule: the landing pad lies inside the block's code, and LLDB places that line's breakpoint there.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #295** (two blocking issues, one non-blocking; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `2d0ff62`): a row reached both from before the declaration and from a later line's code counted as proof that the declaration ran, so the local showed plainly, past the loop and `setjmp` checks. `runs_into` is now True only where every way in from below is another line's code and no jump comes back, False only where every one is code at or above the line, and None otherwise, which shows the value with a note and refuses a change. A call's return was taken as falling into the next instruction, though `_Unwind_Resume`, `__cxa_throw` or `abort` never return: it, and any instruction in a function that jumps through a register, is now a way in Peeky can't follow (None). The fallback test now injects the failure through `architecture`, which main has, so it fails on main for the missing note.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #295** (one blocking issue, one non-blocking; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `47a8174`): the disassembly shows no exception's way in, so a landing pad that a later line's code also falls into gave True. Peeky now reads the landing pads from the binary's exception tables (`landing_pads`: ELF `.eh_frame` FDEs and `.gcc_except_table`; Mach-O's `__unwind_info` LSDA array, `__eh_frame` and `__gcc_except_tab`), once per session; a row the unwinder may enter, or tables Peeky can't read, gives None. And a row only jumps back come to is now False, so the loop's note says why, not the note for paths Peeky can't tell apart.
  - **Review round 3 of PR #295** (one blocking issue; the fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `82cec79`): the exception-table parsers weren't bounded to their records, so malformed tables could read as having no landing pads. A CIE's fields, read past its end into the terminator, gave none; so did a call site's landing pad of 2**64 (a 64-bit overflow, masked to 0), and an `__unwind_info` whose index lay over its header. `_leb128` and `_eh_pointer` now take a limit: every CIE and FDE field stays in its record and its augmentation data, and every call-site field in its table. A LEB128 number over 64 bits, an `__unwind_info` array outside its section, over its header, misaligned or out of order, and a DWARF64 `.eh_frame` record all raise. Sixteen malformed tables are each unreadable, for the reason given; on `82cec79`, ten of them read as tables, four of those as having no landing pads.
- **#290: a block in a lambda is the lambda's** (branch `fix/lambda-block-scope`, PR #299), with tests that fail on `2730d91`: a block in a lambda wholly on the stop's line counted for the function holding the lambda. On `for (const auto &[t, u] : make2(t, [] { long r; { static long t = 8; r = t; } return r; })) {`, the loop's own binding was withheld as `this row is the outer local`, and on a range whose lambda does the same, `main`'s `t` was withheld for the lambda's `static`. The other way round, in the lambda's own frame its blocks were skipped: on `[&](long t) { { static long t = 5; use(t++); } return use(t); }`, the parameter showed 13 with no note, though the code there names the block's `static`. Now, where the frame's function is known and goes on past the line (`block_storage`'s `others`), a function's or lambda's body wholly on the line, and every block in it, counts for nothing; and in a frame wholly on the line, its body's blocks count as its statements do. Where Peeky can't place the frame, or the lambda goes on past the line, they count as before.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #299** (one blocking issue, one non-blocking; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `0f9f601`): a lambda declared `constexpr` was no lambda to `Statements._brace` (`_QUALIFIED` lacked the word), nor one with `noexcept(true)`, `__attribute__((...))` or `-> decltype(t)` after its parameters (the last `)` before its brace was taken for theirs), nor a generic one (`[]<class T>(T t)`, read as a template function's): their blocks still counted for `main`. `_parameters` now steps back past such a group, `_QUALIFIED` takes `constexpr`, `consteval`, `static`, attributes and a trailing `requires`, and a `]<...>(...)` is a lambda's. And in a frame wholly on a line holding several functions' bodies, the note named a declaration from one of them: it now says Peeky cannot tell which one the frame runs (`block_storage`'s `("bodies", line)`).
  - **Review round 2 of PR #299** (one non-blocking issue, with a test that fails on the reviewed head `4b87675`): `noexcept(...)` and `__attribute__((...))` after a lambda's parameters were read by a regular expression three parentheses deep, so `noexcept((((((true))))))` left the brace `other` and the lambda's blocks counting for its caller. `Statements._qualified` and `_parameters` now walk to each group's paired bracket, `[[...]]` attributes too.
- **#304: `peeky sql --schema` is a short index, and `peeky queries --help` lists each query's parameters** (branch `fix/sql-schema-short`; eleventh evaluation, tides, rota and grades), with tests that fail on `8b8c2f2`: `--schema` printed 20.7 KB, which two agents' tools cut off, and an agent passed `set_effect` `a` and `b`. `--schema` alone now prints a line per table, view and other part (1.5 KB); `--schema NAME ...` prints those in full, `--schema all` the whole text as before, and an unknown name is `INVALID_QUERY` listing the names. `--schema --json` is still the whole manifest. `queries --help` lists `diff [a] [b]`, `set_effect [run]` and the rest, and a wrong canned query or `--param` hints at it.

The tests now pin `--schema all` to main's renderer, copied into the test, and spell out the names and help lines they expect.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #307** (three blocking issues, one non-blocking; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `cac9a12`): the `joins` part said the evidence view "already has the run's columns", but it has only some; it now names them and says that `output`, `error_code`, `seconds` and `warnings` need a join with `runs`. `diff`'s summary called every row a difference, though its `(status)` row also shows two runs that failed alike; `hits` and `values` read returned values as well as observed ones; `join`'s key may be several selectors. - **#301: `queries diff`'s alignment note leads with its verdict** (branch `fix/diff-alignment-verdict`, PR #308; found by the eleventh evaluation: grades, seats and rota), with tests that fail on `8b8c2f2`: three agents quoted one three-clause sentence ("… though that does not prove … but runs 2 and 4 saw `quiz`'s values at other hits … it keeps Peeky from saying the hits line up") where a key (`name`, `week`) paired every hit and the repeated value came from the edit or chance. Each note where both runs stopped as often now starts with a verdict line (`hits pair by`, `hits pair as far as`, or `hits may not line up:`), then one detail per line after `- ` (the text table indents them under `note:`; `--json` keeps one string in `notes`, its lines split by newlines). A left-out value's sign of a reorder beside a key comes last. Keys and codes are unchanged.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #308** (two blocking issues; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `299f829`): with no change, `i` 1 to 4 in both runs and the first two moments swapped (other arguments, or a program that runs differently each time) gave `hits line up by `i``, as main's `the hits line up by` did: agreeing, and unique within each run, proves only that hit-number pairing is what `join` by `i` gives. No note says the hits line up now: the verdict is `hits pair by `i`: it agrees … and is unique across these hits`, then `… does not prove the hits are the same moments`. And `that is a weak sign of a reorder` graded a sign beside a key that may be a counter, which is no evidence against a reorder; it now says `that alternate alignment does not settle whether the calls were reordered`.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #308** (one blocking issue, on main too; the fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `12e571d`): alignment compares values with addresses masked, but plain `join` compares the stored text. grades' `name` (a `const std::string &`: its address, then its text) agreed only masked, and the note said hit-number pairing is what `join` by it gives, though `join --param key=name` paired no hits. The note now names the `join` whole (`peeky queries join --param a=1 --param b=2 --param key=name --param masked=1`), says `with addresses masked` where a value agrees only so, and the `join` it suggests where hits may not line up has `--param masked=1` where only values that carry addresses tell the hits apart. A test runs the command a note names and checks that it pairs the hits as the note says.
  - **Review round 3 of PR #308** (one blocking issue; the fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `809ab9a`): the key a pairing note named could be more selectors than `join` takes (8): with `k1` to `k9` each 1 at one hit and all 0 at the tenth, the note named all nine, and its command failed (`key names at most 8 selectors`). `_join_key` now names the fewest selectors it finds unique across the hits, at most 8: one alone where one is, else the greedy choice, then smaller sets in order within a bounded search. Where none is found, the verdict is `hits pair as far as … show`, saying no `join` key of 8 selectors or fewer was found, and no command is named.
- **#303: an `Option` of a tuple or crate struct reads from memory, and an opaque enum says why, where it is and what to read** (branch `fix/rust-option-struct`; eleventh evaluation, rota), with tests that fail on `16dfa00`: rota's `last: Option<Engineer>`, the variable at the heart of the bug, read `<unqualified Rust enum layout>`, with no address to compare `l` of `if let Some(l) = &last` with. Such a local now shows `Some(Engineer { name: "Björn", shifts: 1, .. })` (`[active-variant]`), its payload `last.__0` with the fields a container's element reads (`last.__0.name`), under an owned `Vec`'s proofs. The layout comes from the DWARF variant part: a tag of its own, or a niche whose field (found through nested structs) is a capacity, a `NonNull`, a reference, a `Box`, a `bool` or a `char`, with `None`'s value outside its valid values; a tag holding neither variant's value is `<unverified Rust enum value>`. LLDB's view of it isn't used (Apple's names `None` `$variant$0`). Every opaque enum's row now has a note (`opaque_reason` from the worker): why, the address it is stored at, as a pointer prints, and what to read instead (what `if let Some(x) = &last` binds). With lldb-dap 18, which can't read memory, `last` is withheld and its note's address is `l`'s. `describe` lists these Options on `qualified enum views:`. `Option<u32>`, `Option<String>` and other enums stay opaque.

A `Result` names `if let Ok(x)` or `Err(e)`, any other enum a `match` arm. Real-rustc cases now cover a tag of its own (`Option<Point>`), a `char` niche, a `Box` niche and a reference niche (`Option<Pick<'a>>`, which reads now that `option_name` drops the MIR's lifetimes), on host and with lldb-dap 18 and 20.
- **#302: a C or C++ pointer says at which element's address of a captured array it is, and `diff` compares them** (branch `fix/pointer-element-offsets`, PR #312; eleventh evaluation, tides 1, 2, 6, grades 2, prep 15), with tests that fail on `16dfa00`: `turn const int * 0x…7040` showed nothing that tied it to `level`, the agent divided by a hard-coded `sizeof(int)` (the store had no element size), and `queries diff` said `addresses differ … compare offsets with address()` for a pointer at element 6 in one run and 4 in the other, and for a `const std::string &` that read `"Maya"` in both. A pointer captured with an array at the same stop and frame now says `[at level[6]'s address]` (or `[at the address one past level's end]`, never read), an address fact, where the build's debug information proves it and one captured base alone matches: the array's own type gives its length (never a pointer, such as a parameter; a struct or scalar is an array of one), the pointer points at its element type (typedefs and qualifiers aside, by the type's DWARF identity and size), and the address is a whole number of elements from the first, at most one past the end; the array's address is the debugger's own (`memoryReference`, or lldb-dap's `T @ 0x…`). Where the array is a pointer (tides' line 40, `const int *level`), a pointer of the same type says how far it is from it by address alone, `[level + 6 by address; same array not proven]` (negative before it; a whole number of elements, at most 2^20 of them; counted from the first pointer parameter, else the first captured). Rows give `at_element_of`, `offset_from` and `element_size` (`session get` too); schema 17 stores `captures.element_size`, `at_element_of`, `element_index`, `offset_from` and `element_offset`. `diff` ranks another element's address of the same captured array first among the addresses that differ (``the address of `level[6]` in run a, of `level[4]` in run b; by address alone: the pointer or the array may have moved``), the same element's as `addresses only`, offsets from a base pointer with the addresses that differ, showing both (``by address, `level + 6` in run a and `level + 4` in run b``: two pointers may be separate blocks), and a C or C++ pointer or reference whose text after its address agrees as `addresses only` with a hedge. Rust and Java rows are unchanged.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #312** (three blocking issues; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `382bf0e`): an address does not prove which object a pointer designates (a union's two arrays, the array after this one, reused storage), yet rows said `points at u.a[2]` and the first captured base was named: rows now state the address fact, `[at u.b[2]'s address]`, name no base where several match, and the column is `at_element_of`; offsets from a base pointer were real differences, though `src` and `dst` in two heap blocks differ by layout: they now rank with the addresses that differ, showing both offsets, and the alignment note no longer counts them; and `pair AS MATERIALIZED` needed SQLite 3.35, while Python 3.11 may link an older one: the tiers name `kind` where `note` is `kind`, so the diff needs 41 aggregate terms under the column limit Peeky sets (main needed 70), on any SQLite, with the same rows.

Such rows now rank first among the addresses that differ, with their text and a hedge; where the pointer reads the same in both runs, `diff` leaves the row out and `compare` says it; the alignment note reads them with addresses masked, as any pointer; the docs no longer say that layout can't change the index.
- **#300: the standard library's `ptr::eq` withholds no local, and a refusal states a read only where the MIR shows it** (branch `fix/raw-pointer-copy-reason`, PR #310; eleventh evaluation, `rota`), with tests that fail on `8b8c2f2`: a line breakpoint on `if ptr::eq(e, l) {` stops inside the inlined `ptr::eq`, which the MIR places past the call, and `session inspect` withheld `[settable]` from every local because "a statement under way at the stop has already read it into a temporary (at line 39)", though line 39 reads none of `week`, `pick` or `log`; probe rows there showed no mark and no reason. The call's arguments, `&raw const *e` and `&raw const *l`, read through `e` and `l`, references the copy analysis does not follow, so each counted as a pointer into any local, and a call given one as a copy of it. A call of `std::ptr::eq` or `addr_eq` that #259's proof shows is the standard library's (`_std_compares`) reads only the two addresses, so its result now holds only what its operands hold: `week`, `pick` and `log` are `[settable]` there, and `--set week=7` reaches the program. A refusal says "has already read it into a temporary" only where a must-analysis (`Body.read_copies_at`) finds a read on every path to every place the stop can be: a copy of the changed part, or a read through a pointer surely made from a borrow of the local. Otherwise it says what the statement does and that it may hold a copy: "reads it into a temporary (at line 12)", "uses a pointer that may point into it", or "uses `p` and `q` (at line 23), raw pointers Peeky can't trace that may point at it", each name by its kind; before the run too, advising a stop before the statement starts. A root row a statement under way withholds (a String's too) now says why, as `inspect` does: `[not settable: a statement under way may hold a copy (line 39)]`, `a statement under way read it (line 17)`, ``copied into `n` (line 35)``, `part-way through line 12`. A rule by type (a `&Engineer` cannot point into a `u32`) was left out: it holds only for the crate's own types with no pointer in them, which only the debug information shows, and the standard library views a local as another type (`Cell::from_mut`, `slice::from_ref`).
  - **Review round 1 of PR #310** (three blocking issues; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `f31ee71`): a raw pointer given to a call counted for nothing wherever the source showed no unsafe code, but a standard-library macro brings unsafe code with no `unsafe` in the source (`#[allow_internal_unsafe]`), so `core::ptr::read` of a pointer to `week` left it `[settable]`; only a call proven the standard library's `ptr::eq` or `addr_eq` is exempt now, and every other call given a raw pointer may read through it. "Traced" counted a call given a pointer into the local as a read, so in a crate with unsafe code `last` and `e` were said to have "already read it" at `ptr::eq(e, l)`, and the places the stop can be were joined, so a read at one stood for all. A refusal now states a read only where every place has one the MIR surely makes; a second look found that a may-analysis (joined over paths, through an aggregate holding other pointers, any part of the local) can't show that, so `Body.read_copies_at` is a must-analysis (met by intersection, the changed part only, no aggregate or call-made pointer, and a temporary computed from a binding's copy no read of the statement's, but the binding's copy, named as #192 names it). The rest is hedged by what the statement does: a read by value on some path, a read of another part, the program's own copy, a pointer that may point into it, or one Peeky does not follow. Every name was called "a reference", raw pointers too; each is now named by its kind (a reference, a raw pointer, a closure, a coroutine), or as a value holding a pointer.
  - **Review round 2 of PR #310** (two blocking issues; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `2d8b580`): the bindings each place's copy came from were joined, so a read at one place and a binding's copy at another named the binding as "the program has already copied its value into `n`", though a stop at the first never used it, and different bindings at each place named both; a binding is now named only where every place has such a copy and the bindings are common to all, and where each place holds a sure copy another way the refusal says only "the program already holds a copy of it". A write to the changed part kept the must-analysis's reads standing (`_2 = copy (_1.0: u32); (_1.0: u32) = const 3_u32;`): a write that may overlap the part (the whole local, the same field, an element not known, through a pointer surely into it) now ends them, and one surely apart (another field, another constant element) keeps them.
  - **Review round 3 of PR #310** (one blocking issue and a ruling; each with tests that fail on the reviewed head `27c2e39`): the must-analysis followed a pointer only for the whole local, so `_2 = copy (_1.0: u32); _6 = &mut (_1.0: u32); (*_6) = const 3_u32;` left `_2` read for a change to `s.0`, though it holds the old field. A write through a pointer now ends what it knows unless the pointer surely points apart from the part (a borrow of another local, another field or constant element, a copy of one, or an argument); `&mut _1[_5]` may be the element, and a pointer loaded from memory may point anywhere. The ruling: where the body lends a place that may overlap the part `&mut` or raw (`Body._exposed`), a call, a drop or a `copy_nonoverlapping` may change it, so it ends what is known too, on the unwind edge as well. So `let y = x; keep(&mut x);` no longer gives "the program has already copied `x` into `y`": the refusal stays, and says "the program's own `y` (at line 34) may hold a copy of `x` that it goes on with", apart from the statement under way, which may not use `y` (`pause();`); before, it said the statement uses `y`. Rows say `the program may hold a copy (line 34)`, and the advice is to change it before the copy, or the copy. With no such call, the factual note stays (a real build's `let y = x; pause();`).
  - **Review round 4 of PR #310** (one blocking issue; the fix with a test that fails on the reviewed head `78940d0`): a pointer argument was known to point apart from every local of the frame, but a loan of it retargets it. In `fn f(mut x: u32, mut p: *mut u32) { let y = x; let q = &mut p; let r = &raw mut x; *q = r; unsafe { *p = 3; } … }`, `q` pointed apart (at `p`), so `*q = r` kept `p` apart, and `*p = 3`, which writes `x`, left `y` holding a sure copy. A local any borrow is taken of anywhere in the body (`&mut p`, `&raw mut p`, or `&p` for a `Cell`) is now never apart, nor is a pointer copied from it; the test is rustc's own MIR of that function.
- **#306, the CLI's Lows: what a probe's and a session's text say** (branch `fix/eval11-lows-cli`; eleventh evaluation: grades 4, 5 and 6, rota 4, seats 2, 3, 5, 6 and 7, tides 5, prep 13 and 14), with tests that fail on `cbf4ba9`: the `after-set` line counts the rows it leaves out as scalar rows; an `@all`'s fourth change is shown whole, and a write of the value already there says `no change` and is counted apart; a `(return)` row's line reads `to 49`, the caller's; a C or C++ function with no return type shows `(return): void (no value: the function returns nothing)`; session commands name files as `session start` did, from any directory; `session report` says where the program is stopped, and its hits and steps so far; a step onto the breakpoint's line says it is not a hit, and what `continue` does next; `queries runs` adds `output_end`; a candidate's first progress line says which of its lines `--at` names; `NO_STOP` after `--set-at` changes counts them, where they were, and says to compare with a run without them; and `verify` edits a config's one source without `--at`.

- **#293: a line that names the outer local outside its block's declaration hedges that local's note** (branch `fix/same-line-scope-hedge`; found in the review of #287), with tests that fail on `5548cd2`: on `do { static long t = 5; use(t); } while (t--);` and on `use(t); { static long t = 5; use(t); }`, the outer `t` said `not what the code reads` and that the code names the block's `static`, though the `while` condition runs after that `static` leaves scope and the first call runs before the block: both name the outer `t`. Now a name on the line outside the scope of the declaration that hides the local (`Statements.named_outside`, with `block_storage` read at that name's scope) gives the row `may not be what the code reads`: the line also names `t` before that declaration, after its scope ends, or both, and Peeky cannot tell whether that code reads this local. That holds at every stop on the line, in optimized builds and in a caller frame; a header naming it before the declaration keeps #286's hedge. Members, and names a declaration there declares, don't count; in a lambda's own frame, only its body does. The value is still withheld.
  - **Review round 1 of PR #319** (two blocking issues; each fix with tests that fail on the reviewed head `173bcfb`): a name taken for a declarator was no mention, but `_declarator` leans that way, so `{ static long t = 5; use(t); } return use(0), t;` (also from a macro) and C++'s `return ok and t;` kept the plain note; only a name surely declared there is left out now (`Statements._declaring`).
